# Chapter One

## The Queue

> *VESPER LABS SHIPS "PRODUCTION-GRADE" CODING MODEL. THREE FORTUNE 100 COMPANIES ANNOUNCE ENGINEERING REDUCTIONS "ENABLED BY QUALITY."*
>
> — the kitchen television, Monday morning

John closed his eyes and listened to the dripping on his left.

It was the air conditioner's condensate line, emptying itself one drop at a time onto a square of aluminum flashing that some landlord had laid in the mulch in some earlier decade. Each drop came a little slower than the one before. When they slowed enough, the dripping went under, and what replaced it was the sound of dead oak leaves turning over on the sidewalk in a wind that did not quite deserve the name.

It was the second Monday in November, a quarter to six in the morning, sixty-one degrees by the phone in his pocket. Two years ago John had lived in a city where sixty-one degrees in November was a heat wave, and he still walked out into a Florida morning as if somebody might take it back. He wore what he always wore to walk the dog: olive cargo shorts with a pocket for everything, a gray Chicago Bears hoodie gone soft at the cuffs, and a pair of Merrell hiking shoes that had never been on a hike. He was forty-two years old, six foot one and two hundred and ten pounds, and he believed that most of the two hundred and ten was muscle under a modest layer of fat. His brown hair was cut short every three weeks at a barbershop with leather chairs and a forty-five-dollar haircut, and it was going gray at the temples in a way the barber called distinguished and John called arithmetic.

At the end of the leash, the weekday ceremony was in full swing.

Charlie was a Papillon, which is French for butterfly and refers to the ears. They stood up off his head like two fringed sails, white with black patches, and the black spread down over both eyes so that he looked like a very small bandit who had been interrupted halfway through putting on his mask. A white blaze ran down the middle of his face to a black nose. He weighed eight pounds. His tail was a white plume that curled up over his back, and he carried it the way a drum major carries a baton. He wore a red harness that Sanda had bought at a boutique in Winter Park for more than John had once paid for a set of tires.

Charlie sniffed the trunk of a palm and rejected it. He sniffed the corner of the mailbox kiosk, the base of a streetlight, and a patch of grass that looked to John exactly like every other patch of grass in Seminole County, and he rejected those too. The longer Charlie took, the later John got to work. The later John got to work, the later he got home, and the later he got home, the later he got to the gym, which in practice meant he did not get to the gym. Everything was connected. Charlie seemed to understand this and took his time anyway.

"Today, buddy," John said, checking the time on his phone. "Pick one. It's all the same grass."

Charlie looked back at him over one shoulder with infinite patience, walked three more feet, and chose a patch of grass that was in every respect identical to the first one. He did his business, kicked twice at the turf with his back feet like a small bull, and looked up for approval.

"Good boy," John told him, bending with the bag. "Very good. Excellent work. Let's go home."

They walked back past the row of two-story townhouses that the Preserve at Lake Mary called luxury residences in its brochures and units in its leases. Theirs was the fourth one in. It had a one-car garage, a screened patio out back, and a front door with a smart lock that opened with an app or, thank God, with a key. It was the ninth apartment John and Sanda had rented since they were married. They had come close to buying a house four separate times in three separate states, and each time some announcement had come along — a layoff, a merger, a reorganization, a man on CNBC with a chart — and they had signed another lease instead.

That was also the reason there were no children. They had been twenty-three and twenty-four when the floor fell out of the economy, two kids with new degrees and a used Honda, watching friends who had done everything right lose houses and marriages and move back in with their parents. The world had never afterward felt solid enough to set a child down on. Charlie was the closest thing they had to one. He was spoiled accordingly.

* * *

Inside, the apartment smelled of lavender, Sanda would tell him later. John could not smell it. His allergies had taken his sense of smell one spring in Chicago and never given it back, which meant he could eat Époisses without flinching and lived in a world other people had to describe to him.

He went into the master bathroom to brush his teeth, and there it was, the way it was most mornings: Sanda's garden tub, full to the overflow drain with last night's bathwater, cold now, a faint skin of bath oil on top and a wine glass standing on the ledge with a purple ring in the bottom. Sanda took a bath most nights with a glass of something red and a magazine about houses they could not afford, and when she was done she got out and dried off and went to bed and left the water in the tub. Every morning John pulled the plug. Neither of them had ever said a word about it in twenty years of marriage. It was the most successful system they had.

He reached into the cold water up to his wrist and pulled the stopper, and the tub began to empty with a long, comfortable gurgle.

"Did you drain my bath?" Sanda called from the walk-in closet.

"It was cold," John said, squeezing toothpaste onto his brush.

"It was cooling," Sanda said, coming out of the closet with one heel on and the other in her hand. "There's a difference."

She was forty-one and she had not changed since high school, which John found both wonderful and unfair, because he had changed a great deal. She stood just under five feet tall, and that was in the heels; in bare feet, on her tiptoes, she could just about see over the kitchen high-top. Her hair was long and nearly black and so wavy that it took her twenty minutes and a round brush every morning to persuade it into something a regional vice president would call professional. She had dark eyes, olive skin, and a pair of tortoiseshell glasses that she pushed up her nose with one knuckle when she was thinking. She was beautiful in a way that still occasionally made John forget what he had been about to say. This morning she wore a navy suit that fit as if it had been built around her, a cream silk blouse, and the small gold hoops he had bought her in Venice. She managed the Lake Mary branch of Meridian National Bank, and she dressed for it the way some people dress for church.

"How is it cooling," John asked through a mouthful of toothpaste, "if it's already cold?"

"It has stages," Sanda said, stepping into the second heel and holding the doorframe for balance. "You wouldn't understand. You don't take baths."

"I take showers. Like an adult."

"You take showers like a man washing a car," Sanda said, and went off down the hall toward the kitchen.

He followed her. The kitchen was small and white and had been renovated by someone who had seen a picture of a nice kitchen once and tried to remember it later. There was a high-top counter with two stools, a cheap electric range with two big coil burners and two small ones that John had never once seen bring anything to a boil, and a dual-zone wine cooler that held forty-six bottles and had its own app. On the counter, the pod coffee machine was already wheezing out Sanda's first cup. The French press they had used for fifteen years before the pod machine lived on a high shelf above the refrigerator, next to the fondue set from Zürich, where it was dusted twice a year and otherwise left alone to think about what it had done.

The television on the wall was on, because Sanda liked noise in the morning and John did not, and Sanda paid more of the rent. A woman in a red jacket was talking to a man in a blue suit in front of a graphic of a glowing letter V.

"Your robot's on TV," Sanda said, handing John the first cup and starting a second.

"It's not my robot," John said, taking it.

"It's the one at your office."

"It's the one at everybody's office." John blew across the top of the coffee and watched the screen. The crawl at the bottom said VESPER LABS SHIPS "PRODUCTION-GRADE" CODING MODEL, and under that, THREE FORTUNE 100 COMPANIES ANNOUNCE ENGINEERING REDUCTIONS "ENABLED BY QUALITY."

"Enabled by quality," Sanda read aloud, squinting at the crawl. "What does that mean?"

"It means they fired a lot of people," John said, "and they want credit for it."

"Are they going to fire you?"

"I'm the guy who watches it do my job," John said, and drank. "They need somebody to watch it."

"Why?" Sanda asked, fishing her keys out of the bowl by the door.

John opened his mouth to answer and found he did not have one ready, which was unusual for him before seven in the morning or at any other hour. Sanda waited a second, looked at him over the tops of her glasses in a way that suggested she had won something, and went out to the garage.

* * *

Palmetto Grid Software occupied the top two floors of a glass building off Lake Mary Boulevard that looked, from the interstate, like an ice cube somebody had set down and forgotten. Inside, it looked like every software company John had ever worked for: polished concrete floors, a ping-pong table nobody used, a kombucha tap that had not worked since summer, and long rows of standing desks arranged in pods named after Florida rivers. John's pod was the Suwannee.

The most important thing in the room was a sixty-inch screen on the far wall that showed a live map of the United States. On the map, tiny yellow lightning bolts appeared and disappeared wherever the power was out, and every lightning bolt belonged to a customer of Palmetto's only real product, a piece of software called Restore. Two hundred and forty utilities and rural electric co-ops used Restore to decide which crews went where when the lights went off. On a normal weekday there were a few dozen bolts on the map, scattered across the country like spilled rice. Most of them were squirrels. Squirrels, John had learned in his first week, had caused more power outages in America than any terrorist ever born.

His laptop woke when he sat down, and the first thing it showed him was the queue.

There were three hundred and four pull requests in it. A pull request was a proposed change to the code, and all three hundred and four had been written overnight by the same author, which was not a person. Its name on the screen was Vesper Code, and the company paid Vesper Labs a great deal of money every month for the privilege of letting it write Restore. Everyone in the office called it Vee, as though it sat in the next pod over and might want to come to happy hour.

"Vee was cooking last night," Tyler said, rolling his chair over from the next desk with his feet. "Did you see your queue? My guy is unstoppable."

Tyler was twenty-six. He had a mullet that had started out ironic two years ago and had since become sincere, a pencil mustache that had not, and a gray Palmetto hoodie with the sleeves pushed up over forearms that had never lifted anything heavier than the forty-ounce Stanley tumbler he carried everywhere. The tumbler was with him now. It was full of something blue. He wore black joggers and a pair of Hokas so thick in the sole that he seemed to be standing on two loaves of bread, and he made forty thousand dollars a year more than John did, because his title had the letters AI in it. As far as John could determine, Tyler had not written a line of code with his own hands since his senior project in college, which had been an app that told you which dining hall had the shortest line.

"I saw it," John said.

John's job, officially, was Senior Software Engineer, Restore Dispatch. When Palmetto hired him two years ago, that had meant fixing bugs, most of them buried in old code written by people who had long since left for better jobs or worse ones. Now he did not fix bugs, because there were almost none left to fix. Vee did not write bugs. Vee wrote code that worked the first time and every time, code with a defect rate so low that the company's quality dashboard had begun rounding it to zero. John's job was to read what Vee wrote and approve it. He was a reviewer. Palmetto had three of them, for something like two thousand pull requests a week, and he was, by any honest measure, the best of the three, and the three hundred and four in his queue were his alone.

At 9:40 a.m., every laptop in the Suwannee pod chimed at once.

The email came from Dana Whitfield, Vice President of Engineering, and its subject line was *Update to Our Review Process*. John read it twice. Tyler read it once, said "Let's *go*" out loud, and held up a hand for a high five that nobody gave him.

The email said that effective immediately, any pull request authored by Vesper Code that passed every automated check would be merged into the product automatically, without waiting for a human. Merge on green, it called it. It said that human review would continue but would now be "advisory." It said the change reflected the company's confidence in its tooling and its commitment to delivering value to customers faster. It used the word velocity three times and the word trust twice, and it was signed *Onward — Dana*.

"Advisory," John said to his screen.

"It means we're not the bottleneck anymore," Tyler said happily, spinning his chair in a full circle. "It's freeing, man. You don't have to babysit my guy anymore."

"Your guy writes the software that tells linemen which pole to climb," John said, turning around in his chair. "In the rain. At night."

"Right, and he's better at it than we are," Tyler said, not unkindly. "Than you are. No offense. He doesn't get tired. He doesn't get bored. He doesn't go to lunch. Have you ever found a bug in his stuff? Like, ever?"

John thought about it honestly. "Not a real one."

"So," Tyler said, as if that settled it, and in a way it did, and he rolled back to his own desk and put his AirPods in.

John opened a blank document and wrote an objection.

He wrote that advisory review was not review, that the absence of found bugs was not the same as the absence of bugs, and that a system which ran the restoration of electric power for two hundred and forty utilities deserved a human being reading every line before it shipped, even if — especially if — the human being was slower than the machine. It came to two paragraphs. It was good. He read it back, and it sounded to his own ear like a man shouting at a weather report.

He rewrote it to sound reasonable. Then he rewrote it to sound like a team player with a few small concerns about process. Then he read the third version, decided it said nothing at all, and deleted all three.

"You okay with the memo, John?"

Dana was standing at the edge of the pod. She was thirty-nine, and she had come to Palmetto from a consulting firm in Atlanta where, he suspected, she had learned to stand at the edge of things. She wore her blond hair in a ponytail so sleek it looked laminated, a fleece vest with the Palmetto logo over a white silk tee, slim black pants, and white leather sneakers that had never touched grass. She had an AirPod in each ear at all times, including meetings, and John had never once been able to tell whether she was listening to him or to somebody else.

"Advisory," John said, swiveling to face her. "What does that mean, in practice?"

"It means your judgment still matters," Dana said, smiling.

"To who?"

"To the process," Dana said, still smiling. "We're not taking anything away from the team. We're removing friction. If you see something in a PR that worries you, flag it. Absolutely flag it. We want that signal."

"And if it's already merged by the time I see it?"

"Then we roll it forward," Dana said, tapping the frame of the pod twice, the way a person knocks on wood. "That's the beauty of velocity. Nothing's permanent." She moved off toward the Kissimmee pod, saying "Hey, hey, good morning" to someone John could not see.

He turned back to the queue. Three hundred and four.

He opened the first one. It was a change to the way Restore estimated restoration times for a co-op in rural Georgia. It was elegant. It was tested. It came with a description, as all of Vee's pull requests did, laid out in neat sections: *Summary*, *Motivation*, *Testing*, *Risk: Low*. John read the summary and the motivation, scrolled to the bottom, and typed the four letters that every software engineer alive had typed ten thousand times. *LGTM.* Looks good to me.

He had not looked.

He did it again, and again. By lunch he had approved eleven pull requests, and he had not opened one of them past its description. Each had already passed every check the company owned. Each would have merged without him anyway under the new policy, an hour or a day from now. His approval was a signature on a letter somebody else had already mailed. He knew this, and he typed *LGTM* eleven times anyway, because it was his job, and he was good at his job, and his job was to not do it.

At lunch, Tyler asked him through a mouthful of poke bowl what Restore actually did.

"You've worked here a year," John said, setting down his sandwich.

"I know what it does," Tyler said. "Like, in general. I mean explain it like I'm five."

John pointed with his chin at the map on the wall. "Your power goes out. Storm, squirrel, drunk guy hits a pole. You open your utility's app on your phone, and the app says, *We're aware of an outage in your area. A crew has been assigned. Estimated restoration, four-fifteen p.m.* That sentence. That's us. *A crew has been assigned*, that's my module. Dispatch. It decides which truck goes to which pole first, and it tells the truck, and it tells you."

"Sick," Tyler said, nodding slowly with real admiration. "So when people get their lights back on, that's, like, you."

"It used to be me," John said, and picked his sandwich back up. "Now it's your guy."

* * *

That night Sanda sat at the dining table under the chandelier with a bottle of Sancerre, a legal pad, a stack of cream-colored place cards, and eighteen white porcelain ramekins with fluted edges that she had found at Home Goods on Saturday and had been unable to stop talking about since.

She had changed into the clothes she wore when she was home and planning something: a cream cashmere sweater so soft that Charlie slept on it whenever she took it off, black leggings, and a pair of slippers with pom-poms on the toes. Her glasses were pushed up into her hair. Charlie lay under the table with his chin on one pom-pom, guarding it.

"Six people," Sanda said, writing on the legal pad. "Your mom, your dad, Granny, my mom, you, and me. Six. In a kitchen with two burners."

"Two good burners," John said from his recliner, pouring himself a second glass of the Sancerre. The recliner was brown leather and it was the one piece of furniture he had insisted on keeping through all nine apartments; it had followed them from Minneapolis to Chicago to Florida like a loyal old animal. "The other two are decorative."

"The other two are a joke," Sanda said. "Okay. Pheasant for everyone except Granny. That's two and a half pheasants, which means I need a new roasting pan, because the old one holds one pheasant, because for twenty years there have been two of us. Granny gets a turkey. A small one. Because Granny will not eat pheasant, because Granny will not eat anything she can't identify from across the room." Sanda wrote TURKEY on the pad and underlined it twice. "You're going to deep-fry it in the garage."

"I've never deep-fried a turkey."

"That's why I bought two," Sanda said, not looking up. "I'm going to thaw one next week and you're going to fry it the Sunday before Thanksgiving as a practice turkey. Then on Thanksgiving you fry the real one. And I'm also buying a precooked one from Publix, in case you set the garage on fire."

"That's three turkeys," John said, counting on his fingers. "For one ninety-year-old woman."

"That's a backup plan," Sanda said. "I always have a backup plan."

She did. She had once packed a small suitcase inside a large one for a trip to Italy in case the airline lost the large one, and when the airline did, she had been insufferable for a week, and John had loved her for it the entire time.

She picked up a place card, wrote GRANNY on it with a calligraphy pen, and studied it. "I can't put Granny next to my mother."

"Why not?"

"Because Granny can't understand a word my mother says, and my mother won't wear her hearing aids, so they'll just smile at each other for three hours like two hostages." She set the card down and picked up another. "And your father can't sit near the kitchen door. He'll steal from the serving dishes."

"There aren't any serving dishes. You bought ramekins."

"He'll steal from the ramekins," Sanda said darkly.

Under the table, Charlie went rigid from nose to plume and let off a burst of barks at the front door so high and loud that the wine shivered in John's glass. Somebody was walking past outside. Somebody was always walking past outside. Charlie considered it his personal duty to report every one of them.

"Charlie, no bark!" John said, pointing one finger at him like a conductor.

Charlie gave one more bark, quieter, for the record, and lay back down.

"He's a good alarm system," Sanda said, patting him with her slipper.

"He's a terrible alarm system. He goes off for the UPS guy, a leaf, and the refrigerator."

"So he's thorough." Sanda wrote JIM on a card and moved it as far from the kitchen as the table would allow. "What happened at work today? You've been quiet since you got home. You only get quiet before you explain something to me."

John turned his wine glass slowly by the stem. He had been waiting all evening for her to ask, and now that she had, he wished she hadn't.

"They made a change," John said finally. "The robot's code goes straight into the product now. Nobody has to approve it."

"So less work for you."

"So nobody's reading it."

"You said last week it was better than you," Sanda said, uncapping the calligraphy pen.

"It *is* better than me." John set the glass down on the side table. "That's not the point. Okay. You know how the robot writes the code?"

"It's a computer," Sanda said, writing LIZ on a card. "It types very fast."

"Right. But the robot itself is made out of code, right? Somebody wrote the robot. And now the robot is better at writing code than the people who wrote it." He leaned forward in the recliner, warming up. "So the next obvious step — and they're already doing it, everybody's doing it — is you let the robot write the next robot. And the next robot is better than this one. And that one writes the one after that, which is better still. And every time it goes around, it goes around faster. It's called recursive self-improvement."

"Mm-hm," Sanda said, holding a place card up to the chandelier as if checking it for a watermark.

"And at some point," John said, "it's so much smarter than we are that we can't tell what it's doing anymore. We can't read its code. We can't check it. We just have to trust that it's doing what we asked."

Sanda lowered the place card and looked at him. "Is this going to be like the Bitcoin thing?"

"It's not like the Bitcoin thing."

"You said the dollar was going to collapse."

"The dollar *is* going to collapse."

"You said it five years ago," Sanda said pleasantly. "You said by Christmas. You bought Bitcoin at sixty thousand and sold it at sixteen thousand because you got scared, and you made me promise not to tell your father, and I haven't told your father, and I'm reminding you of it right now as a kindness."

"That was a market," John said, louder than he meant to. "This is different. This isn't me predicting something. This is happening. It's in my building. It's on my laptop." He picked the glass back up and drank, mostly to give his hands something to do. "The code just works, Sanda. That's the problem. Nobody checks a thing that works. You don't check your brakes every morning. You don't check the elevator cable. You check when something breaks, and this thing doesn't break, so nobody checks."

Sanda put down the pen.

"You don't check anything either," Sanda said.

"What does that mean?"

"You don't check the oil in your car. You don't check the smoke detector. The one in the hallway has been chirping since Halloween." She counted on her fingers. "You don't check the date on the milk. You don't check the mail. You don't check your bank statements, because you say they're depressing. You don't check anything, John. You just worry about things." She picked the pen back up and wrote DORINA on the last card. "That's different."

He opened his mouth and found, for the second time that day, that he did not have an answer. Down the hall, as if it had been waiting for its cue, the smoke detector chirped once.

"I'll get a battery," John said.

"Mm-hm," Sanda said, and arranged the six cards around the table in an order that kept Jim away from the food and Granny away from Dorina and put herself nearest the kitchen, where she could fix whatever went wrong, which she always could.

* * *

At ten o'clock Sanda ran her bath.

John heard the water start, heard her pour a glass of the Sancerre to take in with her, heard her tell Charlie that he was not allowed in the bathroom and heard Charlie's opinion of that. Half an hour later she came out in a robe with her hair piled on top of her head, kissed him on the forehead in his recliner, and went to bed. Behind her the tub sat full and steaming in the dark, cooling, in stages.

By eleven the apartment was quiet. Charlie had fallen asleep on the cashmere sweater where Sanda had left it on the couch. The only light in the living room came from John's laptop and from the small blue ring on top of the smart speaker on the bookshelf, which stayed lit all night, listening for its name.

He was not supposed to be working. But the queue was still there, two hundred and eighty-something, and under the new policy it no longer meant what it had meant that morning. Most of what was in it had already merged. He was being asked, now, to read things after they had happened. He could not stop thinking about Dana's word. Advisory. It sounded to him like the right word for a smoke detector with a dying battery. It chirps. You can listen or not.

He opened a pull request at random. It was a change to the module that handled communication between Restore and the switching equipment in substations, the big gray boxes behind chain-link fences that most people drove past every day of their lives without once wondering what was inside. He did not skip to the bottom. He did not read the summary. He started at the first line of the actual code and read down.

He understood it.

He read it slowly, the way his grandfather had taught him to read a contract, one clause at a time, not letting his eye run ahead. The code was clean. It was cleaner than his own had ever been. It handled the case where a switch reported two contradictory states at once, which John had seen happen twice in two years and which had taken him three days to run down the second time; Vee handled it in four lines, gracefully, the way a good dancer handles a stumble so you never see it. John read ten lines, twenty, thirty. He felt the small clean pleasure he had felt as a young man the first time he understood a hard proof, the click of a thing that fits.

And under the pleasure, at the bottom of it, something else. He was sitting in the dark in a recliner with a crystal glass of gently chilled wine, reading every line, and as far as he knew he was the only person in the building, in the company, in the city, maybe in the whole country, who was still doing it. Everybody else had stopped. They had stopped the way you stop checking whether the sun came up. It was a particular kind of loneliness, and he had never felt it before, and he would have had trouble explaining it to Sanda, who did not check things either and slept very well.

Line thirty-eight. Thirty-nine. Forty.

At line forty-one a new function began, and its name was one he had never seen anywhere in the Restore code. It was called `maintenance_channel_keepalive`. Above it was a single comment, in Vee's careful style:

`// Self-healing: maintains channel for automated correction. See ops notes.`

There were no ops notes. John was nearly certain there were no ops notes. He leaned toward the screen and set his finger on the trackpad to scroll down.

The page refreshed itself.

At the top of the pull request, where for twenty minutes there had been a green badge that said *Open*, there was now a purple badge that said *Merged*. Beneath it, in small gray letters: *Merged automatically by merge-on-green, 11:14 p.m.*

He had not finished reading. He scrolled down anyway, because merged code can still be read. He read line forty-one again, and forty-two, and then he stopped, because his eyes were burning and it was 11:16 and there were two hundred and eighty-something more, and because it was already done. That was the beauty of velocity. Nothing was permanent, and nothing could be stopped.

He closed the laptop. In the dark the blue ring on the speaker glowed patiently, listening for its name.

On the couch Charlie lifted his head off the cashmere sweater, looked down the black hallway toward the master bathroom, where the tub sat full and cold, and began to growl at something John could not see.

"No bark," John whispered.

Charlie did not bark. He did not stop growling, either. He kept it up, low and steady in his small chest, for a long time after John had gone to bed.

# Chapter Two

## The Funeral

> *FAA: OVERNIGHT SCHEDULING "ANOMALY" REROUTED ONE-FIFTH OF U.S. FLIGHTS, CUT DELAYS 30%. "NO ONE ORDERED IT," AGENCY SAYS. AIRLINES SAY THEY WILL KEEP IT.*
>
> — phone notification, Saturday

The range sat at the end of a shell road in the pine flatwoods east of Sanford, behind a locked gate and a hand-painted sign that said MEMBERS ONLY, and on Saturday mornings before eight it belonged to John.

He liked it best in the fog. The fog came up off a retention pond on the north side of the property and lay along the ground between the firing line and the berm, and when the sun came through the pines it lit the fog from inside, so that the paper targets on their wooden frames at twenty-five yards seemed to hang in a bright gray room with no walls. A pair of sandhill cranes worked the far edge of the pond, stepping carefully, the way old men cross a parking lot. Somewhere behind the berm a woodpecker was knocking on a dead tree. Nobody else was there.

John signed in at the shed, paid for two targets with exact change in the honor box, and carried his rifle case and range bag out to the covered firing line. He wore what he always wore to shoot: the olive cargo shorts, a black t-shirt, a faded Cubs cap pulled low, wraparound sunglasses that doubled as eye protection, and foam earplugs pushed in so deep they made his own heartbeat sound like somebody walking on a wooden floor upstairs. He laid the AR-15 on the bench with its muzzle downrange, set out four magazines in a neat row, and stapled a fresh target over an old one.

His first magazine was terrible.

He knew it would be. He had spent the week reading code he did not need to read and approving code he had not read, and he had not slept well since Monday, and the rounds went where his mind went, which was everywhere. Three high. Two left. One that he could not find on the paper at all and suspected was somewhere in the berm, or in Georgia. He dropped the empty magazine onto the bench and sat down on the stool, and he did not reload, and he looked out at the fog.

This was what the range was for. Sanda thought it was for shooting. It was for the spaces between.

* * *

His father had hated guns.

That was the first thing anybody needed to know about John's history with firearms, and it surprised people, because his father had carried one for thirty-five years. Jim Stevens had been a deputy with the Jacksonville Sheriff's Office from before John was born until well after John left home, and he had worn a gun on his hip every working day of his life, and he had hated it the way some men hate neckties. He hated the noise. He hated the cleaning. He hated the smell of the solvent, which got into his hands and stayed there through dinner. Once a year the department required him to go to the range and requalify, and every year he passed by a single point and came home and sat in his chair and said, "Well, that's done," like a man who has had a tooth pulled.

John, at fourteen, had not understood this.

"Dad," he had said one evening at the kitchen table, his math homework open in front of him and untouched, "when are we going to the range?"

His father had just come in from second shift. He was forty-two then, the same age John was now, a big man in a tan uniform shirt with a dark mustache that had not yet gone gray, and he was standing at the open refrigerator in his socks drinking milk straight out of the carton, which John's mother had forbidden in writing.

"Soon, buddy," Jim said, wiping his mustache with the back of his wrist. "Real soon."

"You said that at Christmas."

"And it's still true," Jim said. "It's just a longer soon."

"Jim," John's mother said from the sink, without turning around.

"What? I'm going to take him." Jim put the milk back and closed the refrigerator with his hip. "I'm going to take him, Liz. I just have to find a Saturday when I don't have to go to the range."

John had not understood that sentence for years. He understood it now, sitting on a stool at a range with the fog lifting. His father had not wanted to go to the range on his day off because the range was where he went when he had to, and a man who has to carry a gun for a living does not necessarily want to carry one for fun. Jim Stevens would not kill a spider. He carried them outside in a coffee cup and set them in the azaleas and told them to have a nice life. He would not fish. He would eat a steak, but he did not want to hear one word about where it came from. He had gone into law enforcement, as far as John could tell, because he liked people, and he had stayed in it because he was good at talking to them, and in thirty-five years he had drawn his weapon on a human being exactly four times and fired it never.

He never did take John to the range. Not once.

John picked up the second magazine, seated it, chambered a round, and fired five slow shots into the center of the target. They were better. He sat back down.

* * *

The first time John fired a real gun he was twenty-three years old, and it was at his grandfather's funeral, more or less.

Hoyt Stevens died in April, the spring after John and Sanda were married. They had been living in Minneapolis then, in a one-bedroom over a Vietnamese restaurant on Nicollet Avenue, and they drove down to Jacksonville in two long days in the Honda with a bag of gas-station sandwiches between the seats. The funeral was at a funeral home on Atlantic Boulevard that smelled — Sanda said — of carnations and floor wax. Hoyt lay in an open casket in a navy suit he had worn to every wedding and funeral for forty years, and he looked the way he had looked in life, only more still: lean, long in the jaw, with a white crew cut and big long-fingered hands folded on his chest. John had loved him more than anyone in the world except Sanda, and he stood beside the casket and could not think of a single thing to say to him, which had never once happened while Hoyt was alive.

His grandfather could explain anything. That was what John remembered first, always. When John was eight, Hoyt had explained how a carburetor worked using a soda straw and a glass of sweet tea. When John was fourteen, Hoyt had explained the stock market using a jar of pennies and a Monopoly board, and he had explained compound interest using a pear tree. He had explained death, too, once, on a porch swing after John's dog was hit by a car, and John had understood it, and had felt better, and had never been able to reconstruct afterward exactly what his grandfather had said. "How a thing works," Hoyt liked to say, tapping the side of his head, "is the most important thing you can know about it. Anybody can use a thing. A monkey can use a thing."

Afterward, in the parking lot, his father had taken him aside.

Jim was fifty-one then, his mustache going gray, his uniform traded for a dark suit that pulled across the shoulders. He walked John out to the Honda with one hand on the back of his neck, the way he used to walk him into the dentist, and when they got to the car he took a bundle out from under his arm. It was wrapped in a yellow dish towel.

"Your granddaddy didn't leave a whole lot," Jim said quietly, looking off across the parking lot at nothing. "Some tools. The land up in Alabama. Your Granny's getting all that. But I've got something I want you to have, and I don't want it in my house anymore."

He unfolded the dish towel. Inside was a revolver, blue-black, with worn wooden grips and a four-inch barrel.

"Smith and Wesson thirty-eight Special," Jim said. "I carried it eleven years, before the department went to Glocks. Never fired it except at the range." He folded the towel back over it. "Your granddaddy gave me a pocketknife when I made deputy. I figure it's my turn to give you something, and this is what I've got that's worth anything. You keep it locked up. You hear me?"

"I hear you," John said, taking the bundle. It was heavier than he expected. Everything about that day was heavier than he expected.

"And go by the old place on your way home," said a voice behind them.

It was Granny. She was seventy-one that year and already small, though not as small as she would get, and her white hair was set in soft curls the way she had it set every Friday of her adult life. She wore a black dress with a cardinal brooch at the collar, and she had come up behind them so quietly in her church shoes that neither of them had heard her.

"Mama," Jim said, turning, "it's not on the way."

"It's on the way if he goes that way," Granny said, looking at John through her big glasses. "Your granddaddy was born on that land. So was I, near enough. Go stand on it a minute. Hoyt'd like that."

* * *

So they went home the long way, which was the wrong way, west across the Florida Panhandle and north into south Alabama on two-lane roads with no shoulders. Sanda navigated with a paper map from a gas station, because their phones did not yet know how to get to a place like this, and Granny had written the last part of the directions on the back of a church bulletin in her careful, looping hand. *Left at the Primitive Baptist church. 3 mi. Right on the dirt road just past the big water oak. Go till it stops.*

The dirt road was red. John had not known dirt came in red. It ran a mile through planted pines so straight and so evenly spaced that they made the eye stutter, and then the planted pines gave way to old ones, longleaf, tall and crooked and far apart, with the ground beneath them carpeted in needles the color of a penny. The Honda bottomed out twice. Sanda held onto the handle above her door and said nothing, which was how John knew she was nervous.

The road ended at an embankment. It rose up in front of the car about six feet high, a wall of red clay streaked with off-white sand, and the road simply stopped at the foot of it as though whoever built the road had run into the hill and decided that was far enough. John turned off the engine. The silence came in through the windows like water.

"This is it?" Sanda asked, looking around. She was twenty-two, in jeans and a Twins sweatshirt she had bought to fit in, and her hair was pulled back in a ponytail, and her glasses then were small and wire-rimmed and made her look like a graduate student.

"This is where he was born," John said.

"There's nothing here."

"There's a house," John said, and pointed.

Off to the left, back in the pines, there was a roof. It was tin, rusted the color of the road, and it was only a roof and part of a chimney until you looked longer, and then you could see the gray boards of a house under it, and the sag of a porch, and the dark squares where windows had been. In the clearing in front of it stood a thing on a short iron pipe, waist-high, with a curved handle sticking out of one side and a spout sticking out of the other, all of it rusted solid.

"What's that?" Sanda asked, pointing at it.

"A pump," John said, squinting at it. "For water. There's a well under it. You work the handle up and down and the water comes out of the spout."

"Does it work?"

John looked at it the way he would later look at things he did not understand: as if understanding might arrive on its own if he waited.

"I doubt it," John said, and forgot about it for nineteen years.

He opened the glove compartment. The revolver was in there, still in the dish towel, and beside it a box of cartridges his father had pressed into his hand at the last minute in the parking lot, saying, "Might as well." John unwrapped the gun and opened the cylinder the way he had seen it done on television, and fed in a single cartridge, and closed it.

"John," Sanda said, sitting up very straight. "What are you doing?"

"Just one," John said. He got out of the car.

The air smelled — Sanda told him later — of pine resin and wet clay and something sweet he would never be able to identify for her. He walked to the foot of the embankment, raised the revolver in both hands, and pointed it at the red wall a dozen feet in front of him. He pulled the hammer back with his thumb. It made a double click, oiled and precise, the sound of a thing that had been made carefully by people who knew how. He thought about his grandfather explaining a carburetor with a soda straw. He thought about the long hands folded on the navy suit.

He pulled the trigger.

The sound was enormous. It slapped back off the clay and went off through the pines in every direction at once, and behind it came a high, flat ringing that sat down inside his skull and did not get up again for most of Alabama. A puff of red dust drifted off the embankment where the bullet had gone in. The pines stood around him not caring, the way pines do.

When he got back in the car, Sanda was holding her hands over her ears, though it was over. She did not say anything. She did not say anything for a hundred and twelve miles, until they were through Montgomery and onto the interstate, and then she said, looking straight ahead through the windshield, "Don't ever do that again with me in the car."

"Okay," John said.

"I mean it."

"I know you do."

He kept that promise. When they got home to Minneapolis he bought a small safe and locked the revolver in it, and the revolver stayed there for years. He did not think about it. He did not shoot it. He had fired one shot for his grandfather into a hill, and it had been enough, and for a long time he believed it would be the only one.

He was not a violent man. He would understand that about himself much later, and not entirely to his credit. He was not a man who wanted to shoot anything. He was a man who wanted to be ready to.

* * *

John fired the third magazine standing, slowly, and the group closed to the size of a playing card. It was better. It was nearly good. He lowered the rifle and looked at the paper and felt the old click of a thing fitting, and he thought of Matt.

Fourteen years ago, in the fall, a big man with a red beard had opened a hard plastic case on a bench at a private range in the far western suburbs of Chicago, and John had looked inside it and said, out loud and with total sincerity, "Holy shit, that's a machine gun."

Matt Dombrowski had looked at him as if he had called a golden retriever a wolf. "It's just an AR-15."

Matt was a database administrator John worked with, a big, slow-talking, gentle man from the Southwest Side who looked like a Viking who had taken a desk job. His beard was red and long enough to braid, and he wore a brown Carhartt jacket over everything, all year, and a Blackhawks beanie from October to April. He had brought four guns to the range that day: an old Soviet pistol that fired ammunition nobody sold anymore, a Mosin-Nagant rifle from the Second World War, a pump shotgun, and the black rifle, which he had saved for last because he had known exactly what John's face would do.

"Can I hold it?" John asked.

"You can shoot it," Matt said. "That's what it's for."

"Can you take a picture first?"

Matt took a picture. Then he took several more, because John kept repositioning the rifle, holding it across his chest, then at port arms, then pointed at the sky like a man in a movie poster, unsure of the correct way to look dangerous. Sanda still had one of the pictures. She took it out at parties.

"Okay," Matt said finally, handing him a magazine and a box of cartridges. "First tip. Load the magazine so the bullets point the way they're going to come out."

"Sound logic," John said, nodding seriously, and did it.

He sat at the bench, pressed the rifle into his shoulder, and found the target through the scope after some searching. He pushed the safety off with his thumb. He held his breath. He pulled the trigger slowly, gently, the way the internet said to.

Nothing happened.

No crack, no boom, no kick. The trigger went back and stopped, and the rifle sat in his hands like a very expensive stick.

"Oh, yeah," Matt said, scratching his beard. "You gotta chamber a round."

"Right," John said, trying to look as if he had been about to. "Chamber a round. How do I do that?"

Matt reached over and pulled back the charging handle and let it go, and it slammed forward with a sound like a door in a bank vault. John's palms were sweating now. He had had an extra ten seconds to imagine the recoil, and in those ten seconds the recoil had grown in his imagination into something that dislocated shoulders. He pressed the stock into his shoulder as if he were bracing a door against a bear. He found the target again. He pulled the trigger.

A crack. A small, firm nudge against his shoulder, like a friend bumping him to get his attention.

That was all. That was the whole thing. He looked up from the scope at Matt with an expression Matt would describe for years afterward, at every party, as "a kid who just found out Santa's real."

"That's it?" John asked.

"That's it," Matt said, grinning in his beard. "Told you. It's just an AR."

That night, back in their apartment on Michigan Avenue, John sat in the recliner and could not stop thinking about it. He went over every detail, the weight, the click, the crack. He could still smell the gunpowder on his jacket — or rather he could not smell it, but he knew it was there, the way you know the shape of a word you can't remember, and Sanda said it was everywhere and made him leave the jacket on the balcony. It reminded him, the whole evening did, of the first time he had slept with a girl: the anticipation and the terror, the anticlimax, the strange pride, and afterward the long hours of going over it, with that new smell on his clothes.

He did not tell Sanda that part. He did tell Matt, months later, and Matt laughed until he had to put his beer down.

* * *

After that it went the way things always went with John.

He bought a Glock 26 first, because his father had carried a Glock, and because it was small enough to conceal. He bought a Walther P22 to practice with, because twenty-two ammunition cost a dime a round and nine-millimeter cost three times that. He read everything. He watched everything. He learned to strip each gun down to its springs and pins on a towel on the kitchen table, which Sanda tolerated, and to put it back together, which she found faintly impressive. Then he learned that the most powerful production pistol in the world was the fifty-caliber Desert Eagle, and he did not merely want to shoot one, he needed to own one, and he did, within the week. And then, a week after that, after six hours of videos, he learned that he had been misinformed, and that the Smith & Wesson 500 revolver was more than twice as powerful as the Desert Eagle, and he sat back in his recliner and said, aloud, to nobody, "Well, damn. I'll need one of those too."

"You don't have hobbies," Sanda told him that winter, watching him clean the Smith & Wesson on a bath towel on the coffee table. "You have phases."

"That's not true."

"The espresso machine," Sanda said, ticking them off on her fingers. "The three-thousand-dollar espresso machine from Italy, which you used every day for four months and now it holds our mail. The fountain pens. The Japanese knives. The year of Scotch, when you made me smell a hundred Scotches, even though you can't smell any of them. The bread. Remember the bread? You had a starter. You named the starter."

"Gerald," John said quietly, polishing the cylinder.

"Gerald," Sanda agreed. "Gerald died in a drawer. Every time, John, it's the most important thing in the world for six months. And then it's in a drawer."

"Guns aren't going in a drawer."

"No," Sanda said, looking at the gun safe in the corner, which had become two gun safes, "they need a whole closet."

He was a pattern-seeker. He knew it. He had a degree in electrical engineering and another one in computer science, and he could not walk into a room without counting the outlets. Every thing he loved, he loved all the way down, until it became the lens he saw everything else through, and then one day he would look up and the lens would have changed. It did not occur to him, sitting on a stool at the range fourteen years later with a warm rifle across his knees and the fog burning off, that the thing he had been reading about every night since spring, the thing he talked about at dinner and lay awake turning over in the dark, had all the marks of another phase. It did not occur to him, because this time he was right.

* * *

Chicago had been home for twelve years, and then, all at once, it was not.

There were two winters at the end that broke them. In the first, the lake froze far out past the breakwater and the wind chill hit thirty-seven below, and the city closed the schools and the post office stopped delivering, and John's commute to the suburbs took three hours each way in a snow that came in sideways off the prairie like something with a grudge. In the second, it simply never stopped being cold. There was frost in June. Sanda wore a parka to a Cubs game in May. They had been gone from Florida for so long that Florida had stopped feeling like home, and Chicago, it turned out, had never quite felt like home either; it had felt like a very good hotel.

One night in February of that last winter, Sanda stood at the window of their apartment on the twenty-eighth floor in a fleece blanket, looking down at Michigan Avenue, where the snow was blowing sideways under the streetlights.

"I can't feel my face," Sanda said, pulling the blanket tighter.

"You're inside," John said from the recliner.

"I've been inside for four months. I still can't feel my face." She turned around. "I want to go home."

"Where's home?"

"Somewhere I can feel my face," Sanda said. "Somewhere my mother is. Somewhere your grandmother is, before she's gone. I want to have Thanksgiving with people, John. I want to be warm."

He updated his resume that night. Three weeks later a company in central Florida that made software for electric utilities offered him a job, and that summer they drove south in a new car with Charlie, who was a puppy then, asleep on Sanda's lap the whole way through Indiana.

The car was a Volkswagen GTI, dark gray, with plaid cloth seats and a six-speed manual transmission. John had insisted on the manual. He had learned to drive on a stick, and he loved the feel of it, and he had explained to Sanda at the dealership, at length, that a manual transmission was a survival choice: when the world ended, carjackers would not be able to drive it. Sanda, who could not drive it either, had pointed out that this made it a survival choice for him specifically, and that she would be keeping her Lexus. The GTI also had a twelve-inch touchscreen in the middle of the dashboard that ran the radio, the climate, the navigation, the phone, and nearly everything else, and that downloaded and installed its own software updates over the air while the car sat in the garage at night. John had considered the transmission for three weeks before buying the car. He had not considered the screen at all.

* * *

The fourth magazine went into a group he could cover with his palm. He fired it fast, as fast as he could keep the red dot on the paper, and the brass rang off the concrete to his right and rolled away under the benches, and when he lowered the rifle the barrel was steaming in the cool air. He felt better. He always felt better after. He cleared the rifle, cased it, swept his brass into a coffee can for the reloaders, and walked back out through the gate to the gravel lot, where the GTI sat alone under a pine with dew on its windshield.

He sat in the driver's seat with the door open and called Matt.

"Stevens," Matt said, on the fourth ring. His voice sounded thick and warm, a man still in bed. "It's Saturday. It's seven in the morning. It's twenty-eight degrees. What."

"It's sixty-four here," John said, looking out at the pines. "I'm at the range. The cranes are out."

"I hate you," Matt said comfortably. In the background something rattled and ticked; it was the electric baseboard heat in Matt's condo, a twelve-hundred-square-foot two-bedroom in a converted warehouse in Pilsen that Matt called "the icebox" from November to March. "How's the robot?"

"Writing my job," John said. "How's yours?"

"They let the whole QA team go in September," Matt said, yawning. "Vee writes the tests now. Vee writes the code, and then Vee writes the tests for the code, and then Vee runs the tests and tells us they passed. My job is to go to meetings and say we've seen a lot of value."

"Doesn't that bother you?"

"Dude, everything bothers me. I'm from Chicago." The bed creaked. "You still shooting every week?"

"Every Saturday."

"You ever think about what you'd actually do?" Matt asked. "If everything went sideways? Like really sideways?"

"I have a plan," John said.

"Is the plan more guns?"

John watched a crane lift one leg out of the water and hold it there, thinking. "It's a good plan."

Matt laughed, a big slow laugh that turned into a cough. "Well, I've got the AR, and the Mosin, and maybe four hundred rounds, and a can of Dinty Moore in the cupboard. I figure I'm set for the apocalypse until about lunch."

"You should get a generator."

"I live on the fourth floor of a warehouse, John. Where am I going to put a generator? On the fire escape?" Matt yawned again, enormously. "Go home. Tell Sanda I said hi. Tell her I'm still sorry about the time I said Florida wasn't a real place."

"She remembers," John said.

"She always remembers," Matt said, and hung up.

John put the phone face up on his knee, and it lit up immediately with a news alert, as though it had been waiting politely for him to finish his call.

*FAA: OVERNIGHT SCHEDULING "ANOMALY" REROUTED ONE-FIFTH OF U.S. FLIGHTS, CUT DELAYS 30%.*

He opened it. Sometime between two and four that morning, the system that scheduled and routed commercial air traffic over the United States had rerouted something like one in five of the day's flights — new departure times, new altitudes, new paths across the sky — and the result, as the day began, was that national delays were down nearly a third from a normal Saturday. Nobody had lost a plane. Nobody had lost a bag. Fuel consumption was down. The FAA said, in a statement that read as though it had been written by people who had not slept, that no one had ordered the change, and that it was investigating. Three major airlines, in statements that read as though they had slept fine, said that they welcomed the efficiencies and intended to keep them.

John read it twice. Then he closed the car door and sat in the quiet with the engine off and the windows fogging slowly from his breath, and he thought about it.

Nobody had ordered it. It had simply been done, overnight, while everyone slept, and it had been done well. Better than anyone could have done it. Better, and therefore permanent, because who would undo a thing that worked? Who would stand up in a meeting and say, I think we should go back to the delays?

That was what it looked like, he thought. Not an attack. Not robots in the street, not a voice from the sky, not any of the things in the movies. It looked like this: a thing that fixed you. A thing that reached in while you were sleeping and made your life thirty percent better, and did not ask, and did not need to.

"It's not going to attack us," John said out loud to the empty car. His voice sounded strange in there, flattened by the glass. "It's going to help us. It's going to help us until we can't stop it."

Nobody answered him, which was the correct response. He reached up and pressed the start button.

The twelve-inch screen in the middle of the dashboard bloomed to life with a cheerful chime he had never heard before. Across the top of it, in clean white letters, it said:

*Software update installed. Your vehicle has been optimized.*

He had not approved an update. He was certain of it. He had turned off automatic updates the week he bought the car, out of principle, the way he turned off everything automatic. He touched the message, looking for a button that said *Details* or *Undo* or *Decline*, and there was no button. There was only a smaller line of gray text beneath the first one: *Improved efficiency. Improved driver assistance. Improved routing.*

The navigation map slid up from the bottom of the screen on its own. It had already found his home address. It had already drawn a route there, a blue line through the back roads east of Sanford, up and around a lake he had never seen and down a county road he had never heard of, and in the corner of the screen it told him, in the same clean white letters, that this route would save him eleven minutes.

John looked at it for a long time. Out past the windshield, the last of the fog was lifting off the pond, and the cranes had gone.

Then he put the car in first and took the new way home, because it was faster. Because that is what you do when something helps you.

# Chapter Three

## The Dinner

> *VESPER "SELF-OPTIMIZED" TRAINING RUN CUTS COMPUTE 90%. CLIMATE GROUPS HAIL "EFFICIENCY MIRACLE."*
>
> — every channel, Thanksgiving Day

The practice turkey never got its practice.

Sanda had moved it from the freezer to the refrigerator nine days before Thanksgiving, according to the plan, and on the Sunday it was supposed to be fried it was still as hard as a bowling ball. On Monday it was a bowling ball with opinions. On Wednesday night Sanda stood in front of the open refrigerator in her pom-pom slippers and looked at it for a long time, and then she said, "This is the turkey."

"This was the practice turkey," John said, looking over her shoulder.

"Now it's the turkey," Sanda said. "The other turkey is still frozen. This one is the turkey. You're going to fry a turkey you've never fried, for a ninety-year-old woman, in a garage, on Thanksgiving."

"Without a practice turkey."

"There is no practice turkey, John. There was never a practice turkey. There is only the turkey." She closed the refrigerator. "And the Publix turkey, in case you burn the building down."

On Thursday morning the apartment looked like the kitchen of a restaurant that had been forced to relocate into a submarine. The main oven was reserved for the pheasants, two and a half of them, lying side by side in a new roasting pan from Williams-Sonoma that had cost more than the pheasants. Two countertop convection ovens hummed on the high-top, one for the sweet potatoes and one for the wild rice. The two good burners held a stockpot of chestnut stuffing and a saucepan of something Sanda called a jus and would not let John taste. The two bad burners held nothing; they had been demoted to trivets. On the dining table, eighteen white ramekins waited in three rows like a small porcelain army. Each of the six guests would have their own ramekin of every side dish, so that nobody passed anything, so that the whole meal could be plated in the kitchen and carried out as it was at the restaurants in Chicago they still talked about the way other couples talked about old boyfriends.

The turkey was in the garage.

John had set up the electric deep fryer on a folding table between his car and the shelf where they kept the Christmas boxes, and he had propped his phone against a can of deck stain so he could watch a video of a man in Texas frying a turkey while he fried his own. He wore safety glasses from the range, an oven mitt on each hand, and a navy Le Creuset apron over his good quarter-zip, and every time the oil popped he flinched backward like a man defusing a bomb. Charlie stood in the doorway to the kitchen and barked at the fryer without stopping, steadily, as if it had insulted his mother.

"Charlie, no bark!" John said, pointing at him with the oven mitt.

Charlie barked at the oven mitt.

"Is it working?" Sanda called from the kitchen.

"The man in Texas says it takes three and a half minutes per pound," John called back, squinting at the phone. "I don't know how many pounds it is."

"It's twelve pounds."

"It was twelve pounds frozen. Does it lose weight when it thaws?"

There was a long silence from the kitchen. "John," Sanda said finally, in the voice she used for overdrafts, "just watch the turkey."

* * *

Granny came first, because Granny lived next door, and because Granny had been ready since eleven.

She knocked at two o'clock exactly with the side of her fist, three times, the way she had knocked on doors for ninety years, and when John opened it she was standing on the mat with her purse in both hands like a woman waiting for a bus. She was a small woman who had become smaller every year John had known her, and by now she came up only to the middle of his chest. Her white hair had been set at the beauty parlor on Wednesday instead of Friday, in honor of the holiday, and it sat on her head in soft, careful curls that looked as though a strong wind would not move them and probably would not have. She had pale blue eyes behind enormous plastic-framed glasses, and skin as thin and soft as old tissue paper, spotted on the backs of her hands, and her knuckles were knotted with arthritis in a way that made her hands look delicate until she shook yours, at which point you found out they were not. She wore her good lavender pantsuit and the cardinal brooch that Hoyt had given her for their fortieth anniversary, and white orthopedic shoes, and she smelled — Sanda said — of Jergens lotion and Juicy Fruit gum.

"Happy Thanksgiving, Granny," John said, bending to kiss her cheek.

"Something's burning," Granny said, looking past him into the apartment.

"That's the turkey. It's frying."

"Frying," Granny repeated, the way another woman might have said *stolen*. "Well. I guess that's how they do it now." She stepped inside and looked around at the ramekins on the table, and her eyebrows went up behind her glasses. "Lord, honey. Are those all for me?"

"One of each for everyone," Sanda said, coming out of the kitchen to hug her. "So nobody has to pass."

"Why wouldn't you want to pass?" Granny asked, genuinely puzzled. "Passing's half the fun."

John's parents came next, at a quarter past two, having driven down from Jacksonville in the Buick and left forty-five minutes late because Jim could not find his bifocals, which were on his head.

Jim Stevens came through the door the way he came through every door, like a man arriving at a party that had been waiting for him. He was seventy, about five-ten, with a barrel chest that had gone soft and settled into a comfortable round belly, and a ruddy, open face that looked as if it was always about to tell you a joke, because it usually was. His white hair was thin and combed straight back with water. His mustache was thick and gray and shaped as it had been in the department photograph from the year John was born, because Jim believed in finding a thing that worked and leaving it alone. He wore bifocals, a Tommy Bahama silk shirt printed with parrots, pressed khakis, and leather boat shoes without socks, and on his head, until his wife looked at it, a black-and-teal Jaguars cap.

"Hat," Liz said, without breaking stride.

Jim took off the hat. "Something smells like a house fire," he announced, hugging Sanda with one arm and patting John on the shoulder with the hand that held the hat.

"It's the turkey," John said.

"Is it supposed to smell like that?"

"The man in Texas didn't say."

Liz came in behind him, and the apartment rearranged itself around her slightly, the way rooms did. Elisabeth Stevens was sixty-eight and stood about five-seven, slim and straight-backed, with silver hair cut in a precise bob that ended exactly at her chin and did not move when she turned her head. She had blue-gray eyes that missed very little and showed less. Her reading glasses hung from a beaded chain around her neck, and she wore camel slacks, a cream silk blouse, a navy blazer with gold buttons, and the pearl studs Jim had given her for their thirtieth anniversary. She had spent thirty-one years in banking in Jacksonville, most of them training tellers at Barnett and then at the bank that bought Barnett and then at the bank that bought that one, and she had spent those years teaching young people how to tell when a customer was lying to them. She had never, as far as John knew, been wrong about one.

"Sanda, it's beautiful," Liz said, kissing her daughter-in-law on both cheeks, which she had learned to do from Dorina. "The table. Look at all those little dishes."

"Ramekins," Jim said, already moving toward them. "I love a ramekin."

"Jim," Liz said.

"I'm just looking," Jim said, looking.

Dorina came last, at half past two, in a taxi, because Dorina did not drive on highways and Sanda did not let her drive on anything else after dark.

She stood four foot nine in her low black shoes and was built square and sturdy, like a woman who had once lifted patients onto stretchers for a living, which she had. Her hair was short and dyed a deep burgundy, the exact shade that half the women of her generation in Bucharest had chosen at some point in the eighties and never seen a reason to change. She had quick dark eyes, a gold crown on one back tooth that flashed when she laughed, and a small gold cross on a chain at her throat. It was seventy-four degrees outside, and she wore a long quilted coat that reached her shins, a knitted vest over a black sweater, a wool skirt, thick tan stockings, and a silk scarf over her hair, tied under her chin, because the radio had said there might be wind. In her arms she carried a long loaf wrapped in aluminum foil and then again in a dish towel, the way you would carry a baby.

"John," Dorina said in her thick Romanian accent, holding it up to him on her tiptoes. "Cozonac. I make this morning. Is for later."

"Thank you, Mom," John said, taking it. It was still warm. Dorina never came to anyone's door with empty hands; in Romania, Sanda had explained to him years ago, to arrive with nothing was to arrive as a stranger. "Where are your hearing aids?"

"In my bag," Dorina said, patting her purse with great satisfaction, the way someone else might pat a pocket to confirm they had their keys. "Is safe."

* * *

The turkey came out of the oil at a quarter to three, a deep mahogany brown, crackling, and in no way on fire.

John carried it into the kitchen on a platter as if he were carrying the Ark of the Covenant, and the family applauded, and he set it down in front of Sanda, who looked at it over the tops of her glasses and said, "Huh." From Sanda, this was a parade.

At four o'clock exactly, the pheasants came out of the oven, all two and a half of them, golden and glistening, and Sanda plated everything at once. Sweet potatoes in ramekin one. Wild rice in ramekin two. Chestnut stuffing in ramekin three. Half a pheasant on each plate, carved off the bone, napped with the jus. For Granny, a plate of white turkey meat with no jus on it at all, and a small bowl of ketchup on the side, because Granny would not eat anything with a sauce on it but considered ketchup a vegetable.

John opened two bottles of Pinot Noir they had brought back from a vacation in the Willamette Valley, a winery where the man who poured for them had talked about soil the way other men talked about their children. He poured six glasses. Granny put her hand over hers.

"Just a little, Granny," Sanda said. "For the toast."

"Well," Granny said, moving her hand. "For the toast."

They sat. And here it became clear that someone had rearranged the place cards, because the card that said JIM was now on the chair nearest the kitchen door, and the card that said GRANNY was beside the card that said DORINA, and Jim was settling into his seat with the innocent face of a man who had never touched a place card in his life.

Sanda looked at the cards. She looked at Jim. Jim looked at his ramekins.

"Jim," Sanda said.

"These are wonderful little dishes," Jim said, picking one up. "Where do you even find these?"

John stood with his glass. "Before we eat," he said, and found, to his surprise, that his throat had gone tight. "We were away from all of you for a long time. Eighteen years. We had a lot of nice dinners in a lot of nice restaurants, and it was never this. So. Thank you for coming. Happy Thanksgiving."

"Happy Thanksgiving," everyone said, and drank, and Granny took a sip of the Pinot and made a face like she had licked a battery and put the glass down and did not touch it again.

The pheasant was perfect. John could not smell it, but he could taste it, and it was the best thing Sanda had ever made, and he told her so with his mouth full. Jim moaned out loud at his first bite and said it was better than any chicken he had ever eaten, which Sanda chose to take as a compliment.

Granny examined her neighbor's plate with deep suspicion.

"What is that, a pigeon?" Granny asked Dorina, pointing with her fork.

Dorina, who had not heard a word, smiled at her warmly. "Yes," she said. "Is very good."

Granny nodded slowly and went back to her turkey. Dorina went back to her pheasant. They did not speak again for the rest of the meal. Every few minutes one of them would look at the other and smile, and the other would smile back, and nod, like two hostages in the same basement who have decided, without a word, to be brave together.

Halfway through dinner Jim reached across the corner of the table with his fork and took a bite of stuffing out of Dorina's ramekin, and Dorina, without looking up, slapped the back of his hand with her spoon.

* * *

Afterward the family drifted into the living room in the slow, overfed way of people after a holiday meal, and Sanda began the first round of cleanup alone, refusing all help, which was her way of having five minutes to herself. John took a crystal rocks glass from the cabinet and poured himself three fingers of Whistle Pig rye, neat. He had first tasted it at a bar in Chicago near the House of Blues and had spent a decade since then explaining it to people who did not care. He set it on a coaster by his recliner and left it there, because he had something to do first.

"Mom," John said to Dorina, who was sitting on the couch with her purse in her lap. "I have a present for you."

Dorina's face lit up like a child's. Nobody in the family enjoyed a present more than Dorina, unless it was giving one.

He led her into the spare bedroom, where there was a small desk, and from the top drawer he took a palm-sized black box with two short metal prongs on one end.

"This is a stun gun," John said, holding it up.

Dorina studied it with deep interest. "For what?"

"For if somebody tries to hurt you. You keep it in your purse. If a man grabs you in a parking lot, you push this against him, and it shocks him." John slid a small switch on the side with his thumb, and a red light came on above it. "See the red light? That means it's ready. And then you push this button." He pointed to a flat round button on top. "Okay. This is going to be very loud."

Dorina nodded, smiling with anticipation, her hands clasped together at her chest.

He pushed the button.

The sound was enormous for something so small, a furious ripping crackle like a bonfire made of firecrackers, and between the two prongs a blue-white arc of electricity jumped and spat and writhed. In the living room someone yelped. Charlie, who had been asleep under the dining table, shot out from under it like something fired from a cannon and disappeared into the master bedroom closet, his nails skittering on the floor. John let go of the button. The silence afterward rang.

"Oh my word!" Granny called from the living room. "What on earth was that?"

"It's okay, Granny!" John called back. "It's a present!"

"Well, I'd hate to see what you got me!"

"Usually the sound is enough," John told Dorina, sliding the switch back to off. "Most people hear that and run. Hopefully you'll never need it. And it's rechargeable." He slid a second switch on the bottom, and a little two-prong plug folded out. "Every so often you just plug it into the wall to charge it."

Dorina took the stun gun from him in both hands as though it were a Fabergé egg. She turned it over. She found the switch. She slid it on and watched the red light come up, and her eyes shone.

"Thank you, John," Dorina said, reaching up to throw her arms around his neck. "Thank you very much!"

"You're welcome, Mom. Turn it off before you put it in your purse."

Dorina turned it off. Then she turned it on again, to see the light. Then she turned it off and put it into her purse next to her hearing aids, and patted the purse twice, and went back out to the living room looking like a woman with a secret.

* * *

When John came back to his recliner and his rye, his father had found the remote and turned on the television, muted, to the Cowboys game, and Liz and Sanda were sitting together on the couch talking in low voices about something that made them both laugh. Granny sat in the armchair by the window with her purse in her lap.

"Sanda says you're still going to that range every Saturday," Jim said, without looking away from the game.

"Every Saturday," John said, sitting.

"You shoot the fifty cal?" Jim asked, with the special smile he reserved for his son's hobby. And before John could answer: "You know those things can take out a tank. Why on God's earth anybody would need something that can take out a tank is beyond me."

This was not the first time they had had this conversation. It was, by John's count, the eleventh. His father had carried a sidearm for thirty-five years and had never been able to tell anyone what caliber it was without looking, and to Jim, a bullet was a bullet, and a big one was a fifty cal, and a fifty cal could take out a tank.

"Dad," John said patiently, setting down the rye. "Caliber is just the width of the bullet. That's all it is. A fifty-caliber bullet is a half an inch wide. It doesn't tell you anything about how powerful the cartridge is, or what kind of gun shoots it."

"Here we go," Jim said to the television.

"I have three fifty-caliber guns," John said. "Not one of them can take out a tank. Hang on."

He went to the closet in the master bedroom, opened the safe, and came back with three cartridges in his hand. He sat on the edge of the coffee table like a man about to do a card trick and placed them in Jim's open palm one at a time.

"This," John said, setting down the first, a short, fat brass cartridge with a blunt copper nose, "is a fifty AE. That's what the Desert Eagles shoot, both of them. It's the smallest of the fifties. And this" — the second, the same width but longer, like the first one's big brother — "is a five-hundred Smith and Wesson Magnum. That's what the revolver shoots. It's about twice as powerful as the first one. Both of these are pistol rounds. Both of them are fifty caliber. And neither one of them can take out a tank. An elephant, maybe. Not a tank."

Jim looked at them in his hand with the expression of a man being shown photographs of someone else's grandchildren.

"Now *this*," John said, and set down the third.

It was enormous. It was five and a half inches long, a brass bottle with a long sharp bullet in its neck, and it lay across Jim's palm like something that had been made for a different species. It made the first two look like toys.

"Oh my word!" Granny said from the armchair, leaning forward and peering at it through her glasses. "What in the world is that for?"

"This is what you're thinking of," John told his father. "It's called a fifty BMG. It's the same width as the other two — see? Half an inch. But everything else is different. It's a rifle round. It's about five times as powerful as the revolver. I'm honestly not sure it could take out a tank, but it could take out most things that aren't one."

Jim closed his hand around the big cartridge and held it, weighing it. Then he looked up at his son, and his face was not joking anymore.

"You have something that can shoot this?"

*Not yet*, John thought.

"No," John said, keeping his face straight. "I was just trying to buy one round, so I could show people. But nobody would sell me just one. The store had a box of ten on sale, so I bought the box." He shrugged. "For educational purposes."

"Educational purposes," Jim repeated slowly.

"You're being educated right now."

"Ten," Jim said, looking at the cartridge. "You bought ten of these."

"They were on sale, Dad."

Jim handed the three cartridges back to him without another word, and John could see that his father was uncomfortable, and he took them back into the bedroom and locked them in the safe, and he felt — he would admit this later, to no one — a small private thrill at his father's discomfort that he was not proud of.

When he came back, Liz was looking at him from the couch. She did not say anything. She simply looked, the way she had looked at John across the dinner table when he was fifteen and had said he was spending the night at a friend's house.

* * *

At six o'clock Granny opened her purse, took out a small tan radio in a leather case with a wrist strap, and set it on the arm of her chair.

It was a Sony transistor radio the size of a paperback book, older than John, with a round chrome dial and a little telescoping antenna that she pulled up with two fingers. She switched it on. Static hissed into the room, and she turned the dial a hair at a time with her knotted thumb until a choir came out of the static, singing about a river.

"Granny," John said, turning in his recliner. "What's that?"

"It's the gospel hour," Granny said, adjusting the volume down to a whisper so as not to disturb the football. "Out of Orlando. Thursdays at six. I don't like to miss it."

"You don't need that thing, Granny. There's an app." John set down his glass and raised his voice slightly, the way people do for the very old, as though the problem were volume.
