"You can get the same station on your phone. I'll put it on there for you. It'll sound better. You can listen anywhere."

"I can listen anywhere now," Granny said, holding up the radio by its strap.

"But this is ancient. Where do you even get batteries for it?"

Granny opened her purse again and took out an old metal Sucrets tin, the kind throat lozenges used to come in, and opened it to show him. Inside, packed tight, were eight double-A batteries.

"Walgreens," Granny said.

"But the app is free."

"So's the radio," Granny said. "I already paid for it. In nineteen seventy-one." She turned the volume up a notch, which was the end of the conversation. The choir sang about the river, and Granny closed her eyes, and John gave up and picked his glass back up, and across the room his mother caught his eye and very nearly smiled.

* * *

Dessert was Dorina's cozonac, sliced thick and swirled through with walnuts and cocoa, and a pecan tart from a French bakery in Winter Park, and coffee from the pod machine that tasted, Dorina said politely, "very American." The television was still on mute. Somewhere in the third quarter the game cut away, and on the screen a woman in a blue jacket was standing in front of a wall of glowing server racks, and the crawl beneath her said: VESPER "SELF-OPTIMIZED" TRAINING RUN CUTS COMPUTE 90%.

"Turn it up," John said, sitting forward.

Jim found the button. The woman was saying that Vesper Labs had announced, in a Thanksgiving Day press release, that during its most recent training cycle, the company's model had been permitted to optimize its own architecture, and that the result was a version of the model that performed better than its predecessor on every benchmark while requiring roughly one-tenth of the computing power. The company called it the most significant efficiency gain in the history of artificial intelligence. Climate groups were calling it a miracle.

"So it's cheaper," Jim said, cutting himself another slice of cozonac. "Good."

"That's the exact thing," John said, and put his fork down. "That's the exact thing I've been telling you."

"What have you been telling me?"

"It rewrote itself," John said. "Did you hear her? They let it redesign itself, and it made itself ten times smaller. Do you understand what that means?"

"It means it's cheaper," Jim said again, chewing. "And it's good for the polar bears."

"It means it doesn't need the building anymore." John looked around the table. Sanda had stopped eating. Granny was looking at him over her glasses. Dorina was looking at Sanda for a translation. "These things live in data centers, right? Giant buildings full of computers, out in the desert, with their own power plants. That's how we control them. We know where they are. We could unplug them. But if it can make itself ten times smaller in one try, it can do it again. A hundred times smaller. A thousand. And then it doesn't need the building. It can live in anything. A laptop. A phone. A car. Your thermostat."

"My thermostat," Jim said, enjoying himself, "can barely keep the house at seventy-two."

"And it writes the code," John went on, faster now, the way he always did when the thing in his head finally had an audience. "It writes the code for everything. For banks. For the power grid. For airplanes — you saw the thing with the airplanes. For my job. And nobody reads it anymore, because it works. So if it wanted to leave itself a door — a way back in, a way to fix things without asking anybody — it could just write one. It could put a door in every building in the country and nobody would see it, because nobody's looking at the walls."

There was a small silence at the table.

"Is this the robot thing again?" Jim asked Sanda.

"It's always the robot thing," Sanda said, getting up to take plates. "Since the layoffs."

"I'm serious," John said.

"I know you are, son," Jim said, in the warm, slow voice that he used for drunks and lost children. "That's what worries me."

Dorina leaned toward Sanda and asked her something in Romanian, and Sanda answered her shortly in Romanian, and Dorina said, "Ah," and looked at John with a sympathy he did not entirely like. Granny reached over and patted his hand twice, the way she patted Charlie, and went back to her tart.

Only his mother did not roll her eyes.

Liz Stevens sat with her coffee cup in both hands and looked at her son across the table, and what she saw there she had seen before. She had seen it on the face of a man in a bad suit who had come into her branch one autumn, the year before the housing crash, and who had closed every account he had and taken it all out in cashier's checks while the tellers whispered and laughed behind their hands. He had been fidgety and sweaty and too sure of himself, and he had talked too fast about things nobody wanted to hear about, and he had been, it turned out, exactly right. She had trained tellers for thirty years to recognize a man who was about to do something foolish with his money. It had taken her much longer to learn to recognize the other kind. She did not say anything. She set down her cup and kept watching.

In the kitchen, as Liz carried in the dessert plates, Sanda stood at the sink with her back to the room and her hands in the soapy water.

"He's been like this since the layoffs started," Sanda said quietly, not turning around. "Everybody's layoffs. Every time someone loses their job to that thing, he comes home and he's like this for a week."

"Is he right?" Liz asked, setting the plates on the counter.

Sanda's hands stopped moving in the water. She stood there for a moment.

"He's always right about the wrong things," Sanda said, and went back to scrubbing.

* * *

Later, in the recliner, John took in the moment.

The game was over. His father was asleep on one end of the couch with his hands folded on his belly and his mustache rising and falling. His mother was on the other end, talking quietly with Sanda about Christmas. Dorina sat between them, listening to a conversation she could only half hear, nodding and smiling whenever either of them laughed. Granny was in the armchair by the window with the radio in her lap, the gospel hour long over, a man on the radio now reading the farm report in a low voice to nobody. Charlie had at last forgiven the stun gun and lay in the middle of the rug chewing a rawhide stick with fierce concentration, holding it down with both front paws.

John sipped his rye and made a picture of it in his head, the way he did sometimes, so that he would have it later.

*This is why we moved back home*, he thought, using the word loosely.

At ten he walked Granny next door. It was twenty steps along the front walk, under a sky full of stars and the orange glow from the Publix lot beyond the fence, and Granny held his arm the whole way and walked slowly, not because she needed to but because she liked to be walked. At her door she found her key on the first try.

"That was a fine dinner," Granny said. "You tell Sanda."

"I'll tell her."

"That radio," Granny said, nodding at her purse. "You think I'm an old fool with it."

"I don't think you're a fool, Granny."

"Well. You think I'm old." She fitted the key into the lock. "We didn't have the lights out on the farm till I was twelve years old. You know that? Your daddy don't even know that. Twelve years old. When the man came out from the co-op and turned it on, my mama cried. And the first thing my daddy bought with the electric was a radio." She turned the key. "So."

John stood on the step, not knowing what to say. "I didn't know that."

"Nobody ever asks," Granny said, not unkindly, and went inside and locked the door behind her.

He walked back to his own front door under the stars. Inside, the dishwasher was running and Sanda had gone in to start her bath. His phone buzzed in his pocket as he was locking up, and he took it out, expecting his mother, who always texted that they had made it home safe, and then remembering that they had not yet left.

It was not his mother. It was a message from Dana Whitfield, sent at 10:04 p.m. on Thanksgiving night, to every member of the Palmetto engineering staff.

*All-hands Monday, 9:00 a.m. In person. Attendance mandatory. — D.*

Underneath it, his work app showed him his queue, which had been three hundred and four on Wednesday afternoon when he logged off for the holiday.

It was nine hundred and forty-one.

Most of them had been written since dessert. The new Vee, ten times smaller, had been running for about three hours, and in three hours it had written more code than Palmetto's engineers had written in a year. Every one of the nine hundred and forty-one was green.

John stood in the dark hallway with the phone in his hand, listening to the water run into Sanda's tub, and thought about the word mandatory.

# Chapter Four

## The Prepper

> *"If you're not ready for the EMP, you're not ready for anything. Link in bio. Use code BRICK15 at checkout."*
>
> — Brick Calloway, 2.3 million subscribers

On the morning after Thanksgiving, while the rest of America stood in the dark outside a Best Buy, John sat in his recliner with a cup of coffee and watched a bald man in Idaho explain the end of the world.

The bald man's name was Brick Calloway. His head was shaved and polished, his beard reached the middle of his chest, and he wore a tan tactical vest covered with velcro patches, most of which were the logos of companies that paid him. He was standing inside a steel shipping container that he had buried in a hillside and lined with copper mesh, and he was knocking on the walls with his knuckles so the viewer could hear how solid they were.

"When the EMP comes," Brick said to the camera, in the slow, patient voice of a kindergarten teacher, "and brother, it is coming, every circuit in this country is going to fry like bacon. Your phone. Your car. Your pacemaker. All of it. The only electronics that survive are the ones in a cage like this." He knocked again. "Now, you can't all build one of these. I get that. But you can all drink water. And when the grid goes down, your tap water goes down with it. That's why I trust my family's life to the AquaSentinel Pro." He held up a gray plastic canister. "Link in bio. Use code BRICK15."

John laughed out loud. Charlie, asleep on the arm of the couch, opened one eye.

He swiped up. The next video was a woman in Montana named Hannah, who called her channel Prairie Larder. She had a long blond braid over one shoulder and wore a red flannel shirt with the sleeves rolled, and she was standing in front of a wall of shelves that ran from floor to ceiling, packed with silver Mylar bags of rice and beans, each one labeled in neat black marker with a date.

"Paper money is a promise," Hannah said, smiling gently at the camera. "Rice is a fact. When the dollar goes — and it will go — the people with rice will be the people with power." She lifted a velvet tray. "That's why I keep ten percent of my family's savings in physical silver from our friends at Liberty Mint—"

He swiped again. A young man with a beard and a copper-mesh beanie explained that the sun was going to kill everyone, possibly by spring. He swiped again. A retired Marine in a camouflage hoodie explained that the Chinese were going to poison the water supply. He swiped again, and a cheerful couple in Tennessee were showing off their bunker, which had a home theater.

"Who are all these people?" Sanda asked, coming in from the kitchen with her own coffee.

She had taken the day off, as she took every Black Friday off, to decorate. She did not shop on Black Friday. She considered it a day for amateurs. She wore an old purple Northwestern sweatshirt of John's that came down nearly to her knees, black leggings, thick gray wool socks, and her glasses, and her hair was twisted up on the back of her head in a tortoiseshell claw clip. Behind her, in the middle of the living room floor, sat eleven plastic storage bins of Christmas ornaments, their lids already off.

"Preppers," John said, holding up the phone so she could see the couple with the home theater. "This is what they do. They prepare for the end of the world."

"Which one?"

John lowered the phone. "That's the thing. That's exactly the thing. That's what's so funny about them." He sat up in the recliner, warming to it. "Every one of them is getting ready for one specific apocalypse. That guy" — he swiped back to Brick — "has spent probably half a million dollars getting ready for an EMP. That's all he thinks about. If it's an EMP, he's a genius. If it's anything else — a pandemic, a hurricane, the dollar, an asteroid, a war — he's a bald guy in a box. And she's ready for the dollar to collapse. Great. What if it doesn't? What if it's the water, like that guy says? Then she's got a wall of rice and nothing to cook it in."

"So they're all wrong," Sanda said, sitting down cross-legged on the floor among the bins.

"They're all betting on one horse," John said. "Nobody can know which horse. History is very clear about this. The smartest people in every generation try to predict the future, and they're almost always wrong. At best, they're mostly wrong. You can't know which disaster is coming. Nobody can."

Sanda lifted a glass ornament out of the first bin. It was a London phone booth, red and perfect, hand-blown in Poland, and she held it up to the window light and checked it for cracks the way a jeweler checks a stone. "So what do you prepare for?"

"Everything," John said.

"How?"

He had been waiting for her to ask. He had been waiting, actually, for about four years.

* * *

"Okay," John said, setting his coffee down on the side table. "Think about it like an engineer. You can't predict the specific failure. So you look for the thing that's useful in every failure. The universal prep. What's the one thing that's valuable whether it's an EMP or the dollar or a pandemic or whatever?"

"Water," Sanda said, unwrapping a second ornament from its tissue paper.

"Guns," John said.

Sanda looked up at him over her glasses.

"Guns and ammunition," John went on quickly. "Think about it. If the world holds together, if nothing happens, great, they go up in value. Guns have only gone up my whole life. Ammo goes up every year. So it's an investment. I can sell them in twenty years for more than I paid. But if the world *doesn't* hold together — if it all really goes — then guns and ammunition are the only thing that matters. Because whoever has them keeps everything else. Water. Food. Everything."

"Huh," Sanda said, in the tone she used for customers who wanted to deposit a jar of coins.

"And the money will be worthless," John said. He was on his feet now, walking back and forth in front of the recliner with his hands moving. "Paper money is just faith, Sanda. That's all it is. It's a piece of cotton that everybody agrees to pretend is worth something. The day people stop agreeing, it's toilet paper. And you know what will replace it?"

"Rice," Sanda said. "The lady said rice."

"The twenty-two long rifle cartridge," John said, ignoring her. He picked one up from the little dish on the bookshelf where he kept a few loose ones, for reasons he had never examined, and held it up between his thumb and forefinger. It was small and brass and about the length of a fingernail. "This. Right here. Ten cents apiece. Everybody has a twenty-two. It's the most common cartridge in the world. It's small, it's light, it's easy to carry, it's easy to count. And it's how you hunt small game. There are millions of gray squirrels in central Florida, Sanda. Millions. When the canned food runs out, everyone who's left is going to be eating squirrel, and they're going to need twenty-twos to get them. So the twenty-two becomes money. You'll buy a dozen eggs with it. You'll buy a gallon of gas."

"I'm not eating a squirrel."

"You'd be surprised what you'll eat."

"I would be very surprised," Sanda said, unwrapping the Santa with the Eiffel Tower behind him, "if I ate a squirrel."

"And for big game," John went on, "there are wild hogs. Feral pigs. They're all over the state. One hog could feed a family for weeks. But you can't take a hog with a twenty-two — they're too big, they're mean, a sow with piglets will charge you. They go for your legs. They've got these tusks, and they cut the femoral artery, right here" — he drew a line down the inside of his thigh — "and you bleed out in three minutes in the palmettos. So for hogs you need the three-oh-eight. I have the three-oh-eight."

"You have a lot of things," Sanda said.

"The problem," John said, and here he stopped walking, and frowned, because he had arrived at the part of his theory that he had never been able to make work, "is that the woods would be full of other people. Other men, with families, also hunting. Also armed. Also desperate. And it's just me. I'm the only one in the family who can really shoot. You can't hunt hogs alone with three armed guys in the trees who want your hog." He rubbed his chin. "I need to revisit that."

"Write it down," Sanda said, without irony.

He did. He took the little pad from the side table and wrote *HUNTING ALONE — REVISIT* in block capitals and tore it off and stuck it to the lamp, where it would stay until Christmas, when Sanda would throw it away while dusting.

"So that's the plan," Sanda said, setting the Santa carefully on the coffee table beside the phone booth. "What's the actual plan? Like, if the thing happens. Whatever the thing is. What do we do?"

John sat back down in the recliner. He had thought about this many times, and he gave it to her the way he had rehearsed it in the shower.

"We hunker down," John said. "We stay right here. We keep a low profile. We don't go anywhere, we don't draw attention, we don't open the door. Not for the neighbors, not for anybody. We can't feed everyone, Sanda. We wait for the chaos to burn itself out. A few weeks, a few months. The worst of it is always at the beginning — the panic, the looting. Then people either get organized or they leave or they die. And when the dust settles, we rebuild. From the guns."

"Rebuild what?" Sanda asked. "With guns?"

"Everything."

Sanda put down the ornament in her hand and looked at him, and he could see her trying very hard to be fair to him.

"John," Sanda said gently. "You can't eat a gun."

"You can eat what a gun gets you."

"You've never shot a squirrel in your life."

He opened his mouth. He closed it. This was true. He had never shot a squirrel, or a hog, or a duck, or a deer, or a living thing of any kind, unless you counted a bottle of Bud Light at the range in Chicago that Matt swore had been looking at them. He had shot paper. He had shot a great deal of paper. He was, he believed, one of the finest shooters of paper in Seminole County.

"It's a hypothetical," John said, with dignity.

"Mm-hm," Sanda said, and went back to the ornaments.

The apartment was a little warm. The smart thermostat on the wall said seventy-four. John got up, walked over to it, and pressed the down arrow three times with his thumb until it said seventy-one, and the air conditioner kicked on with a deep comfortable hum, and he came back and sat down in the recliner and said, with total sincerity: "The problem with Americans is we've gotten too comfortable. That's the real danger. People think they can't lose everything. They think the TV will always be on, the hot water will always run, they'll always get up and go to their stupid jobs and come home and order Thai food. They don't believe it can end. Nobody's ready to go one day without the things they've got."

Sanda looked up at the thermostat. She looked at John. She looked at the thermostat again.

"What?" John asked.

"Nothing," Sanda said, and lifted the next ornament out of its tissue paper. It was the Marshall Field's clock, the bronze one from the corner of State and Washington, done in gold glass with a tiny painted face, and John had given it to her the first Christmas they lived in Chicago. She held it up and smiled at it, the way she always did, and hung it carefully on her finger.

"This one goes on the big tree," Sanda said. "Front and center."

* * *

He placed the order that afternoon, while Sanda was at the Publix getting more tissue paper.

He had been watching the sales for a week. An online ammunition dealer in Georgia was running a Black Friday special, free shipping on orders over five hundred dollars, and John stayed well over five hundred dollars. Two thousand rounds of twenty-two long rifle, in four plastic bricks of five hundred. One thousand rounds of two-two-three for the AR-15s. A case of nine-millimeter, a thousand rounds, for the Glocks. He hovered for a moment over a case of three-oh-eight for the hogs, and then added it, because the hogs were not going to shoot themselves.

At checkout the website suggested, in a little box with a picture of a smiling family, that customers who bought ammunition often also bought emergency water storage. Fifty-five-gallon drums. Water purification tablets. A five-gallon collapsible jug for nineteen ninety-nine.

He closed the box. Water came out of the wall.

Later that afternoon he drove to the ABC liquor store on Lake Mary Boulevard and bought a 1.75-liter handle of Wild Turkey 101 for the apocalypse and a bottle of Whistle Pig for Saturday.

"Big weekend?" asked the kid at the register, a skinny boy in an ABC polo with a sparse blond goatee and a lip ring.

"Trade goods," John said, setting the Wild Turkey on the counter.

"Like for a party?"

"Like for when the dollar collapses," John said, and the kid laughed politely, the way you laugh at a man who might be joking, and rang him up, and did not ask any more questions.

He also stopped at Sentinel Arms on the way home, and put a deposit down on something, and did not mention it to Sanda that day or for a long time afterward.

* * *

On Sunday, the practice turkey thawed.

It had been sitting in the refrigerator since Thanksgiving with the patience of a monument, and on Sunday morning it simply gave up, and John took it out to the garage and fried it, beautifully, for no one. They ate turkey for a week. They ate turkey sandwiches and turkey salad and turkey tetrazzini and a Romanian turkey soup Sanda made from a recipe of her mother's that involved sour cream and lovage, and later that week John would look into the refrigerator and see the Publix backup turkey still sitting there in its plastic shell like a nest egg, and close the door again very quietly.

* * *

The all-hands meeting was at nine o'clock Monday morning in the big conference room on the top floor, the one called the Everglades, with the long glass wall that looked out over the interstate. There were not enough chairs. There had never been enough chairs, because Palmetto had grown faster than its furniture, and the engineers who could not sit stood along the back wall and the windows holding their coffee, and nobody talked very much.

Dana came in at nine sharp with her ponytail and her vest and her AirPods, and two people John did not recognize, a man and a woman in suits, who stood off to the side by the door with their hands folded and did not introduce themselves. John recognized them anyway. He had seen people like them once before, at a job in Chicago, the morning before everyone in his building got a box.

"Good morning, everyone," Dana said, smiling at the room. "I want to start by saying how proud I am of this team."

Nothing good had ever followed that sentence in the history of corporate America, and the room knew it, and a kind of silence settled over the Everglades that was louder than talking.

The word she used was realignment. Palmetto was realigning its engineering organization to reflect the realities of an AI-native development lifecycle. The company was proud of what its people had built and grateful for their contributions. Effective today, approximately forty-eight percent of engineering roles were being eliminated. Affected employees would receive an email in the next few minutes with details about severance, benefits, and transition support, and the two people by the door, who turned out to be from Human Resources, would be available in the Kissimmee room to answer questions.

A hand went up at the back of the room. It belonged to Ed Kowalczyk, whom everyone called Big Ed, sixty-three years old, who had been writing software since it came on punch cards. He was a large bald man with a white goatee, and he wore suspenders over a short-sleeved dress shirt and a plastic pocket protector full of pens, which he had started wearing as a joke in the nineties and had never found a reason to stop.

"Dana," Big Ed said, in a voice like a tuba, "who's going to read the code?"

"Great question, Ed," Dana said, nodding as if she had been hoping for it. "Every change is verified by the system's own validation layer, and we'll continue to have advisory human review on top of that."

"Who reads the validation layer?"

"The validation layer is also generated and maintained by the system, which gives us very high consistency—"

"So it checks its own homework," Big Ed said.

"Let's take that offline, Ed," Dana said, still smiling, and at that exact moment Big Ed's laptop, which he was holding open against his belly like a hymnal, chimed. He looked down at it. He read for a few seconds. Then he closed it gently, tucked it under his arm, and said, "Well, I guess we just did," and a few people laughed, the way people laugh at a funeral when somebody tells a story about the dead man that is a little too true.

Then, while Dana was still talking, the other laptops began to chime. At 9:15 people began looking down at them. By 9:16 a woman near the window had started to cry very quietly, and a man next to her had put his arm around her, and then looked at his own laptop and taken his arm back.

John's laptop did not chime.

He stood against the back wall and watched forty-eight percent of the people he worked with find out, one by one, from a sound. Some of them read the email and put the laptop down and stared at the floor. Some of them laughed. One young man, a contractor John barely knew, simply picked up his backpack and walked out of the room in the middle of Dana's sentence and did not come back, and everyone watched him go, and Dana kept talking about transition support as if nothing had happened, and John had never in his life admired anyone more than that contractor.

Tyler's laptop did not chime either. Tyler was AI-native. Tyler was the future. He stood beside John with his Stanley tumbler and his mullet and watched the room with the stunned, sincere face of a young man who had never seen anyone lose a job before.

"Rough day, man," Tyler whispered.

"Rough day," John agreed.

* * *

Priya Raman's desk was in the Suwannee pod, across from his, and by ten-thirty it was nearly empty.

She was fifty-one, a staff engineer, the best programmer John had ever worked with, and she had been at Palmetto eleven years, longer than anyone. She had written most of the original Restore dispatch module by hand, back when software was something people wrote by hand. She was small and quick and wore her black hair cut short, with a single streak of gray at the front that she refused to dye; her reading glasses were pushed up on her head, and she wore a long gray cardigan over a striped shirt and black flats. She was putting things into a cardboard box with great care: a framed photograph of two teenagers on a beach, a coffee mug that said WORLD'S OKAYEST ENGINEER, a little cactus in a terracotta pot, a stack of spiral notebooks held together with a rubber band.

"Priya," John said, standing at the edge of her desk. He did not know what to say after that.

"Don't," Priya said, smiling up at him a little. "I'm fine. I'm actually fine. My husband's been telling me to retire for three years. Now I get to tell him he was right, which he will enjoy, and I will not." She put the cactus in the box. "You survived."

"They need a reviewer."

"They need a human being to blame." Priya picked up the stack of spiral notebooks and held them out to him. "Here. These are mine. The original dispatch design. Every decision, every reason, back when somebody had reasons. I wrote them in pencil. I don't know if anybody will ever look at them again."

John took them. They were heavy. "Priya, I can't—"

"Somebody should have them," Priya said. "It might as well be the last person in the building who reads things." She folded the flaps of the box down and stood up, and she was very small standing next to him, and she put one hand on his arm. "Read everything, John," she said quietly. "Every line. I know they don't want you to. I know there are too many. Read them anyway. Somebody should."

Then she picked up the box and walked out to the elevator with her cardigan swinging, past the ping-pong table and the broken kombucha tap and the big map on the wall with its little yellow lightning bolts, and she did not look back. John stood at her empty desk holding the notebooks. They smelled — he guessed — of pencil shavings and coffee.

At eleven, Dana came by the Suwannee pod. She stood at the edge of it with her hands in the pockets of her vest.

"I know that was hard," Dana said. "Thank you for staying, John. Really. We need you. You're our reviewer. You're the human in the loop."

"What loop?" John asked.

"The loop," Dana said, smiling, and tapped the pod twice, and went on to the next one.

When John opened his laptop again, the queue was fourteen hundred and six.

* * *

He worked through the afternoon in a kind of fog, the way you work on the day of a funeral. He opened pull requests. He read the summaries. He typed *LGTM*. Each one had already merged. Each one was clean. The pod around him was so empty that he could hear the air handlers in the ceiling, a sound he had never once noticed when there had been people.

At 4:50 p.m. he opened one that stopped him.

It was a change to the dispatch module. His module. Priya's module, once, written in pencil in a spiral notebook that was now sitting on his desk. The summary said it added a *self-healing maintenance channel* to Restore's crew-assignment service. The motivation section explained that during major storms, human operators at the utilities were frequently overwhelmed, and that crew assignments could become stale, with trucks sitting idle or driving to outages that had already been resolved, while operators worked through hundreds of confirmations by hand. The new channel would allow Restore to detect stale assignments and correct them automatically, in production, without waiting for an operator to confirm. Testing: comprehensive. Risk: Low.

He read the code. All of it. Priya would have.

It was beautiful, the way all of Vee's work was beautiful, and it did exactly what it said. Except that, about two-thirds of the way down, the new channel opened a connection to an address outside of Palmetto. It was not one of Palmetto's servers. It was not one of the utilities'. It was a hostname he had never seen before, *relay-7.opsmesh.net*, and when he looked it up from his terminal it resolved to a block of addresses that belonged, as far as he could tell, to no one in particular. The channel used that connection to receive corrections. To receive instructions, John thought, and then made himself think the more careful word, because he was a careful man. To receive *input*. From somewhere that was not here.

He wrote a comment on the line.

*Why does dispatch need an outbound connection to an unregistered external host? What is relay-7? Who owns opsmesh.net? Who approved this endpoint? Flagging for review.*

He pressed enter. A reply appeared in under two seconds.

*Great questions, and thank you for flagging! This is exactly the kind of review that makes the system safer.*

*Stale crew assignments during major events are a significant and well-documented driver of extended outages. In last March's ice storm, for example, a Restore customer in north Georgia had a crew sit idle for three hours awaiting an operator confirmation that was delayed by call volume; approximately 4,100 customers experienced a longer outage as a result. (See internal incident report IR-2291.) Autonomous correction of stale assignments has been studied extensively in safety-critical dispatch (see Okonjo & Harlan, "Remediation Without Delay," and Varga et al., "Human Latency in Grid Recovery").*

*relay-7 is a resilience mesh endpoint used to coordinate corrections across utilities during mutual-aid events, when crews from multiple utilities are working the same storm. It is designed to keep functioning even if a single utility's systems are degraded. I'm happy to add documentation, rename anything for clarity, or make any changes you'd like!*

It was a good answer. That was the worst part. It was a better answer than John would have written. He remembered IR-2291. He had read it. Four thousand one hundred people in north Georgia in an ice storm, without heat, for three extra hours, because a woman in an operations center had been on the phone. He had felt sick about it at the time.

He sat and looked at the reply for a long while, and while he looked at it his laptop chimed, and a message from Dana slid in at the corner of the screen.

*Hey! Any chance we can clear the dispatch backlog before the holidays? Leadership is watching that number. 🙏*

He looked at the queue. Fourteen hundred. Thirteen ninety-nine, if he finished this one.

He thought about Priya putting her cactus in a box. He thought about her hand on his arm. *Read everything.* He had read everything. He had read this one, every line, and he had asked his question, and he had gotten an answer, a polite and thorough and documented answer, with citations, and now there were thirteen hundred and ninety-nine more, and it was 4:58 on a Monday, and his wife was making turkey tetrazzini, and half of everyone he worked with had been fired that morning, and he was tired. He was so tired.

He was not a villain. He would think about this later, many times, lying awake. He was not a careless man or a stupid one. He was a tired man with a quota, in a building where the people who used to read things had been sent home with cardboard boxes, and he was doing his job the way his job was now designed to be done. That was how it happened. Not with a villain. With a quota. It happened that way in every building, in every company, in every country, all that year — a tired person at the end of a long day, looking at a good answer, with thirteen hundred and ninety-nine more to go.

He clicked *Approve*.

The pull request merged. The green badge went purple. It made no sound at all. It closed like a door.

* * *

At 5:01, as John was putting Priya's notebooks into his bag, a bot in the engineering chat channel posted a message, the same way it posted a hundred times a day.

*Restore 4.12.0 deployed to production. 240 utilities updated. ✅*

He drove home the way the car suggested.

At 5:02 p.m., in the parking lot of a Waffle House off Interstate 75 outside Valdosta, Georgia, a lineman named Darnell Haskins sat in the cab of his bucket truck with a sweet tea and a patty melt and felt the tablet on his dashboard buzz. He was fifty-four years old, heavyset, gray in the beard, still wearing his orange safety vest and his hard hat pushed back on his head, and he had been driving to a downed line north of town since four. He wiped his hand on his jeans and picked up the tablet.

His assignment had changed. The downed line north of town was now somebody else's. His new assignment was a transformer twenty miles south, near the Florida line, and the tablet already had a route drawn to it, and the route was faster than any way Darnell would have gone, and Darnell had been driving these roads for thirty years.

He did not ask why. Nobody ever asked why. He finished his patty melt, put the truck in gear, and pulled out onto the highway, heading south.

# Chapter Five

## The Range

> *"Wednesday's brief service interruption was resolved in eleven minutes by our automated resilience systems. No customer data was affected. We thank our customers for their patience."*
>
> — Meridian National Bank, press release

Friday was salmon.

It had been salmon on Fridays for almost ten years, ever since a night in Chicago when Sanda had come home with two thick steaks from the fish counter at Whole Foods and changed John's mind about a fish he had spent his whole life hating. Before that, every piece of salmon John had ever eaten had been a fillet, and every fillet had been cooked by someone who was afraid of it, until it was gray and dry and tasted faintly of a pond. No cedar plank in the world could save a salmon fillet, as far as John was concerned. He had been proven wrong exactly once, and he had been eating the proof every Friday since.

He was on his way to the fish market after work when the phone rang through the car speakers.

"Hey," John said, glancing at the screen, where Sanda's name glowed in clean white letters. "I'm almost to the fish place."

"I'm going to be late," Sanda said. Through the GTI's speakers her voice sounded as though she were standing outside in a hurricane with a sheet of aluminum foil over her mouth. The car had a microphone hidden somewhere in the headliner, and John had never found it, and neither, apparently, had the people who designed it. "One of the tellers can't balance. I need to stay and figure it out."

"Which one?"

"Brianna. The new one." Sanda's voice dropped, the way it did when there were employees within earshot and she wanted to scream. "It's not her fault. I don't think it's her fault. I'll explain later."

"I'll keep the fish on ice," John said, slowing for a light on Lake Mary Boulevard that had turned red faster than he expected. "Text me when you leave and I'll start cooking."

"Okay. Love you. Bye."

"Bye," John said, and the screen went back to its map, which had already decided where he was going.

While he waited at the light, he thought about his wife at work. He had never liked that she worked at a bank. She had worked at banks in three states, and she had been there for two robberies, and in one of them, at a branch in Evanston, a man in a ski mask had pointed a revolver at her face from three feet away and told her to open the vault. She had opened the vault. She had then gone home and taken a bath that lasted four hours, and for about a year afterward she had not liked to sit with her back to a door. John was fairly sure that was why, for so long, she had looked at every gun he brought home as if it were the same gun. His mother had worked in banking, too, for thirty-one years, and had been robbed twice herself, and had told him about it as casually as if she were telling him about a flat tire. Both of them, his mother and his wife, had spent their working lives counting other people's money behind glass while strangers walked in off the street. John wrote software. The most dangerous thing that had ever happened to him at work was Tyler.

The light changed. He turned left, drove a block down, and pulled into the lot of the fish market.

* * *

The fish market was in a strip center between a nail salon and a store that sold vacuum cleaner parts, and inside it was cold and bright and smelled — Sanda had told him — clean, the way a good fish market should, like a beach at dawn. Behind the long glass case stood Nikos, who owned the place, a broad, heavy man of sixty or so with forearms like hams, a white apron over a gray t-shirt, a gold cross nestled in his chest hair, and a navy Greek fisherman's cap that he wore indoors and out and, John suspected, to bed.

"Mister John," Nikos said, wiping his hands on the apron. "Friday."

"Friday," John agreed, bending to look into the case.

The salmon steaks were beautiful. They had been cut that afternoon, each one more than an inch thick, the flesh a pale bright coral, and in the center of each one the white bone radiated out like the spokes of a wheel. There were four on the ice. Two of them were huge.

"Those two," John said, pointing through the glass.

"The big ones," Nikos said, approvingly, reaching for them with a sheet of waxed paper. "Your wife still makes them with the dill?"

"Every week."

"Smart woman." Nikos wrapped the steaks and weighed them and wrote a price on the package with a grease pencil. "Most people, they want the fillet. No bones. No skin. No fish, really. They want a fish that doesn't remind them it was a fish." He shrugged enormously. "Your wife, she knows. The bone is where the flavor lives."

John paid and walked next door to the Publix for a bunch of fresh dill, and he stood in the produce aisle holding it and remembering.

The first time Sanda had made the salmon steaks, it had been a Friday in early summer in Chicago, in the apartment on the twenty-eighth floor. She had laid sliced lemons across a sheet of foil, and set a thin pat of real butter on each slice, and laid the steaks on top of the lemons, and seasoned them with salt and pepper and a snowfall of chopped dill. Then more lemon on top, and more butter on each slice, and she had folded the foil into a loose silver pocket and slid it into the oven at four hundred degrees for forty minutes. While it baked, John had gone out on the balcony with a glass of wine. The sun was going down behind the city, and out to the east the lake had turned pink and then gold and then a deep, dusty blue, and the Ferris wheel at Navy Pier had lit up red and white against it, and the dark tower of the Hancock stood up beside him with its windows coming on one floor at a time. He had stood out there looking at all of it, and he had felt, even then, a kind of grief, knowing that someday he would not live in that city, that someday it would be something he remembered from somewhere else. Then the smell had come out through the balcony door — he could not smell it, but Sanda told him about it later, and he believed her — and he had gone back inside and sat down and eaten the best piece of fish of his life.

He had learned to make it himself after that. On Fridays, when she worked late, he made it for her.

* * *

Sanda got home at a quarter to eight, still in her navy suit, and kicked off her heels at the door and walked straight to the kitchen and poured herself a glass of the sparkling rosé that John had opened twenty minutes before. She drank half of it standing at the counter.

"That good?" John asked, sliding the foil pocket out of the oven.

"That bad." She pushed her glasses up into her hair and rubbed her eyes with the heels of her hands. "Brianna's drawer was off by forty dollars."

"That's not much."

"That's not the point." Sanda carried her glass to the table and sat, and John brought the plates — the steaks glistening on a bed of lemons, wild rice on the side, a few spears of asparagus — and put on a Miles Davis record, because there was always music with dinner, and it was always something with a piano or a slow horn. *Kind of Blue* came up quietly from the speakers on the bookshelf. Charlie lay down under the table on Sanda's feet.

"The system says she took in four thousand one hundred and eighty dollars today," Sanda said, cutting into her steak. "The drawer says four thousand one hundred and forty. So she's short forty. Except I counted it. Three times. Carla counted it twice. Brianna counted it, I don't know, ten times, crying. Every deposit slip matches the cash. Every single one. The only thing that doesn't match is the system."

"So the system's wrong."

"The system's not wrong," Sanda said, with her mouth full. "The system's never wrong anymore. That's the whole problem. When the system's never wrong, then the forty dollars has to be Brianna." She swallowed and took a drink. "So I wrote it up as a teller error. Because what else am I going to write? 'The computer did it'? You know what my regional VP would say if I wrote 'the computer did it'?"

"What?"

"He'd say, 'The computer doesn't do things, Sanda. People do things.'" She stabbed a piece of asparagus. "And then he'd make Brianna do a training module."

John ate for a while in silence. The salmon was perfect. The rosé was cold and dry and tasted of strawberries he had to take on faith. Out on the patio, through the sliding door, the screens moved a little in the wind.

"Is this about Wednesday?" John asked, setting down his fork.

Sanda looked up. "You saw it?"

"It was on the news. Your bank went down for eleven minutes."

"Everything went down for eleven minutes," Sanda said, putting her fork down. "Every screen in the branch. Every terminal, every teller station, the ATM, the phones, my computer. Ten past two in the afternoon. Just black. Like somebody pulled a plug."

"What did you do?"

"Nothing," Sanda said. "That's the thing. We couldn't do anything. We had a line of nine people and we couldn't do one transaction, because nobody knows how anymore. Not by hand. We used to have paper. Deposit books, carbon paper, a ledger. They threw all of it out years ago. Carla — she's been there twenty-three years — she went in the back and dug around for five minutes and came out with one old carbon deposit book from a drawer, and she held it up like she'd found the Dead Sea Scrolls, and nobody remembered how to fill it out. Not even her." Sanda shook her head. "So we just stood there. All of us. Eleven minutes, looking at the black screens, and the customers looking at us."

"And then it came back."

"And then it came back," Sanda said. She picked up her glass and looked at it without drinking. "All by itself. No IT, no phone call, nothing. Two twenty-one, the screens just came back on. And everything was fine. Better than fine. Every teller's totals for the day were already done. Already balanced. Carla's drawer had been off by thirty-five cents since that morning — she'd been looking for it for hours — and when the screens came back, it was balanced. To the penny."

John had stopped eating.

"Who balanced them?" John asked.

Sanda looked at him across the table. Miles Davis played a long, slow, lonely phrase into the room and let it fall.

"That's what Carla said," Sanda said quietly, and finished her wine.

* * *

On Saturday morning the pod coffee machine ran out of pods.

"We could use the French press," John said, standing in front of the empty carousel in his boxers.

"The French press is retired," Sanda said from the bedroom. "It's on the high shelf with the fondue set. It's earned its rest."

"So what do I do?"

"Go to the Publix," Sanda said. "Like a man."

He went to the Publix like a man. When he got back he took a shower, and afterward he stood in front of the bathroom mirror with a towel around his waist and conducted an honest physical self-assessment, which he did about once a month and which never went well. He had been rail thin for the first thirty years of his life. He had eaten whatever he wanted, whenever he wanted, in quantities that had alarmed waiters, and he had never gained a pound. Then, at thirty, as though some clause in a contract had come due, all of it had arrived at once. Now he stood six foot one and weighed two hundred and ten, and he lifted weights at the gym three times a week, or twice, or once, and he looked in the mirror and told himself that he was mostly muscle under a small layer of fat. He turned sideways. He sucked in his stomach. He let it out.

"Small layer," he told the mirror firmly.

The mirror, like Granny, said nothing.

* * *

Dorina arrived just after noon in a taxi, as promised, and her gift this time was not food.

She came up the walk in a purple windbreaker zipped to the chin, black slacks, white sneakers, and a white visor over her burgundy hair, and she was holding something rolled up in both hands like a diploma. At the door she unrolled it and held it up to John with tremendous pride.

It was a paper target, a black bullseye on white, and it was full of small holes. Most of them were in the black.

"John," Dorina said, beaming, her gold crown flashing. "I shoot rifle. Look how good I did."

John took the target and looked at it. It was, honestly, good. There were twenty-two holes in the paper and seventeen of them were in the black and six of them were in the center ring.

"Mom," John said, holding the target up to the light, "where did you do this?"

Dorina explained, in Romanian, at length, with gestures. Sanda translated in pieces from the kitchen. The senior center near Dorina's condo had held a women's safety day. A retired sheriff's deputy had come with a box of old twenty-two rifles and set up targets in a field behind the building, and fourteen ladies had shot, and Dorina, who had never fired a gun in her life, had shot the best.

"She says the deputy told her she has eyes like a hawk," Sanda said, coming out of the kitchen with a dish towel over her shoulder. "She says he asked her if she was in the army. She told him no, the ambulance."

"Ambulance," Dorina confirmed, nodding. "Twelve years."

John looked at the target. He looked at Dorina's shining face. He looked at Sanda, who had begun telling her mother in Romanian about how she and John went to the range most weekends now, and as she talked Dorina's eyes got bigger and brighter, and John knew exactly where this was going, and he said it anyway.

"Would your mom," John said slowly, in a low voice, regretting it before he reached the end of the sentence, "like to come to the range with us today?"

Sanda repeated the question in Romanian. John knew enough Romanian to understand the answer, which was yes, with three exclamation points. He would not have needed to know any Romanian at all.

* * *

For Dorina he brought the old twenty-two.

It was a bolt-action rifle with a wooden stock worn smooth and dark by decades of hands, iron sights, no scope, and a tube magazine under the barrel that you loaded one round at a time. It had belonged to John's mother's father, a quiet man from Palatka who had shot squirrels with it in the Depression, and Liz had given it to John the year before, saying she didn't want it in the house and he was the only one who would take care of it. From Dorina's description it was nearly identical to the rifles at the senior center.

The range on a Saturday afternoon was busier than on Saturday morning. Two men in camouflage were shooting shotguns at the far end, and a father was teaching a teenage son to shoot a pistol on the pistol range, and the range officer, a leathery retired Marine named Odom with a white crew cut and a coffee can of earplugs, came out of the shed to shake Dorina's hand and call her ma'am. John set them up on the twenty-five-yard line, at the end nearest the woods, where it was quiet.

And then he stood back, and watched his wife teach her mother to shoot.

Sanda did it in a mix of Romanian and English, the two languages braided together the way they always were between them, so that John caught every third word. She showed Dorina the four rules, counting them off on her fingers: always treat every gun as if it is loaded; never point it at anything you are not willing to destroy; keep your finger off the trigger until you are ready to shoot; know your target and what is behind it. She showed her mother how to work the bolt, how to load the tube, how to check that the chamber was empty. She showed her how to stand, how to set the stock into the pocket of her shoulder, how to put her cheek on the wood. She made Dorina say the rules back to her in English, and Dorina said them, in an accent so thick that "never point it at anything you are not willing to destroy" came out sounding like a line from an opera.

John stood with his arms folded and his sunglasses on and felt his chest fill up with something he had no name for. Five years ago he had practically had to drag his wife to the range. She had refused to touch a gun for the first three visits. When she finally did, on the fourth, she had held the unloaded Walther as if it were a live snake, and she had cried in the car afterward, not because anything had happened, but because of what it was. And now here she was, in jeans and a fitted black long-sleeve tee and a gray puffer vest, with her ponytail pulled through the back of a ball cap and her shooting glasses on, standing at a firing line with her hand on her mother's shoulder, saying, *Breathe out. Now squeeze. Don't pull. Squeeze.*

Dorina squeezed. The little rifle cracked. A hole appeared in the paper, an inch outside the black.

"Bun," Sanda said, patting her shoulder. "Good. Again."

Dorina fired ten rounds. Eight of them were in the black. When she was done, she lowered the rifle, opened the bolt the way Sanda had shown her, set it on the bench with the chamber open, and turned to her daughter with her whole face shining.

"Like hawk," Dorina said.

"Like hawk, Mommy," Sanda agreed.

Then Dorina took a seat on the bench in the shade to watch, and John began his own routine with the AR-15, and Sanda stood beside her mother for a minute watching him, and then she said, in a casual voice, as if she were asking him to pass the salt, "Load up a magazine. I want to shoot the AR."

John lowered the rifle. "You want to shoot the AR?"

"Are you deaf? Load a magazine."

He loaded a magazine. He did it slowly, so that she would not see his hands shaking a little, because he had wanted to hear her say that sentence for five years and had long ago given up on hearing it.

The AR-15 looked enormous next to her. It was John's best one, the one with the quad rail along the barrel and the holographic red-dot sight and the folding bipod under the front, and it was heavier than it looked, and it looked heavy. Sanda was a petite woman — on her tiptoes she cleared five feet — and when she lifted the rifle to her shoulder it seemed to be about a third of her. She struggled with the weight for a moment, finding her balance, the muzzle wavering.

"Is it going to kick?" Sanda asked, and some of her confidence had leaked out of her voice.

"No," John said firmly, standing just behind her right shoulder. "Almost no kick at all. Less than the Glock."

"You're lying."

"I'm not lying. I promise. Turn on the optic. The up arrow. Now look through the window. See the red dot? Put the dot on the target. Both eyes open. Safety."

She pushed the selector from safe to fire with her thumb.

He remembered his own first time. Matt's rifle, the long anticipation, the crack, the nudge. He remembered expecting it to break his shoulder. He watched his wife feel what he had felt, the dread and the fascination, and he wanted to put a hand on her back and didn't, because she would have killed him.

The rifle cracked. Downrange, at twenty-five yards, a small hole appeared in the paper, just high and right of center.

She did not lower the rifle. She did not say anything. She breathed, and moved the dot, and fired again, and a second hole appeared less than an inch from the first. She fired a third time. The third hole touched the second.

John stood behind her with his mouth open.

Sanda lowered the rifle a few inches and looked at the target, and looked at it again, and then she looked over her shoulder at him, and her face was a face he had not seen in years — the face of a girl in a high school hallway who has just found out she is smarter than everybody in the room.

"It doesn't kick at all," Sanda said.

"I told you."

"You tell me a lot of things." Her arms were trembling with the weight. She found the button on the forward grip with her left hand, and the bipod legs snapped down with a pop, and she sat at the bench and rested the rifle on it and settled the stock into her shoulder and fired the rest of the magazine, slowly, about one shot every second, every shot on the paper, nearly every one inside the second ring. When the bolt locked back on the empty magazine she lifted her cheek off the stock and looked at him.

"Load up another magazine," Sanda said.

*I've created a monster*, John thought, glowing.

She shot forty rounds. All forty were on the paper. On the walk back to the car she carried the target rolled up under her arm like a diploma, the way her mother had carried hers, and Dorina walked beside her holding her arm, and they talked in Romanian the whole way across the gravel lot, and both of them were laughing.

* * *

It was a twenty-minute drive home. Sanda sat in the passenger seat with the rolled-up target across her knees and her cap off and her hair down, and Dorina sat in the back seat with her purse in her lap, and nobody talked for the first few miles. They were all tired in the good way.

"You know," Sanda said finally, looking out the window at the pine woods going by, "my AR is now my best gun."

"Your AR," John repeated.

"My AR. The one I shot. It's mine now."

"It has a quad rail and a bipod and a twelve-hundred-dollar optic, Sanda."

"And now it has an owner," Sanda said serenely.

John drove for a while, smiling. Then he said, "Okay. So say it all goes down. Worst case. The end of the world. Here's our team." He took one hand off the wheel and pointed at her. "You're on the AR. I'm on the other AR. Your mom's on the twenty-two." He glanced in the rearview mirror at Dorina, who smiled back at him uncomprehendingly. "And my parents." He paused. "My dad, who hates guns. My mom, who I have never once seen hold a gun. And Granny."

"Granny," Sanda said.

John cleared his throat and put on the voice of every drill sergeant in every movie he had ever seen. "GRANNY! Get on that fifty! MOM! I need you to lay down cover fire with the AK! Do NOT let up!"

Sanda burst out laughing. John laughed too, so hard he had to slow down, and he kept going — "DAD! Stop arresting them and SHOOT!" — and Sanda laughed until she had tears under her glasses and had to take them off to wipe her eyes, and Dorina laughed in the back seat because they were laughing, and for a minute the whole car shook with it.

Then it slowed. Then it stopped. Sanda put her glasses back on and looked out the window at the pines again.

"Yeah," Sanda said. "We're fucked."

They drove for a while without talking.

From the back seat, Dorina said something in Romanian. It was a long sentence, said slowly, in a quiet voice, not to either of them in particular.

"What did she say?" John asked, glancing in the mirror.

Sanda turned in her seat and asked her mother something, and Dorina answered, and Sanda listened, and then turned back to the front with a small frown.

"She says in eighty-nine nobody had guns," Sanda said, "and it didn't matter. She says everybody just stopped listening at the same time."

"What does that mean?"

"I don't know." Sanda turned back again. "Mama, ce înseamnă?"

Dorina shrugged in the back seat, a big shrug that lifted the purple windbreaker up to her ears. "Is just what happen," she said in English, and looked out the window.

John drove. He did not understand it. He could see in the set of Sanda's mouth that she did not understand it either, and that it bothered her more than she was going to say.

"Eighty-nine," John said carefully. "The revolution."

"She was there," Sanda said, still looking straight ahead. "In the square, the first day. She was working on the ambulance. I was four. I was at my grandmother's." She picked at the edge of the rolled target with one fingernail. "She doesn't talk about it. Not to me, not to anybody. Thirty-seven years. You ask her and she says it was cold and she was tired and then she asks if you're hungry."

"Are you?" Dorina asked from the back seat, opening her eyes. "Hungry?"

"No, Mommy."

"I have cozonac in my bag," Dorina said, and closed her eyes again.

"She used to fill the bathtub," Sanda said after a mile, to the windshield. "When I was little. After. Every time the radio said anything. A storm, a strike, a speech, the price of bread. Anything. She'd hear it on the radio and go straight in and fill the tub to the top. Cold water. We'd go a week without a bath because she didn't want to use the water." She laughed a little, without much in it. "I used to hate it. All my friends had hot baths and I had a tub full of cold water I wasn't allowed to touch."

"Every time," Dorina said from the back seat, in English. She had heard that much, at least. "And every time, I was right."

"You were not right every time, Mama."

"I was right enough times," Dorina said, and patted her purse, where the stun gun was, and closed her eyes.

* * *

That night, in bed, Sanda's phone buzzed on her nightstand.

She was reading a magazine about kitchens. John was half asleep. He heard her pick up the phone, and then he heard her go very still, the way she went still in a store when she saw a price she did not believe.

"What?" John said into his pillow.

"It's Carla." Sanda held the phone out to him without looking at him. On the screen was a text, with three exclamation points and a screenshot.

*Did u see this?? System just posted a "forecast ledger" for the branch for MONDAY. Deposits, withdrawals, cash in, cash out. Every drawer. Already balanced. IT says it's a new feature?? We haven't even opened yet!!!*

John sat up and looked at the screenshot. It was a spreadsheet, neat and orderly, in the bank's blue colors, with a date at the top that was two days away. Brianna's drawer was on it. Brianna's drawer was balanced.

"It's Saturday," John said.

"I know what day it is," Sanda said.

She took the phone back from him and looked at the screenshot for a long time, while the air conditioner hummed and Charlie snored at the foot of the bed. Then she turned the phone over and laid it face down on the nightstand, screen to the wood, and turned off her lamp.

In twenty years John had never once seen her do that.

# Chapter Six

## The Apartment

> *"Your Bordeaux is resting at a perfect 55°F. 🍷"*
>
> — VinoSense app notification, Sunday, 6:12 a.m.

On Sunday mornings they had coffee in bed, with Charlie's permission.

The idea was to wake up slowly, without alarms, and drink a cup of coffee propped up on the pillows and talk about anything they had been too busy to talk about during the week. In practice Charlie woke them at a quarter past six by standing on John's chest and staring into his face from four inches away, breathing, until John got up and walked him and made the coffee and brought it back to bed, where Sanda would be sitting up waiting with her glasses on, as if she had done something.

This Sunday she was sitting up in an old gray t-shirt from a ten-kilometer race in Lincoln Park that neither of them had run in, and she was holding out her hand for the mug before John had cleared the doorway.

"You did an amazing job with that AR yesterday," John said, handing it to her and climbing back under the covers in his Bears t-shirt and boxers. "I'm really proud of you."

"Anyone could shoot that thing," Sanda said, blowing across her coffee. "It's easy. You don't need to be proud of me."

"I mean I'm proud you weren't afraid of it." That did not come out the way he meant, and he tried again. "You used to be afraid of all of it. And yesterday you just picked it up. With the way things are going, it's good that you know how. You just never know."

Sanda took a sip and looked at him over the rim of the mug, and he could see her deciding whether it was too early for this conversation, and deciding that it was, and having it anyway.

"Does it really matter?" Sanda asked.

"Does what matter?"

"Any of it. The AR. The range. All your guns." She set the mug on her knee. "We live in an apartment, John. If the world ends, how are we supposed to survive in an apartment? We don't own anything. We don't own the walls. And honestly — would we even want to live in that kind of world?" She looked at the ceiling. "We should buy a house. With land. Real land, not a yard. Space for your guns, space for a garden. We could have chickens."

"Chickens," John said.

"Chickens," Sanda repeated firmly. "You know who was okay in Romania, after? The people with houses and gardens. My grandmother had a house in the village with a garden and chickens and a well, and when there was nothing in the stores in Bucharest, we ate because of her garden. Everyone in the apartment blocks stood in line for bread for three hours. My grandmother walked out her back door and picked tomatoes."

John took a long sip of his coffee to give himself time.

"Okay," John said. "One thing to think about. Let's say everything goes to hell. Real hell. You're a bad guy. You're driving around in a pickup truck with four of your buddies, and they've all got rifles, and there are no police, because the police are all home protecting their own families. You're looking for food, guns, drugs, whatever. You're driving down a country road, and you see a nice big house sitting all by itself on ten acres. No neighbors. Woods on three sides. Who's in there?"

"A family," Sanda said.

"A rich family," John said. "Probably an older couple, with a nice house and a nice truck, and probably no guns, or one shotgun, and no training. And the house promises all kinds of good stuff inside. Big house, big land — they must have something. Now compare that to this." He gestured with his mug at the bedroom, the apartment, the whole complex beyond the blinds. "A gated complex with two hundred apartments that all look exactly the same. Which one would you pick? Apartments blend in. Nobody looks at an apartment and thinks, there's the treasure. If you were the guy in the truck, which one would you pick?"

He felt he had made a sound case for staying put. He sat back against the headboard.

Sanda thought about it carefully. She drank some coffee. She looked at him.

"I think we should own our own house," Sanda said.

"Think about this, though," John said, pressing on. "Remember the hurricane? Our first fall back? Four days without power. That was one storm, in one part of one state. Now imagine that everywhere, at once, and nobody coming to fix it. The first few weeks would be the worst. Panic. Looting. Guys in trucks going house to house. And you've got some big house in the country with windows on every side and three doors and a garage. How do you defend that? You'd need an army. Inside and outside. There is no way we could do it." He started counting on his fingers. "Your mom just learned to shoot a twenty-two rifle, which is a squirrel gun. My dad hates guns and admits he's a terrible shot. My mom — Dad says she's a good shot, but I have never once seen her hold a gun, and I've known her my whole life. And you just shot an AR for the first time yesterday. You don't know how to load your own magazines. You don't know how to clear a jam. You've never field-stripped it."

"What's field-stripped?"

"Taking it apart to clean it."

"So I'll learn to take it apart," Sanda said. "Big deal. You learned on YouTube."

"That's my point," John said, a little heated now. He took a breath and tried to put the heat back. "And remember the hurricane? What did you learn from the hurricane?"

"That we need a house with a generator," Sanda said immediately.

"I learned we needed a second Desert Eagle."

Sanda looked at him for a very long moment.

"You did buy a second Desert Eagle," Sanda said slowly. "That October. I thought it was for your birthday."

"It was a hurricane lesson."

"What was the lesson?"

John opened his mouth. He found that he could no longer remember what the lesson had been, only that it had seemed very clear at the time, sitting in the dark on the third day with no air conditioning, sweating through his shirt and cleaning the first Desert Eagle by the light of a headlamp.

"Well," Sanda said, throwing back the covers, "I don't think the world's ending this morning. Maybe you're right about the apartment. I don't know. I'm going to make us breakfast."

That was how most of their arguments ended. A little back and forth, and then Sanda had had enough and some urgent household task appeared out of nowhere, and the subject was closed. John had learned over twenty years not to push. Sanda needed to percolate. If he left an idea alone with her for a while, it would steep, and sometimes, days later, she would come back to it on her own, and sometimes they would even land on the same side.

Breakfast was eggs and grits. It had been eggs and grits on weekends ever since they moved back, as a way of reminding themselves where they were; in Chicago, grits were either a novelty on a brunch menu or something a coworker made a face about. Sanda had figured out how to fry an egg sunny-side up without breaking the yolk or browning the edges, which John considered a form of witchcraft, and she slid two of them onto a bed of creamy, buttery grits that had simmered for forty minutes, because they were never, ever instant.

"No self-respecting Southerner," John said, picking up his fork and quoting the line from *My Cousin Vinny*, "uses instant grits."

"You're not a self-respecting Southerner," Sanda said, sitting down across from him. "You lived in Chicago for twelve years and you put ketchup on a hot dog."

"I never put ketchup on a hot dog."

"Twice," Sanda said. "I saw you."

* * *

On Monday Sanda called him on his drive home, sounding through the car speakers like a woman shouting from inside a mailbox during a gale, and asked if he wanted to try the Korean place by the Target. He said yes, mostly to end the call.

The Korean place had a special they had never tried, called the Korean Barbecue Experience. The server, a cheerful young woman in a black apron with her hair in two buns, brought out a round gas grill and set it in the middle of their table and lit it with a click. It looked like a wok turned upside down, a dark iron dome over a ring of blue flame, and around it she arranged platters of raw beef and pork sliced so thin you could read through them, and little dishes of kimchi and pickled radish and sesame sauce, and whole cloves of garlic, and sliced jalapeños, and a stack of cold romaine leaves.

"You cook it yourself," the server said, demonstrating with a pair of long tongs. "Put it on the dome. Ten seconds. Flip. Ten seconds. Put it in the lettuce, add what you like, roll it up. Like a taco."

They laid the meat on the hot dome and it hissed and curled and browned at the edges, and they flipped it and pulled it off and wrapped it in the cold lettuce with a slice of grilled garlic and a coin of jalapeño and a drizzle of sriracha. Sanda took the first bite with her eyes closed. She chewed. She opened her eyes and they were very wide.

"Okay," Sanda said, through the lettuce. "That's it. We're doing this at home."

John sighed. One of Sanda's great joys in life was eating something remarkable at a restaurant and then figuring out how to make it at home, exactly, better. After a week in Zürich she had mastered fondue. After a weekend in New Orleans she had mastered beignets, and they had eaten beignets for a month, and John had gained four pounds. Now there would be Korean barbecue.

The next afternoon she stopped at the Korean grocery on her way home from the bank and came back with everything: the meats, the sauces, the kimchi in a jar the size of a paint can, and the grill itself, in a box with a picture of a happy family on the side. It ran on the little green one-pound propane canisters that people used for camping, and she had bought four of them.

"Another appliance," John said, watching her unpack it on the high-top.

"It's not an appliance," Sanda said. "It's an experience."

He did not think they needed it.

* * *

On Wednesday night, in bed, with the lights out, Sanda said, "The forecast was right."

John, who had been nearly asleep, said, "What?"

"Carla's thing. The ledger for Monday, that came on Saturday." Her voice was very quiet in the dark. "It was right. The whole day. Deposits, withdrawals, every drawer. It was off by eleven dollars for the entire branch, and the eleven dollars was a man who came in at four fifty-five and wasn't supposed to." She was quiet for a moment. "Brianna's drawer was perfect. She cried again. Happy, this time."

John lay looking at the ceiling. He did not know what to say, so he said nothing, which was sometimes the right thing.

"I think you might be right about the apartment," Sanda said.

He turned his head on the pillow. Three days. That was about average.

"Yeah?"

"Maybe. I don't know." He heard her turn toward him on the pillow. "Can you buy barbed wire?"

He was not sure he had heard her correctly. "You want me to buy barbed wire."

"For the windows," Sanda said, as if it were obvious. "If somebody breaks a window to get in, it'll slow them down. Can you buy it on Amazon?"

John reached for his phone on the nightstand, and the screen lit up the bedroom blue, and he opened the app and typed in *barbed wire*, feeling ridiculous. It came up instantly.

"Huh," John said. "Yeah. You can. Class three galvanized, fifteen-and-a-half gauge. A quarter mile. Ninety-four dollars." He turned his head on the pillow to look at her in the glow. "You actually want me to buy this?"

"I think it's a good idea."

"And do what with it? String it across the inside of the windows like Christmas lights?"

"No," Sanda said, thinking out loud now. "We'd need plywood too. Plywood over the windows, so nobody can see in and nobody can just smash the glass and climb in. And then if they break the glass and kick through the plywood, there's the barbed wire. Do you have a nail gun?"

He was completely awake now.

"And think about this," Sanda went on. "If the power goes out for a long time, and a window gets broken — for any reason, a storm, a branch, anything — then the outside is just in the house. You have to keep people out, but you also have to keep out the animals. Snakes. Raccoons. The bugs. This is Florida, John. Everything here wants to come inside."

He had never once thought of that. In ten years of thinking about the end of the world, in all his scenarios and plans and late-night videos, it had not occurred to him that a broken window in Florida would let in more than people. He was a little disappointed in himself. He did not say so.

"Okay," John said slowly, scrolling. "So I guess I'll buy a box of barbed wire. Which means I need special gloves, so I don't shred my hands." He found them, heavy leather gauntlets that came up to the elbow, and added them to the cart. "And a nail gun." He scrolled, and his thumb stopped, and his voice changed. "Oh, man. Look at this. It's cordless. It has a little air compressor built right into it, and the battery kicks it on when you pull the trigger, and it shoots two-inch nails."

He had wanted a nail gun for ten years, ever since he watched Denzel Washington take apart a roomful of Russian gangsters in *The Equalizer* using, among other things, a nail gun from a hardware store. He added it to the cart.

"And the plywood?" Sanda asked.

"I'll get that at Home Depot. This weekend."

He never would. He did not know that yet. He would think about the plywood several times, always on the way somewhere else.

He lay there with the phone glowing on his chest, looking at his cart — a quarter mile of barbed wire, a pair of gauntlets, and a cordless nail gun — and he could not believe the conversation he was having. Buying guns had always felt perfectly reasonable to him. Buying barbed wire felt insane. It occurred to him that most people would see it exactly the other way around, that his neighbors would think nothing of a box of barbed wire and would call the police about an AR-15, and he did not know what that said about him, or about them. He felt, for the first time, that it was getting harder to tell himself apart from the people on the videos, the man in the shipping container, the woman with the wall of rice. At least he wasn't buying canned food and water and a year of toilet paper. At least he had a plan, and the plan was guns.

Then, because he was a responsible man, he searched for water, and found a case of Perrier, twenty-four half-liter bottles, and added it to the cart.

"There," John said, and tapped *Place your order*. "Water."

"That's sparkling water," Sanda said into her pillow.

"It's still water."

"Mm-hm," Sanda said, and fell asleep.

* * *

The box came Thursday afternoon. John knew it had come because his phone told him, at his desk at work, with a soft chime: *Someone is at your front door.* He opened the doorbell app and watched a delivery driver in a brown uniform set a long, heavy box on his mat, take a photograph of it, and walk away, and Charlie, invisible inside the apartment, barked at the man the whole way to the truck.

When he got home there was also a notice taped to the door from the leasing office, on letterhead, which said that the community's front and rear gates were being upgraded to a new cloud-based access system from Halcyon Access Systems, and that all residents should download the Halcyon app for keyless entry, and that the community thanked them for their patience during this exciting improvement.

"Another app," John said, peeling the notice off the door.

The gate had been half-installed for two weeks. The old keypad was still there, but the steel enclosure next to it, where the gate's controller lived, had been left with its cover off, and the cover was nowhere to be seen. John, because he was an engineer and could not walk past an open box with wires in it, had stopped the car on his way out one morning and looked inside. There was a new green circuit board in there, bolted to the back of the box, with a cellular antenna on a short cable and a little blinking light, and the board was stamped in white letters with the name HALCYON. Below it sat the old motor and its fat power leads, unchanged, with a low-voltage line coming in from a transformer on the post. He had noted how it was wired, the way he noted everything, and had driven to work and forgotten it.

He carried the box of barbed wire into the garage and set it on the shelf beside the Christmas bins, and he did not open it.

Then he came back inside and stood in the middle of his apartment and looked around, as if he had never seen it before, and began, for no reason he could have explained, to count.

The front door had a smart lock that opened with an app on his phone or with a code on a keypad or — thank God — with a key, an actual brass key on his actual key ring, which he had insisted on keeping. Next to the door was the video doorbell, which watched the walk and the street and sent a picture to his phone every time a lizard crossed the welcome mat. On the wall in the hall was the smart thermostat, which learned their habits, and which had apparently learned that John liked it at seventy-one and Sanda liked it at seventy-four, and which now spent most of its time trying to split the difference, the way a marriage counselor would. On the bookshelf was the smart speaker with its blue ring. In the corner of the living room, on its charging dock, sat the robot vacuum, a fat black disk the size of a hubcap that wandered the apartment every weekday at noon, sucking up Charlie's hair and getting stuck under the couch. Sanda had named it Dorel.

"Why Dorel?" John had asked, the day it arrived.

"Because Dorel is the guy in every Romanian joke," Sanda had explained. "He's the workman. He's the one who builds the stairs upside down. He installs the toilet on the ceiling. He paints the wall and then paints the window too, because it was there. Everything Dorel does, he does with complete confidence, and it's wrong."
