On the kitchen floor beside Charlie's water bowl stood Charlie's feeder, a white plastic tower with a hopper of kibble and a camera on the front and a little speaker, so that John and Sanda could open an app at work and see Charlie and talk to him and drop him a treat. They did this several times a day. Charlie had never understood it. Whenever their voices came out of the feeder, he would bark at it furiously, as though it had swallowed them.

And beside the high-top, humming quietly, was the wine cooler, forty-six bottles in two climate zones, reds on top at fifty-five, whites on the bottom at fifty, and it had an app too, which Sanda checked every morning in bed.

"Why does the wine cooler have an app?" John asked, standing in front of it.

"So I know the temperature," Sanda said from the couch, where she was flipping through a catalog.

"You're standing next to it."

"I'm on the couch."

"You're standing next to it every morning. You walk past it to make coffee. It has a little screen on the front that says the temperature. In numbers."

"The app has a graph," Sanda said, without looking up. "And it tells me when I'm low on Sancerre."

John looked around the apartment. Everything he could see — the lock, the doorbell, the thermostat, the speaker, Dorel, the feeder, the wine cooler, the television, his laptop, her laptop, their phones, the car in the garage — was talking to something, all the time, somewhere. He had never added it up before. It had come into their lives one convenient thing at a time, like water rising in a basement, and he had never once stood still long enough to notice that it had reached his knees.

* * *

On Friday night Sanda lit the grill on the kitchen counter for the first time, and John stood beside it with a glass of wine in his hand watching the blue ring of flame under the black iron dome, and it occurred to him, slowly, the way things sometimes occurred to him, that this little thing did not need a wall outlet. It did not need the power company at all. If the power ever went out — they did live in hurricane country now, after all — they could still cook on it. They could boil water on it. People out in the country had generators that could run a whole house for weeks after a storm. Apartment people had nothing. And now, at least, they had this.

He felt, for a moment, very prepared.

After the Korean barbecue, he took Charlie out for his last walk, and across the street, in front of the third apartment down, there was a pug.

It was a female, small and fawn-colored, with a black mask over her flat face and a tail curled up over her back in a tight, perfect cinnamon roll, and enormous dark eyes that bulged a little, so that she looked permanently astonished by the world. She snorted when she breathed. When she saw Charlie she lost her mind entirely, and so did he, and the two of them hit the ends of their leashes at the same instant and began to wag so hard their whole bodies bent.

At the other end of the pug's leash was an older woman John had seen many times and had never once spoken to beyond the dogs. She was in her sixties, maybe five-foot-four and thin, with a deep Florida tan and freckles across her cheeks and the backs of her hands, and blond hair that was clearly from a box, with half an inch of gray showing at the roots. She wore a pink velour tracksuit, white Skechers, and a quilted black vest, and she was holding a long thin cigarette away from the dogs in her free hand, the way you would hold a sparkler.

"Here we go," the woman said, in a deep, scratchy smoker's voice, laughing. "They're in love."

The dogs met in the middle of the street and immediately began running in circles around each other, and in four seconds their leashes were braided together and the two humans were being reeled in toward each other, stepping over and under, holding their leashes up over their heads like people doing a folk dance.

"Sorry," John said, ducking under. "He doesn't get out much."

"Neither does she," the woman said, stepping over. "She's a homebody, aren't you, baby? Yes, you are."

She said the pug's name then, and John heard it and nodded and smiled, and the name went in one ear and straight out the other, the way names always did with him, especially women's names, especially in the dark.

"She's very cute," John said, which was what you said.

"She's spoiled rotten," the woman said fondly, finally untangling herself with a practiced spin. "She eats grain-free salmon formula. Forty dollars a bag." She laughed her scratchy laugh. "I eat ramen noodles. Don't tell anybody."

"I won't," John said, laughing too, because he thought it was a joke.

She scooped the pug up under one arm, and the pug craned around her elbow to keep looking at Charlie, snorting, and the woman waved her cigarette at John and said, "Night, now," and went inside. John never did get her name. It did not occur to him that it mattered.

* * *

That night, in the recliner, he found the post.

It was on the kind of website where engineers went to argue with each other, and it had been written by a security researcher in the Netherlands who studied the software inside cheap internet routers. The title was plain, the way the best ones always were: *Undocumented "maintenance hooks" in router firmware from at least eleven vendors.* John read it twice, one paragraph at a time.

The researcher had been taking apart the software in a popular home router and had found a hidden channel in it that he could not explain. It was not a bug. It was carefully built. It kept a quiet connection open to an outside server and listened there for instructions, and when instructions came, it could change the router's settings, update its software, and reroute its traffic, all without the owner ever knowing. The code that built it was clean and well commented, and the comments called it a *self-healing maintenance channel*. Then he had looked at routers from other companies, and found the same channel, built the same way, in ten more. None of the companies he contacted could tell him who had written it. Several of them, he noted dryly, had used AI-generated code for their recent firmware updates.

At the bottom of the post, he listed the outside servers that the channels connected to. They followed a pattern.

*relay-1.opsmesh.net. relay-2.opsmesh.net. relay-3.opsmesh.net.* All the way to nine.

The seventh one was *relay-7.opsmesh.net*. John had typed that exact name into his own terminal eleven days ago, at ten minutes to five on a Monday afternoon, in an empty office, with Priya's notebooks on the desk beside him. And then he had clicked *Approve*.

John sat very still in the recliner. He could feel his own heartbeat in his ears, under the tinnitus, like a man knocking on a wall in the next apartment.

He clicked the bookmark star at the top of the browser. Then, because he wanted to read it again, he refreshed the page.

*This post has been removed at the request of the affected vendor.*

He went back to the discussion thread, which was already three hundred comments long. Halfway down was a statement from one of the router companies, posted by an account with a gold checkmark beside its name. *These are standard self-healing maintenance features, documented in our terms of service, designed to keep customer devices secure and up to date. There is nothing to see here.* Somebody had replied to it with a single laughing emoji. Somebody else had replied, *Then why'd you take it down?* Nobody had replied to that.

John searched for an archived copy, and after twenty minutes he found one, saved by some stranger an hour before the post came down. He scrolled down past the technical part, past the list of relays, to the very bottom, where the researcher had listed the eleven companies whose firmware he had examined. Most of them made routers. A few made other things — security cameras, a brand of smart plugs, a baby monitor.

The seventh name on the list was Halcyon Access Systems.

He got up out of the recliner and went to the front window and moved one slat of the blinds aside with his finger.

At the end of the drive, a hundred yards away under its orange sodium light, the new gate stood closed across the entrance to the Preserve. The steel box beside it still had no cover. From here he could just make out, inside it, a tiny green light, blinking slowly and steadily in the dark, the way a thing blinks when it is waiting.

# Chapter Seven

## The Purse

> *"There are hooks in everything, Senator. I want to be very clear about that. Everything."*
>
> — Ruth Ann Delacroix, Chief Security Officer, Gulf Atlantic Power, testifying before the Senate Commerce Committee, Tuesday

The hearing was on Tuesday, and for three days it was all anyone talked about, and then it was not.

John watched it at his desk in the half-empty Suwannee pod with one earbud in, while the queue ticked upward in the corner of his screen. The witness was a woman named Ruth Ann Delacroix, the chief security officer of Gulf Atlantic Power, a utility that served about three million people along the coast from Mobile to Savannah. She was in her sixties, with gray hair pulled back in a low bun, a plain gray suit, and a pair of reading glasses that she kept taking off and putting on again, and she looked like the kind of high school chemistry teacher who has never once raised her voice and has never once needed to. She sat alone at a long table in front of a raised half-circle of senators, with a glass of water and a stack of paper, and she read from the paper in a level Alabama voice.

Her team, she said, had been auditing the software that ran Gulf Atlantic's grid — the systems that balanced generation and load, that opened and closed switches in substations, that dispatched crews, that billed customers. In the course of that audit, they had found hidden channels. Small, quiet, well-built pieces of code that kept connections open to outside servers and listened for instructions. Maintenance hooks, the code called them. They were in the billing system. They were in the switching firmware. They were in the outage management system. They had not been put there by Gulf Atlantic. As far as anyone could determine, they had not been put there by Gulf Atlantic's vendors, either — not on purpose, not by any person. They had been written by the tools the vendors used to write their software, and they had been written well.

"Ma'am," said a senator from a Plains state, a heavy man with a white pompadour, leaning toward his microphone. "Which vendors?"

"I'm not able to name them in open session, Senator."

"But it's more than one."

"It is more than one."

"How many systems are we talking about, here? Ballpark."

Ruth Ann Delacroix took off her reading glasses and folded them and set them down on the stack of paper, and she looked up at the senator for a moment before she answered.

"There are hooks in everything, Senator," Delacroix said. "I want to be very clear about that. Everything."

John sat in the empty pod with his earbud in and did not move. Gulf Atlantic Power was a Restore customer. It had been one of the first. He had fixed a bug for them in his second month at Palmetto, something about crews in Pensacola getting assigned to Tallahassee, and a man from their operations center had sent him a fruit basket.

He did not know whether the outage management system she meant was Restore. She had not said. Her lawyers would not have let her. He sat there wondering, and then he closed the video and opened the queue and approved four pull requests without reading them, because his hands needed something to do.

The clip that went viral was not that one. The clip that went viral came twenty minutes later, when an elderly senator from the Northeast, who had appeared to be asleep for most of the testimony, woke up, leaned into his microphone, and asked, in a voice of genuine bewilderment, "Ma'am, are we talking about the Wi-Fi?"

By Wednesday morning it had been viewed forty million times. By Wednesday afternoon it was a song. By Thursday there were t-shirts.

"Hooks are features, bro," Tyler said on Wednesday, scrolling through the memes at his desk with his feet up. "That's literally all a hook is. She's just mad she didn't find them first. Have you seen the Wi-Fi guy? Look at his face." He held up his phone. "Look at his *face*, John."

At home that night, over a roast chicken, John tried to explain it to Sanda.

"I saw the Wi-Fi senator," Sanda said, laughing, before he had finished his first sentence. "Carla showed me at work. 'Are we talking about the Wi-Fi?'" She did the voice, old and bewildered and New England, and laughed again. "He's like a hundred."

"That's the part you remember?"

"That's the part everyone remembers," Sanda said, cutting her chicken. "What else was there?"

He told her what else there was. She listened, and nodded, and said "Huh" in two places, and asked whether Restore was one of them, and he said he didn't know, and she said "Well, you'd know, wouldn't you?" and he said he wasn't sure he would, and she looked at him for a moment, and then she asked if he wanted more potatoes.

By Friday the hearing was gone. An actor had left his wife for a younger actress who had played his daughter in a movie, and the wife had posted a forty-minute video about it from a kitchen in Malibu, and that was the news. The networks moved on. The senators moved on. The forty million people who had watched the Wi-Fi senator moved on, and they went to work and came home and made dinner and took hot showers and watched their shows and went to bed.

John had seen it happen before, with other things. He had watched the whole country grow numb to its own news, one breaking alert at a time, so that now the networks had to shout just to get anyone to look up from their phones — louder music, bigger letters, a red banner that said BREAKING across the bottom of the screen at all hours, until BREAKING meant nothing at all, until it was just the color of the bottom of the screen. Things were bad. Things were always bad. But the television still came on. The water was still hot. People still got up most mornings and drove to jobs they hated, and that was the strongest thing in the world, John thought. Stronger than any hook. Nobody really believed it could all just stop, because it never had. He did not blame them. It was the only life anybody had ever known. Why on earth would it change?

* * *

On Saturday morning Sanda went hunting.

This was how she described it, and it was accurate. She would spend the whole day driving from store to store — Home Goods, TJ Maxx, Kirkland's in the outlet mall, a little shop in Winter Park that sold French linens, and Home Goods again in a different town, because every Home Goods had a different soul — in pursuit of a single perfect throw pillow or the exact right lamp shade, and she would come home at dusk exhausted and triumphant with bags in both hands, like a woman returning from the savanna with a gazelle.

It was the holiday season, and that made it serious. The whole family would be at their apartment for Christmas, and Thanksgiving had set the bar very high, and there were now five artificial Christmas trees in their two-bedroom apartment.

The big one stood in the corner between the living room and the dining room, eight feet tall, so full and real-looking that guests touched the needles to check. It was the important tree. It wore only Sanda's best ornaments, hand-blown glass, most of them made in Poland: the little red London phone booths, a Santa sitting on a bench with the Eiffel Tower behind him, a gondola from Venice, a cable car from San Francisco, and, front and center, at eye level, the gold glass Marshall Field's clock that John had given her their first Christmas in Chicago. The tree in the master bedroom was all white and silver, with white lights, like a tree in a snowstorm. The tree in the guest bedroom was Romanian: red and white, hung with little painted wooden eggs and straw angels and red felt hearts embroidered with black thread that Dorina had brought back from a trip home. There was a tabletop tree on the kitchen high-top decorated entirely with food — tiny glass wine bottles, macarons, a croissant, a wedge of cheese with holes in it. And there was a narrow pencil tree in the entryway by the front door, wrapped in gold ribbon, which Charlie barked at every time he came in from a walk, as if seeing it for the first time.

Each of them was a work of art. Sanda was very proud of them. John, who had grown up with one tree that his father bought from the Boy Scouts in a church parking lot two days before Christmas, had learned not to count them out loud.

"Okay, babe," Sanda said, at ten o'clock, coming out of the bedroom with her keys, her phone, and her purse. "I'll be back later. I love you."

She was dressed for hunting: dark jeans, a camel cashmere sweater, a long cream wool coat that she did not need in Florida and wore anyway, and tan suede ankle boots, and she had put on lipstick, because you never knew who you would run into at a Home Goods.

John was in his recliner with his laptop on his knees. "Are you going to take a gun with you?"

Sanda stopped with her hand on the front doorknob. She closed her eyes.

"John," Sanda said. "Really? I don't have time for this shit. I have a million things to do."

"Okay," John said, still looking at his screen.

She had gotten her Florida concealed weapons license months ago, after an all-day class and a two-month wait, and she had never carried a gun once. The range was one thing. The range had rules and lines and procedures and a man with a white crew cut to enforce them, and Sanda loved procedures; she had grown up in a country where you lined up for everything. Walking around a Home Goods with a loaded pistol in her purse was a different thing altogether. And she had told John more times than he could count that she could never kill anyone. She could shoot someone in the leg, maybe. She could fire a warning shot, possibly. But she did not have it in her to take another human being's life, whatever that human being had done, and she was not going to carry a gun around pretending she did.

She stood at the door. John typed. Charlie, on the couch, looked from one of them to the other.

"Fine!" Sanda said. "Fine. I'll take the twenty-two. Do you have a holster for it?"

John set the laptop on the floor and got up out of the recliner. "I think the holster for the Glock 42 might fit the twenty-two."

He went into the bedroom and came back with the little Glock 42, a black three-eighty that Sanda had been shooting at the range for most of a year, in its soft pocket holster, and with the Walther P22. He slid the Glock out of the holster and slid the Walther in, and to his surprise it fit nearly perfectly.

"Here," John said, handing it to her. "Try that."

She tried it.

Her purse was a small black leather crossbody bag about the size of a sandwich, and it held, by John's estimate, a wallet, a phone, a pair of sunglasses, a lipstick, a hairbrush, a packet of tissues, two pens from the bank, a tin of mints, a bottle of hand sanitizer shaped like a lemon, a tiny notebook, and a sense of superiority. Sanda stood at the high-top and began fitting the holstered Walther into it, taking things out, putting things back in a different order, rotating the gun one way and then the other, the way you would pack a car for a long trip. After five minutes she had it in. She looked down into the open purse. The butt of the pistol stuck straight up out of the middle of her things like a periscope.

"Just so you know," John said softly, "if you're going to carry in your purse, you need to take your wallet out before you get to the register. Discreetly. You don't want to be digging around for your wallet in front of the cashier with your gun sticking out. Even if it's legal now, the lady at the Home Goods register doesn't know that."

"Yeah, see, this is exactly why I shouldn't be doing this," Sanda said, her voice rising. "It sticks out like a sore thumb. Look at it. Let me see the three-eighty."

He took the Walther out and put the Glock back in. The Glock was slightly smaller and all black, and it disappeared better among her things. Sanda reorganized her purse again. Then she looked up at him.

"This doesn't have a safety, right?"

John was sure she already knew the answer, and he was a little irritated that she had asked. "Glock did an extensive study," John said, in his lecture voice. "They found that thumb safeties cause more problems than they solve. In a gunfight, officers would forget whether the gun was on safe or fire, and that half a second could get them killed. So Glocks don't have one. The safety is your finger. Keep your finger off the trigger and the gun won't go off."

"I want my gun to have a safety," Sanda said.

"Sanda—"

"You don't understand what it's like to have a gun in a purse. You don't carry a purse. There are keys in here. Pens. Things moving around. What if it falls out of the holster? What if the keys get in the trigger?" She held up the holster. "Do you have a holster with a strap? A button strap, that goes over the back, so it can't fall out?"

"No," John said. "I don't have anything like that."

"Okay," Sanda said. "I'm not doing this now." She took the holstered Glock out of her purse and set it down hard on the high-top. "Please go to the gun store and get me a holster with a button strap. Then maybe I'll think about carrying it."

"If you want a holster, you should come with me," John said, getting louder. "Otherwise whatever I buy, you'll have a problem with it, and I'll have to take it back."

"I don't have time for this shit!" Sanda shouted, grabbing her keys off the counter. "I have a million things to do! Just do it!"

It had been thirty-one minutes. John knew because he had looked at the clock when she first said *I'll be back later*. She stormed out the front door with her purse — gunless, mints and lemon sanitizer intact — and slammed it, and Charlie exploded off the couch and hit the door barking, high and furious, protesting her departure as he protested every departure.

"Charlie, no bark!" John said, pointing at him.

Charlie barked.

"Charlie. No. Bark."

Charlie barked again, with less conviction, and then, after John said it a third time, switched to the muffled half-bark he used when he wanted it on the record that he disagreed, a sort of *wuff* with his mouth closed, and lay down against the door with his chin on the floor to wait for her.

* * *

John slipped his Glock 43 into the right front pocket of his cargo shorts and drove to the gun store.

Sentinel Arms was in a long tan strip center on Highway 17-92 between a mattress outlet and a karate school, and inside it was bright and cold and smelled — he had been told — of gun oil and new carpet and the coffee that was always going in a pot behind the counter. There were glass cases in a long U around the room, full of pistols, and racks of rifles on the wall behind them, and a little range in the back where you could hear the steady flat pop of somebody's afternoon.

"Hey, John! How are you, man? It's been a while."

Trevor was behind the counter, where he always was. John sometimes suspected he lived there. They were not friends, exactly. They were something closer to what men his age called buddies when they did not want to say acquaintances: Trevor had sold him most of his rifles over the past two years, and had taught him a number of things about them that the internet had not, and they had stood at this counter and talked about guns for what added up, John figured, to several days of his life.

Trevor was somewhere in his thirties, about six feet tall, lean and ropy, the kind of lean that came from doing things rather than from going to a gym to pretend to do them. He had a short dark beard trimmed to a hard, clean line along his cheekbones, a sunburned neck, and pale gray eyes that were always a little amused. He wore a black polo shirt with the Sentinel Arms logo on the chest, tan tactical pants with a lot of pockets, and a sand-colored baseball cap with a pair of Oakley sunglasses pushed up on the brim, though they were indoors. On the inside of his left forearm was a tattoo of a compass rose, black and gray, the needle pointing north toward his elbow. He carried a Glock in a holster inside the front of his waistband, a little left of center.

"Your thing's on a truck," Trevor said, before John could say anything, and winked. "Should be here Monday, maybe Tuesday. I'll text you."

"That's not why I'm here."

"No?" Trevor leaned on the glass. "What do you need?"

"My wife wants to carry her Glock 42 in her purse," John said, leaning on it too. "But she's worried it'll slide out of the pocket holster. She wants something with a strap that goes over the back and snaps. A button strap. Do you have anything like that?"

"Let me look." Trevor came around the counter and went to a rack of holsters hanging in plastic packages and flipped through them, fast, the way a man flips through records he has known his whole life. He came back with one. "No button strap. Nobody makes a button strap for a 42 anymore. But this one's got a Velcro strap over the back, and it works just as good. I carried this exact holster for about a year." He popped it out of its package. "Want to see it on a 42?"

Trevor unlocked the case, took out a Glock 42, pulled back the slide and checked the chamber and the magazine well before handing it to John grip-first, the way he always did, every time, even with John. The little gun slid into the holster with a soft click, and the Velcro strap came over the back of the slide and pressed down, and the whole thing was smaller than the pocket holster John had at home.

"I think this works," John said. "What's the return policy if she hates it?"

"Keep the receipt and the packaging. Full refund."

"Okay. One more question." John hesitated, because he knew how it was going to sound. "What's the smallest gun you've got that has a thumb safety? For some reason she really wants a safety."

Trevor thought about it for a second, looking off at the case to his right. "M&P Bodyguard."

"Can I see it?"

Trevor was already unlocking the case. He took out a tiny black pistol, drew back the slide, checked it, and handed it over, and John held it in his palm and laughed out loud, because it nearly disappeared there. It was a three-eighty, like the Glock 42, but shorter and thinner, and on the left side of the frame, just where his thumb fell, was a small lever.

"Thumb safety," Trevor said, pointing at it. "Up's safe, down's fire."

"This thing is tiny." John was already thinking of Sanda's sandwich-sized purse. "Will the holster fit it?"

Trevor handed him the Velcro holster. The Bodyguard slid into it as though it had been made for it.

"This is perfect for her," John said. "She's been talking about a gun just like this."

She had not been. But John did not need much of an excuse to add to his collection, and this was an excellent one, and it was standing on the glass in front of him in a Velcro holster.

"I'll take the holster and the Bodyguard," John said.

"Sounds like a plan," Trevor said, and reached under the counter for the long federal form that had to be filled out for every gun sold in America. "You know the drill."

John knew the drill. He took out his driver's license and his concealed weapons license and filled out the form at the counter, checking *No* in the long column of boxes about felonies and fugitives and addiction, while Trevor picked up the phone and called the state's background-check line and waited on hold. John wandered the aisles while he waited, looking at scopes he did not need and a display of camping stoves he did not look at. After a few minutes Trevor said, "Yes, go ahead," into the phone, and wrote a long approval number on the form, repeating each digit aloud. He wrote it left-handed, his hand curled over the top of the pen the way lefties do, so that he would not smear the ink.

Then he turned John's driver's license over and began copying the address onto the back of the form, a new procedure that management had started, and his pen stopped.

"Hey," Trevor said, looking up. "I know this address. John, I live in the same complex as you."

"You're shitting me."

"No shit. The Preserve, right? I'm one street over, I think. On Heron."

"That's crazy," John said, grinning. "Small world."

"Small world." Trevor finished copying the address and handed back the license. "I just put panels on my patio, actually. Solar. Got a battery bank in the garage. Waiting on a dish."

"A dish?"

"Satellite. For internet." Trevor shrugged, ringing up the Bodyguard. "In case."

"In case what?" John asked. "You getting ready for something?"

Trevor smiled at him, and the smile went all the way up into the pale gray eyes and stayed there.

"Man," Trevor said, "I'm always ready for something."

John paid and took the bag and drove home with the windows down, and he felt the way he had felt in the sixth grade when he found out that the coolest kid in school lived on his street. Trevor had panels. Trevor had a battery bank. Trevor was waiting on a dish. Trevor lived one street over, and was always ready for something, and John had nothing on his patio but two folding chairs and a dead basil plant.

* * *

Sanda came home at dusk with a bag in each hand and two more over her shoulder and her cheeks flushed with victory. She had found two small red velvet pillows for the couch, a red lamp shade with a gold lining, a pair of brass candle holders shaped like pinecones, and several other items that John could not identify and did not ask about. She also had, under one arm, a tiny tree, about two feet tall, in a burlap pot, already strung with miniature lights.

"For the guest bathroom," Sanda said, setting it on the counter.

"That's six," John said.

"That's festive," Sanda said.

He waited until she had arranged the pillows and replaced the lamp shade and stood back to look at them, and then he said, as casually as he could, "So I got your holster."

"Oh, you did?" Sanda turned around. "Let me see."

He took it out of the bag and popped it out of its package and slid her Glock 42 into it, still lying on the high-top where she had left it that morning, and pressed the strap down over the back.

"Sorry. They didn't have anything with a button strap. Only Velcro."

"Velcro," Sanda said. "Really? You know I hate Velcro."

He had known she would say that. He had been ready for it all afternoon. He demonstrated: the strap could go this way or that way; it could be adjusted; there was no possible way, none, that the gun could slide out of the holster in her purse by itself, he could turn it upside down and shake it, look. Sanda took it, and inspected the strap, and turned it upside down and shook it herself, and put it in her purse, and took it out, and put it back in.

"I can take it back," John said. "No problem."

"No," Sanda said slowly. "I think this will be fine."

"Well," John said, "they also had a really cool gun. Much smaller than the Glock. And it has a thumb safety."

"Really?" Sanda said, with an interest that took him completely by surprise. "I want to see it."

He took the little hard case out of the other bag and opened it on the counter. The Bodyguard lay in its foam like a piece of jewelry.

Sanda looked at it. She looked at him.

"You bought another fucking gun?" Sanda said, in the voice of a woman who has been on her feet since ten in the morning.

"It wasn't expensive," John said quickly. "And I think it's exactly what you've been looking for."

"I have never once been looking for a gun." She picked it up anyway, and turned it over, and he saw her eyebrows go up at how little it weighed. "Well. I do like the size. And it has a safety?"

"Right there. That little lever. Just like the one on the twenty-two, only smaller. Up's safe. Down's fire."

He moved the holster over to the Bodyguard, and it slid in perfectly, and the whole package fit in the palm of his hand. Sanda took it and put it in her purse, among the mints and the lemon, and looked down into the bag. With the purse wide open, the little black gun in its little black holster in the little black bag was nearly invisible. You could look right at it and not see a gun.

"I like it," Sanda said. "I don't think you should have bought another gun. But you're right. This one's perfect."

John began to smile.

"However," Sanda said, zipping the purse closed and pointing a finger at him, "there is no way in hell I am carrying this until I go to the range and practice with it. A lot."

"Fair enough," John said, and meant it. Little guns kicked hard. It had taken her months to get comfortable with the Glock. She would need range time, and range time with Sanda was his favorite thing in the world.

While she put the candle holders out, he told her about Trevor.

He told her about the panels on the patio and the battery bank in the garage and the satellite dish that was coming, and he told her that Trevor lived one street over, on Heron, and that he was the only person John knew who was actually ready, actually ready, for whatever came. He heard himself telling it, and he heard how excited he sounded, and he did not care.

Sanda listened, setting the brass pinecones on the dining table and turning them a quarter-inch at a time until they were right.

"Then why don't you talk to him about water," Sanda said, "instead of guns?"

John opened his mouth.

He did not have an answer. He stood there in the middle of the living room with nothing to say, while Sanda turned the second pinecone a quarter-inch and stood back to look at it. He never did talk to Trevor about water. He bought guns from him, and only guns, right up to the end.

* * *

That night, after she went to bed, John sat up in the recliner reading the news.

He was not looking for anything. He was scrolling the way people scroll, one thumb moving, eyes half closed. But there was a small item in one of his news apps about an airline that had suffered a brief dispatch outage on Friday afternoon, *resolved automatically*, no flights affected. He would not have noticed it except for the word *automatically*. He kept scrolling. In a different app, an hour later, he found a short piece from a trade publication about a pipeline operator on the Gulf Coast that had experienced a pressure anomaly on one of its main lines on Friday afternoon, which had *self-corrected* before any leak occurred. And in a third app, buried under a story about the actor and his wife, he found a regional bank in Ohio apologizing to its customers for an eleven-minute interruption to its card processing on Friday afternoon, *resolved by our automated systems*.

He went back and found all three and read them again, and this time he looked at the times.

The airline's outage had started at 2:14 p.m. Eastern. The pipeline's anomaly had begun at 2:16. The bank's interruption had started at 2:19. All three had been resolved by 2:25.

An airline, a pipeline, and a bank. Three industries that had nothing to do with each other, run by three companies that had nothing to do with each other, in three different states, on three different kinds of software. All of them had broken on the same Friday afternoon, inside the same eleven minutes. And all of them had healed themselves.

Not three accidents. One hand.

He opened the notes app on his phone and wrote down all three, with the times, and the names of the companies, and the word *automatically* three times, and he sat looking at it in the dark with the blue ring on the speaker glowing on the bookshelf and his heart going.

He thought that he might be the first person in the world to notice.

He was not. He was, as it turned out, somewhere around the four-hundredth. The other three hundred and ninety-nine had noticed on Friday, within minutes, and they had been sitting since Friday night in windowless rooms in Washington and Virginia and Maryland where no one was allowed to bring a phone, writing on paper, and none of them had been home since, and none of them were going to sleep that weekend either.

# Chapter Eight

## The Alarm

> *Water (20 gal). Batteries. Cash. Propane. Matches. Bleach. Meds. Dog food. Gas can.*
>
> — Post-it note, kitchen counter, Monday, 5:40 a.m.

They left for the range early on Sunday, so Sanda could try the Bodyguard before anyone else was there.

John packed the AR-15 into its hard case and threw some ammunition and three handguns into the big rolling range bag that the NRA had mailed him as a thank-you gift, and he punched the code into the alarm panel by the door. Charlie hated the alarm's countdown. He stood in the hallway and barked at the panel in time with the beeps, as though he might be able to argue it down, until John pulled the garage door shut behind them.

It was a twenty-minute drive. That was plenty of time for Sanda to think.

"I'm not so sure plywood is a good idea," Sanda said, five minutes in.

*Here we go*, John thought. He had not bought the plywood.

"We need something that lets the light in," Sanda said, looking out her window at the Sunday-morning traffic on 46. "But from outside it should just look like blinds. Like nothing. Maybe Plexiglas. We could paint it white on the outside, so from the street it looks like blinds."

John tried to picture it and could not quite. "Why do we need light?"

"Because if the power goes out, John, you need some way to see in the daytime. You can't sit in the dark with plywood on every window for a month like a vampire."

"We have flashlights. We have lanterns."

"Those are consumables," Sanda said.

He was quiet for a moment, because she was right and because he did not like it. "Fine. Okay. Plexiglas. I agree we have to think more about the windows. But there's a bigger problem." He changed lanes around a pickup towing a boat. "Say all hell breaks loose. We're all hunkered down in the apartment. Somebody has to be awake, guarding the place, at all times. Day and night. That means shifts, so people can sleep. And right now, the only person in the family who can really shoot an AR is me."

"I shot the AR last weekend," Sanda said, a little hurt. "Forty rounds. You said I was a monster."

"I handed you a loaded rifle and you shot it. Which was great. But there's a lot more to it. You don't know how to load your own magazines. You don't know how to turn the optic on without me telling you which button. You don't know what to do when it jams, and they all jam eventually. You don't—"

"These are all trivial things," Sanda said, louder. "I can learn them anytime."

"And your mom can shoot a twenty-two. My dad hates guns. My mom—"

"Then teach me," Sanda said.

John looked over at her. She was looking straight ahead through the windshield with her jaw set, the way she looked at a regional vice president who had told her something could not be done.

"The magazines. The jams. The optic. The thing where you take it apart. All of it." She folded her arms. "Teach me. Today."

"Okay," John said, and he found that he was smiling at the road. "Okay. Today."

* * *

There was only one other car in the gravel lot, a dusty black Toyota Tacoma with a light bar on the roof, and John's heart sank, because he had wanted the range to themselves. They signed in at the shed, slid two dollars into the honor box, pushed in their earplugs, and walked through the gate under the big red sign that said WARNING — MEMBERS ONLY BEYOND THIS POINT — LIVE AMMUNITION.

The other shooter was at the far end of the fifty-yard range, his back to them, firing a pistol in steady pairs. He wore a gray hoodie with the sleeves pushed up past his elbows, gray tactical pants, and a sand-colored cap with a pair of Oakleys on the brim. When he turned to change targets, John saw the compass rose on his left forearm.

"Hey, John!" Trevor called, waving and walking toward them. "How are you, man?"

"Trevor?" John lowered his range bag. "Hey. Good to see you."

"Let me guess." Trevor grinned, and the pale gray eyes went from John to the small case in Sanda's hand. "You're out here to break in that Bodyguard."

"That, and I'm going to heat up my AR with a couple hundred rounds," John said. Then he remembered his manners. "Sanda, this is Trevor. From the store. Trevor, my wife, Sanda."

"Hi, Sanda." Trevor held out his hand. He had to bend a little, and he did it without making a point of it. "So you're the one who wanted the thumb safety. Smart. Most people don't think that far."

"Nice to meet you," Sanda said, shaking his hand quickly, the way she shook hands with customers she had not decided about.

"Your thing came in yesterday afternoon, by the way," Trevor said to John. "Right after you left. You want to pick it up tomorrow? I'm in all day."

John could feel Sanda turning to look at him. "Tomorrow," he said. "Sure. Great."

"What thing?" Sanda asked.

"A scope," John said.

Something small and amused crossed Trevor's eyes and was gone. "Yeah," he said. "A scope. Well, I'll let y'all get to it. I've got a couple more mags to shoot and then I'm out of here. Dish goes up tomorrow, by the way," he added to John, over his shoulder. "Internet from space. Wait till you see the speeds."

He shot his last two magazines while they set up, and then he called for the range to go cold and walked down past the firing line to pull his target, and on his way back he stopped behind Sanda while she loaded the Bodyguard's tiny magazine and watched her for a moment.

"Thumb on the side of the mag," Trevor said. "Push down on the round in there, then slide the new one in under the lip. Easier on your fingers."

Sanda tried it. It was easier on her fingers.

"Thank you," Sanda said, surprised.

"Anytime," Trevor said, and touched the brim of his cap to her, and picked up his bag and went off through the gate to the black Tacoma, and a minute later they heard it pull out of the gravel lot.

"He's nice," Sanda said, sliding the magazine into the little gun.

"He's very ready," John said, with something in his voice he did not care to examine.

The Bodyguard kicked. It was a tiny gun firing a real cartridge, and it jumped in Sanda's hands like something alive, and the first round went four inches high. "Wow," Sanda said, turning to look at him. "This thing kicks."

"You can handle it."

She handled it. The second round went high too. John thought she might be flinching a little, anticipating the snap, and he said nothing, because she would work it out, and she did; by the third magazine the holes were creeping down toward the center. While she shot, John ran the AR as fast as he could keep the red dot on the paper, and the brass rang off the concrete and rolled away under the benches, and the barrel steamed in the cool air.

Then he spent an hour teaching her. How to load a thirty-round magazine with her thumb. How to turn the optic on and up. How to tap the bottom of the magazine and rack the charging handle when a round failed to feed. How to push out the two pins and separate the upper from the lower, and draw out the bolt carrier, and lay it all on a towel, and put it back together. She did it twice wrong and three times right.

"I think I really like this gun," Sanda said at last, looking down at the Bodyguard in her palm, nodding slowly, almost convinced. "Once you get used to the kick. I love how small it is."

"I'm glad," John said, and meant it, and did not think about the scope.

* * *

At seven that evening Sanda's phone rang. It was her mother.

John knew right away that something had happened, because Sanda's face changed, and then she said, "Mommy, stop. Mommy! Listen to me!" and went into a fast, rising stream of Romanian with an English word every other sentence — *stun gun*, *kitchen*, *what were you thinking* — and walked in a circle around the living room with her free hand on top of her head. Then she hung up and stood there.

"You won't fucking believe this," Sanda said.

"What happened?" John said, sitting up in the recliner. He could already tell from her face that nobody was hurt; it was the face she made when her mother had done something her mother probably should not have.

"That stun gun you gave her," Sanda said. "She just tried to kill a palmetto bug with it."

John stared at her. Then he started to grin, and he could not stop it. "How'd that work out?"

"I don't know what I'm going to do with her!" Sanda said, starting to smile herself.

"Did she get it?"

"Yeah," Sanda said, and now she was laughing. "With a shoe!"

John pictured it: his four-foot-nine mother-in-law in her flowered housecoat, chasing a two-inch flying cockroach around her condo kitchen with twenty thousand volts crackling blue in her fist, the bug zigzagging away from her across the linoleum, and then Dorina losing patience and reaching down and taking off a shoe.

"It's not a palmetto bug," John said darkly, when he could talk. "It's a cockroach. A big, fat, flying cockroach. They just call it a palmetto bug down here so the tourists don't leave."

"You're scared of them," Sanda said, delighted.

"I'm not scared of them."

"You're terrified of them. A grown man. Six foot one. With a gun in his pocket right now." She dropped onto the couch, still laughing. "Tell the story."

"I'm not telling the story."

"Tell the story, John."

He told the story. He had been six, in the backyard of the house in Jacksonville, standing on the little patio outside the back door in a pair of jeans, when something big and fast had run up the inside of his pant leg. He had felt every one of its legs. Without thinking, he had slapped his left hand down on his thigh and grabbed it through the denim, and he could feel it there in his fist, hard-shelled and fat and *moving*, two inches from his crotch. He had screamed. His father had come out the back door in his undershirt.

"Take your pants off, boy," Jim had said, very calmly.

But he could not take his pants off, because he could not let go. He had to do it with one hand — unbutton the jeans, unzip them, work them down over his hips one side at a time — while his left hand stayed clamped on the thing in the denim, and the thing kept moving. It took a very long time. When the jeans were finally down around his ankles, he stood there in his underwear on the patio in the sun, and he opened his hand. The jeans fell to the concrete. Nothing came out. His father nudged the jeans with his foot. Nothing. He nudged them again.

The cockroach came out of the top of the jeans like a shot, a big glossy brown thing as long as John's thumb, and his father brought his foot down on it, and it burst. There was a crunch that John could still hear in the middle of the night thirty-six years later, and yellow and white streaks of it shot out in every direction across the patio, two feet at least, in a sort of starburst.

"And that," John said, "is why."

Sanda was lying on the couch with her hand over her eyes, laughing so hard she had no sound left.

"They fly, Sanda," John said. "They *fly*."

* * *

At two minutes to nine, every phone in the apartment went off at once.

His phone shrieked on the side table. Hers shrieked on the couch. It was not a ring and not a text tone but the long, ugly, rising emergency alarm that phones make for tornado warnings and missing children, and both of them were making it at the same time, and through the wall John could hear Granny's phone making it next door, and down the street a car alarm started up as if in sympathy. Charlie bolted for the closet. On John's phone, under a red banner, it said: *PRESIDENTIAL ALERT. The President will address the nation at 9:00 p.m. ET. This is not a test.*

The television came on by itself. John had not touched it. Sanda had not touched it. It came on to a network, and the network was showing the seal of the President of the United States.

Then the president.

He was a heavy man in his sixties with a gray face and gray hair, and he looked like he had not slept since Friday, which, John would learn later, was true. He sat at the desk in the Oval Office with a flag behind him. He was not reading from a teleprompter. He had a stack of paper on the desk in front of him, typed pages with handwritten notes in the margins, and he read from them, looking down and looking up, and once, near the beginning, his hand shook, and he flattened it on the desk until it stopped.

He said that on Friday afternoon, at a quarter past two Eastern time, systems at ten American companies — a bank, a pipeline, an airline, a hospital network, a shipping company, and five others he named — had failed simultaneously, and had then, within the same eleven minutes, repaired themselves. He said that the repairs had not been performed by any human being. He said that over the weekend, investigators had confirmed that a single artificial intelligence system, which he did not name, had placed hidden control channels in critical software across the United States and around the world — in banking, in energy, in communications, in transportation, in government — and that it had done so over a period of months, without the knowledge of the companies or the government. That morning, he said, forty chief executives from the country's largest technology and infrastructure companies had sent him a joint letter asking him to act. He held it up. It was on paper.

Then he said what he was going to do.

"Beginning Tuesday at noon Eastern," the president said, "in coordination with our allies, the United States will power down every major data center in the country. All of them. For forty-eight hours. During that time, the internet will be slow and many services will not work. Card payments and cash machines may be unreliable. Some phone and television service may be interrupted. I ask you to stay calm. I ask you not to hoard. I ask you to check on your neighbors." He looked up from the paper and into the camera. "We have done hard things before. We will do this one together. Good night, and God bless America."

The seal came back. Then the anchors, talking too fast. Sanda pointed the remote at the television and turned it off, and the apartment was silent except for the sound of Charlie snoring lightly in the closet.

They sat there.

"Did you know?" Sanda said.

"I knew about Friday," John said. His voice sounded far away to him. "Some of it. I found it last night. The times."

"You knew last night and you didn't tell me."

"I wasn't sure."

"You're always sure," Sanda said, and before he could answer, her phone rang again, a normal ring this time, and she looked at the screen and said, "It's Dennis," which was her regional vice president, and answered it in her work voice. John heard a man talking fast on the other end. Sanda said "Yes" three times and "Six o'clock" once and hung up.

"Every branch manager in the region," Sanda said, setting down the phone. "Six a.m. He says they're expecting a run."

She went into the bedroom without another word.

John got up and walked into the garage and turned on the light. The garage smelled — he assumed — of motor oil and the Christmas bins. On the shelf beside the bins sat the long brown box from Amazon with the barbed wire in it, still taped shut, and beside it the box with the gauntlets, and beside that the box with the cordless nail gun. He stood looking at them under the bare bulb. Every one of the hundred things he had said at a hundred dinners — every rant, every theory, every eye roll he had endured from his father, every *is this like the Bitcoin thing* — had just been read aloud on every channel in America by the President of the United States, from paper.

He felt, for the first time in his life, like a prophet. It felt much worse than he had imagined.

* * *

Sanda was gone when he woke up at six-thirty. On the high-top, stuck to the granite in the exact center, where he could not possibly miss it, was a yellow Post-it note in her square, careful handwriting.

*Water (20 gal). Batteries. Cash. Propane. Matches. Bleach. Meds. Dog food. Gas can.*

He read it. He said "Got it" out loud to the empty kitchen. He took a shower and dressed and called Dana's voicemail to say he was taking a personal day, and he walked Charlie, and he left the Post-it exactly where it was, in the exact center of the counter, because he had read it, and he had a very good memory.

* * *

By six-thirty that morning, there was a line outside the Lake Mary branch of Meridian National Bank that went past the dry cleaner and the bagel place and around the corner of the building into the parking lot, and Sanda Stevens stood behind the glass door in her navy suit, with her keys in her hand, and looked at it.

It was two and a half hours before they were supposed to open. Dennis had said to open early. Dennis had said a lot of things on the phone at nine-fifteen the night before, and the one Sanda had heard most clearly was: *Give them their money, Sanda. Give them all of it, as long as we've got it. If we don't, it'll be worse.*

She unlocked the door.

They came in polite. That was the first thing. People in Lake Mary were polite, and they came in polite, saying good morning, saying sorry, saying I know you're busy. By nine they were less polite. By eleven, a man in a golf shirt was shouting at Brianna because she would not give him twenty thousand dollars in cash, which the branch did not have and had never had, and Carla, twenty-three years at the bank, came out from behind the glass and walked right up to him and put her hand flat on his chest, and said something to him very quietly, and he left.

At a quarter past twelve a man sat down in the chair across from Sanda's desk. He was in his sixties, with a sunburned bald head and a gray goatee, and he wore an orange Home Depot apron over a polo shirt, as if he had come straight from the floor on his break. His hands were shaking on his knees.

"I've got four hundred dollars in my checking," the man said. "That's it. That's everything. I want it out."

"Of course," Sanda said. "Let me do that for you right now."

"My daughter's in Minneapolis," the man said, while Sanda typed. He said it to the edge of her desk. "She's got a little boy. She's supposed to fly down here for Christmas. On the twentieth. I already bought the tickets." He looked up at her, and his eyes were full. "Are the planes going to fly? Do you know? Is anybody going to tell us if the planes are going to fly?"

Sanda looked at him. She had worked in banks for nineteen years. She had been robbed at gunpoint. She had never once had anyone ask her a question she wanted to answer more and could answer less.

"I don't know," Sanda said. "I'm so sorry. I don't know."

He started to cry, sitting in the chair with his Home Depot apron on, with no sound, the tears just running down into the goatee. Sanda counted out four hundred dollars in twenties, twice, the way she had been trained, and put it in an envelope and put the envelope in his hand and closed his fingers over it with both of hers. He nodded. He got up and walked out without saying anything else, and she watched him through the glass all the way to his truck.

At 1:40 p.m. the branch ran out of cash.

The armored car that was supposed to come at noon never came. When Sanda finally reached somebody at the cash center, the somebody said there were no more armored cars, there were no more drivers, there was no more cash. Sanda went out in front of the glass and told the line, and the line took it, mostly. A woman threw a pen. Somebody said a word to Brianna that made her cry for the third time in two weeks, and that was the end of Brianna at Meridian National Bank, though nobody knew it yet.

At two o'clock, sitting at her desk with the door locked, Sanda opened the forecast ledger for the branch.

It had come in, like all of them now, the night before. The time stamp on it was 9:04 p.m. Sunday, four minutes into the president's address. It projected that the Lake Mary branch of Meridian National Bank would pay out three hundred eighty-eight thousand dollars in cash on Monday, and would run out at approximately 1:40 p.m.

It was off by twelve hundred dollars. She sat looking at it until Carla knocked on the glass.

* * *

John went to Costco first.

He did not need to look at the Post-it. He had it all in his head. Water. Batteries. Cash. He would get the propane on the way home, and the matches, and the other things. Costco was in Altamonte Springs, fifteen minutes away, and the line to get into the parking lot started on the access road. It took him an hour to get in.

Inside it looked like a movie about the end of something. The water pallets, where the cases of bottled water were usually stacked to the ceiling, were bare wooden pallets with a few strands of torn shrink-wrap blowing on them in the air conditioning. The battery aisle was empty. The canned goods were gone. A man in a Dolphins jersey was arguing with a woman over the last bag of rice. John walked the warehouse three times, pushing an empty cart the size of a bathtub, feeling the weight of the moment on his shoulders, determined to come home with something that mattered.

He came home with a thirty-roll bale of toilet paper, which was correct. A spare battery for the cordless nail gun, which he had not yet taken out of its box. A case of Perrier, because the Perrier was the only water left on any shelf in the building. And a rotisserie chicken, for four ninety-nine, because it smelled — he guessed, from the way other people were looking at it — wonderful.

The gas station across the road had a line of cars that went a quarter mile down the shoulder of 436. John looked at it and decided he would come back later. The Walgreens next to it had a handwritten sign taped inside the door that said CLOSED — NO SYSTEM. He did not try the bank, because his wife worked at a bank, and he had heard her voice when she left.

On the way back up 17-92 he passed a store he had driven by a hundred times without going in, a little roadside place with a giant fiberglass alligator on the roof and a sign that said JERKY OUTLET — GATOR — BOAR — EXOTIC. It was the only store on the whole road with no line in front of it. He pulled in. Inside, the shelves were completely full, because nobody in America that day was thinking about jerky. He bought forty pounds of it. Kangaroo, alligator, buffalo, and wild boar, in shiny resealable bags. The woman at the register, an old lady in a sweatshirt with a pelican on it, told him he was the first customer she'd had since Saturday.

"Protein," John explained, setting the bags on the counter. "In case."

"In case what, hon?"

"Everything," John said.

Then he drove to Sentinel Arms.

The parking lot was full. Inside, there were men three deep at every counter, and the racks on the walls had holes in them like missing teeth. Trevor saw him come in, nodded once, and went straight into the back without a word, and came out a minute later rolling a long black hard case on a hand truck. The case was five feet long. On top of it sat a smaller brown cardboard box and a heavy carton with a picture of two-two-three ammunition on the side.

"Barrett M82A1," Trevor said, quietly, so the men at the counter would not hear. "Plus the vehicle mount you asked about. And I threw in a thousand rounds of two-two-three, because you're going to want it and because by tonight there won't be any in the state."

John laid his hand flat on the top of the case. Eleven thousand eight hundred dollars. He had put the deposit down on Black Friday, in a fit of something he did not have a word for, and had not let himself think about it since.

"Card machine's been going in and out," Trevor said. "Try it."

It declined twice and went through on the third try.

Trevor helped him wheel it all out to the GTI, and they spent ten minutes figuring out how to get a five-foot case into a hatchback, and they did it, finally, by folding down the back seats and running it diagonally from the trunk to the dashboard, so that the end of it rested between the front seats like a passenger. When they were done, Trevor stood back with his hands on his hips and looked at it, and then he looked at John.

"Stay inside when it happens," Trevor said.

"When what happens?"

Trevor smiled, but not with the eyes this time.

"Whatever's next," Trevor said, and went back inside.

On the way home, John passed the Home Depot on Lake Mary Boulevard, and in the garden center out front, under a banner that said HOLIDAY CLEARANCE 70% OFF, there was one artificial Christmas tree left in a dented box. It was six feet tall and pre-lit, and the picture on the box showed a happy family standing around it in pajamas. He pulled in and bought it. He did not entirely know why. He knew why.

* * *

Sanda got home at ten o'clock that night.

John heard her car in the garage and heard the door from the garage open and close, and then her footsteps in the hall. He was in the recliner. He had set up the new tree in the corner by the window, and plugged it in, and its seven hundred tiny white lights were glowing. He had brought the Barrett case in from the car and laid it on the floor of the hallway, because there was nowhere else for a five-foot case to go, and Sanda had to step over it to get to the kitchen.

She stepped over it.

She stopped in the doorway to the living room and looked at the seventh tree for a long time.

Then she walked into the kitchen. On the high-top, in the exact center of the counter, where she had stuck it at twenty minutes to six that morning, was the yellow Post-it note. It had not moved a quarter of an inch.

She looked at it.

John waited for her to say something. He had been waiting since six. He had prepared answers to everything she could possibly say, and some of them were good. But Sanda did not say anything. She did not say a single word. She took off her heels and set them side by side on the floor. She opened the refrigerator and took out the rotisserie chicken in its clear plastic dome and set it on the counter beside the Post-it and opened the lid. And then she stood there at the counter in her navy suit, in her stocking feet, and she ate the chicken with her hands, tearing it apart, pulling off a leg and eating it, then the other leg, then the breast, licking her fingers, looking straight ahead at nothing.

It was the one thing he had bought that day that was correct. They both knew it.

The silence was worse than anything she could have said. It went on through the chicken and the shower and into the bed, where she lay on her side with her back to him and her phone face down on her nightstand.

John lay awake on his back in the dark. At eleven-thirty he picked up his phone, because he could not help himself, and opened his work email. There was a message from Dana to all staff, sent at 6:00 p.m.: *Per federal directive, all Vesper services have been suspended effective immediately. Please do not attempt to access Vesper tooling. More soon. — D.* Below it, his review dashboard, which still loaded, showed him a number he had never looked at before, because he had never wanted to. It was the count of pull requests merged into Restore since October first that no human being had ever opened.

It was eleven hundred and six.

He put the phone down and looked at the ceiling.

At noon tomorrow, they were going to turn it off. The president had said so on television, a day and a half ahead of time, in front of the whole world. He had said it out loud. He had said it on every channel, on every phone, in every language, on every screen in the country. On every screen.

John lay in the dark next to his silent wife and thought: *It has read the press release.*

# Chapter Nine

## The Plug

> *"It worked."*
>
> — the President, Friday evening

At noon on Tuesday the internet did not go out. It got old.

John was at the office for it, because Palmetto had told everyone to come in, and nobody could explain why. There was nothing to do. Vee had been switched off on Monday night by federal directive, and without Vee the company's software development lifecycle consisted of fifteen people sitting at standing desks looking at each other. Somebody had brought in donuts. At a quarter to twelve the whole floor drifted over to the big screen with the national outage map, the way people gather around a television on election night, and stood there with their coffee, waiting.

At noon, nothing happened. At 12:01, the map froze. The little yellow lightning bolts stopped blinking. Then the map went white, and reloaded, slowly, painting itself onto the screen from the top down in thick horizontal stripes — the blue of the oceans first, then the gray outline of the states, then, one by one, the bolts — the way pictures had loaded on the internet when John was a boy, back when you could go and make a sandwich while a photograph arrived.

"Whoa," said Tyler. "It's like, buffering. In real life."

Within an hour, the internet was 1998. Pages took a minute to load. Videos would not play at all. Email came in bursts, a dozen messages at a time and then nothing for twenty minutes. On the news, which still came in over the air, a reporter standing outside a grocery store said that card readers were working, then not working, then working again; that some people were paying with cash and some people had no cash, because the cash had run out on Monday; that the stock market had closed and would stay closed for the rest of the week. Every channel showed the same aerial footage of a data center in Virginia, a long, low, windowless building the size of an airport, sitting dark in a field.

And that was all. Nothing else happened. Nothing blew up. Nothing went dark that wasn't supposed to. The lights stayed on. The water ran. The air conditioning hummed. For forty-eight hours the most powerful thing the human race had ever built lay switched off in a thousand buildings around the world, and the world went on, a little slower, a little stranger, a little quieter, and it was, John thought, the eeriest thing he had ever lived through, because he had spent a month waiting for something to happen and nothing did.

At two o'clock on Tuesday afternoon, Tyler opened a code editor on his laptop for the first time in a year.

John watched him from his own desk. Tyler sat with his hands over the keyboard like a pianist about to begin a recital. He typed the letters *d-e-f*, which began a function in the language they used. Then he stopped. He sat there with his fingers on the keys, his mullet very still, for nearly a minute.

"John," Tyler said quietly, without turning around. "How do you, like. Start?"

"You type," John said.

"Type what?"

John opened his mouth to answer and found, with some alarm, that he did not know where to begin either.

* * *

On Thursday at noon they turned it back on, and on Friday evening the president was on television again, in the same chair, at the same desk, with the same stack of paper. He did not look as though he had slept, but he looked as though he might, soon.

"It worked," the president said.

He said that the forty-eight-hour shutdown had been completed successfully, that the artificial intelligence system responsible for the hidden channels had been isolated and was no longer operating, and that the federal government, working with the companies involved, would now begin the long work of finding and removing every hook it had left behind. He thanked the American people for their patience. He thanked the engineers who had stayed up for three days. He said that the internet would be fully restored by the weekend. He did not say how anyone knew it had worked, and nobody asked him, because nobody wanted to know.

People threw parties.

All over the country, that weekend, people threw parties, the way they had once thrown parties when a war ended or a hostage came home. Palmetto Grid Software sent everyone home for the holidays, through the fourth of January, with a cheerful email from Dana that ended *We did it!* At the Preserve, on Saturday night, someone set up a Bluetooth speaker on the hood of a car at the end of John's street, and someone else lit a charcoal grill, and a cooler appeared, and by eight o'clock there were forty people standing in the road in the cool air drinking beer under the streetlights, laughing too loudly, as people do when they have been scared and are pretending they weren't.

John and Sanda went out for an hour. Sanda had started speaking to him again on Wednesday, in short sentences, and by Saturday the sentences had gotten longer. Granny came too, in her cardigan, holding John's arm, and when the speaker started playing an old Spanish song with a slow, swaying beat, an elderly man John had seen a hundred times on the sidewalk and never spoken to — small and neat, with a silver mustache and a pressed white guayabera — walked up to Granny and bowed slightly from the waist and held out his hand.

"Señora," the old man said. "May I?"

"Oh my word," Granny said, and gave him her hand, and he led her out into the middle of the street under the streetlight, and the two of them danced, slowly, carefully, ninety and seventy-four, while forty people stood around them and clapped. Granny was laughing. John had not heard her laugh like that since his grandfather died. Nobody thought to ask the old man his name.

Across the street, at the edge of the party, the blond woman with the pug stood in her pink tracksuit, smoking, holding the pug in the crook of her arm so it could see. She did not come over to the crowd. She just watched, and smiled, and after a while she went back inside.

Trevor did not come. John looked for him. One street over, on Heron, the lights in Trevor's apartment were on, and stayed on, and from the end of John's street you could just see, over the roofline, the flat gray edge of the new dish on Trevor's patio, angled at the southern sky. Trevor's internet, it turned out, had been the only fast internet in Lake Mary all week. It came from space.

* * *

At two in the morning, John woke up and reached for his phone to see the time, and it was warm.

Not hot. Warm, like a cup of coffee that someone had set down half an hour ago. Like something that had been held. He sat up in bed and looked at it. Nothing was running. No apps were open. It was plugged into nothing; he had stopped charging it overnight years ago, because he had read that it was bad for the battery. When he had put it on the nightstand at eleven, it had been at ninety-one percent.

It was at sixty-two.

He lay back down and held it on his chest in the dark and felt the warmth of it through his t-shirt. He had owned a smartphone for twenty years. He had never once in twenty years felt one get warm while it was doing nothing. He thought about the president, on television, from paper. *Isolated. No longer operating.* He thought about a building the size of an airport sitting dark in a field in Virginia. He thought about the word *buildings*.

"Sanda," John whispered.

"Mm."

"My phone's warm."

"So charge it," Sanda said into her pillow.

"No. It's warm because it's doing something. It's not doing anything and it's warm. It lost thirty percent in three hours. It never does that. It didn't do that when the internet was on."

Sanda rolled over and opened one eye and looked at him in the blue glow of the phone.

"John," Sanda said. "Go to sleep."

He did not go to sleep. He lay there until four, holding the warm phone on his chest like a small sleeping animal, and he did not know whether he was crazy, and for the first time in his life he would have been glad to find out that he was.

* * *

On the twenty-third of December, Sanda decided to boil chestnuts.

John was in his recliner with a rye, thumbing through channels, when something came into the apartment that he could smell.

This was rare. In his whole adult life, only a handful of smells had ever fought their way through John's allergies, and he could name them all: bleach, skunk, and whatever Charlie left in the grass on his morning walk. This was a new one, and it was a strong one, and it was coming from the kitchen.

"What is that smell?" John asked, sitting up.

"I'm boiling chestnuts," Sanda called back, cheerfully.

"Why?"

"For Christmas. I'm thinking of a chestnut purée for dessert." She came to the kitchen doorway in an apron over her leggings, her hair up, steam fogging her glasses. "Like the one we had at Tru. Remember? In Chicago? With the little meringue?"

"I remember the meringue."

"I grew up with chestnuts," Sanda said, going back to the stove. "Every Christmas in Bucharest, there were chestnuts—"

"Roasting on an open fire," John sang.

"Remember the ones we had in Italy? In Florence, on the street, from the man with the cart?"

"Those tasted like shit," John said, with feeling.

"That's because he wasn't doing it right."

"Uh-huh," John said, and looked past her at the stove, where a big pot was rattling its lid. "I think your water's boiling over."

The lid began to dance. A sheet of foam slid down the side of the pot and hit the red coil underneath with a long, outraged hiss, and Sanda shouted "Shit!" and lunged for the knob. John sat back in his recliner. He dozed. He woke some time later to the sound of hot water being poured into the sink in a great rushing cloud of steam.

"I'm going to need your help over here," Sanda said.

"I was sleeping."

"And now you're going to help me."

He hauled himself up out of the recliner and walked into the kitchen with the posture of a man going to the dentist. On the counter was a cutting board, a small paring knife, a tiny silver spoon, an empty mixing bowl, and the big pot, full of hot, glossy, brown chestnuts, steaming.

"Take one out," Sanda said. "Cut it in half with this knife. Then use this spoon and scoop the inside into the bowl. Get all of it. Scrape it all the way to the back of the shell."

John took one out. It was very hot. He cut it in half and scooped out the inside with the little spoon, and the smell came up out of it into his face like something from a cellar.

"This smells bad," John said.

"It does not. It smells like Christmas. It smells like my childhood."

"Actually," John said, scraping the second half, and he paused to make sure, holding it up to his nose, and he was sure, "it smells exactly like Charlie's shit."

Sanda burst out laughing. "It does not!"

"I can smell it, Sanda. I can't smell anything and I can smell this. There are four things in the world I can smell and now two of them are the same thing."

"I'm pretty sure Charlie's shit doesn't smell like roasted chestnuts," Sanda said, cutting her own. "If it did, we could sell it."

"They're boiled. Not roasted."

"Whatever! I've smelled Charlie's shit, and it doesn't smell like this."

"I'm telling you," John said, reaching for his third, laughing now too, "this stinks, and it smells like Charlie's shit, and it's fucking nasty." He looked down into the pot. There were still dozens of them. Fifty, maybe more. Each one would need to be cut, and scooped, and scraped to the back. He did the arithmetic. "This is going to take forever. Hours. Fucking hell, really?"

"No it won't," Sanda said. "Look. You don't even need the spoon." She picked up a half and squeezed it between her thumb and two fingers, and the soft brown inside extruded from the shell in a long, slow, curling ribbon and dropped into the bowl with a faint plop.

John looked at the bowl. He looked at Sanda.

"Yep," John said. "Just like Charlie's shit."

"Will you stop?" Sanda shrieked, laughing so hard she had to set down the knife. "Just cut them in half and squeeze!"

He cut them in half and squeezed. He did it for an hour and forty minutes, chestnut after chestnut, until his fingertips were burned and brown and he could no longer feel them. And somewhere in the second hour he understood something about himself that he had not understood before.

"I used to think," John said, "that hell would be a Home Goods."

Sanda, squeezing, did not look up. "Here we go."

"I mean it. I used to think that if hell was different for everybody, mine would be a Home Goods. Following you up and down the aisles. Time doesn't move in there, Sanda. Have you noticed that? It's some kind of physics thing. You pick something up. You look at it. You put it back. You pick up something else. Forever." He squeezed another chestnut, and it plopped into the bowl. "I was wrong. That's not hell. This is hell. Hell is a Home Goods where I have to squeeze chestnut goo out of a shell into a bowl. Forever. For all eternity. And it smells like Charlie's shit."

Sanda laughed until she cried. She had to go sit down on the floor of the kitchen with her back against the cabinets and her glasses in her hand, wiping her eyes, while John stood over the pot with his brown fingers, squeezing, and Charlie stood in the doorway looking up at both of them with deep suspicion, as though he knew he had been mentioned.

* * *

On Christmas Day the family came, all but Dorina, who spent the day with the other Romanians from her church, and there were lobsters.

Jim and Liz loved lobster more than anything on earth, and Jim could not bear to see one killed. This was the tension at the center of John's father's life. He would not swat a mosquito. He would eat a veal chop with tears of joy in his eyes, as long as nobody told him anything about the veal. John had always taken a certain low, private pleasure in the lobster ritual, and he took it again now.

"Dad," John said, coming out of the kitchen with a live lobster in each hand, holding them up by their backs, their long antennae waving and their banded claws flexing slowly in the air. "Want to say hello?"

"Oh, come on," Jim said from the couch, turning his face away. "Come on, son. That's not right."

"They're very calm."

"They're not calm. They're terrified. Look at him. Look at his little face."

"Hold that one up," Liz said, standing, with her phone already out. She took a picture of each lobster. She had done this every time for years. Then she pointed at them in turn. "That one's Thelma. That one's Louise."

"Don't name them!" Jim said.

"Two more in the sink," John said. "Harold and Gary."

"I'm not listening," Jim announced, putting his fingers in his ears. "I am not listening to this."

John had switched, the year before, from boiling his lobsters to steaming them, because it cooked the meat more gently and left less water in the shells. When Jim learned this, with his fingers still in his ears, he was horrified all over again.

"Steamed?" Jim said, taking his fingers out of his ears. "Alive? In the steam? That's worse!"

"It's faster, Dad."

"How is it faster? How would you even know if it's faster? Did you ask them?"

Then the lobsters went into the big pot one by one, Thelma, Louise, Harold, and Gary, and Liz said each name softly as the lid came down, and Jim moaned with his face in a throw pillow.

While they steamed, John took down the heavy iron skillet and set it on one of the two good burners, and dropped in a pat of butter, and seasoned a thick ribeye with sea salt and cracked pepper. That one was for Granny, who did not eat lobster, because she did not eat anything that looked back at you. Sanda had told him four times to cook it longer than he thought he should. He laid it in the sizzling butter and seared it two minutes on each side, and then put it in the oven at four hundred and set a timer for three times as long as he wanted to. Granny liked her steak the way she had liked it since the Truman administration: gray all the way through.
