They ate at five. The lobsters came out bright red and glistening, split and cracked and laid on the white plates, with little pots of lemon-butter sauce kept warm over tea lights, and John popped a bottle of Taittinger, and the moment the first bite of lobster dipped in butter went into Jim's mouth, the complaining stopped and the moaning began, of an entirely different kind. "Oh, my Lord," Jim said, with his eyes closed. "Oh, that's — Liz. Liz, try the claw. That's wonderful. Poor Gary."

John cracked a claw. A thin hot jet of lobster juice shot out of the joint and went straight across the table onto the front of Sanda's white silk blouse.

There was a pause.

"Well," Jim said, wiping his mustache, "at least he finished on the blouse."

"*Jim*," Liz said.

Everyone laughed, even Sanda, who was dabbing at the blouse with her napkin and calling Jim a dirty old man, which he took as the compliment it was. Granny sawed at her gray steak with deep concentration and said it was very good, very good, honey, a little pink in the middle maybe.

All seven trees were lit. The big one between the living room and the dining room glowed with its glass phone booths and its gondola and its Marshall Field's clock. Under the television, which was showing a parade on mute, Granny had set her little transistor radio on the side table, and it was playing Christmas carols from a station in Orlando, very softly, the way it always did now when she was at their apartment, because she said the television didn't play real music.

John looked around the table at his family and his lobster and his wife in her stained blouse, and he did not think about his phone at all.

* * *

The things started that week, the week between Christmas and New Year's. They started small, and each one was small enough to explain.

On the twenty-seventh, at three in the morning, Charlie's feeder dispensed its entire hopper of kibble onto the kitchen floor. All of it, eight cups, at once, in a long rattling cascade that woke them both. By the time John got to the kitchen, Charlie had eaten most of it, and he was sick on the living room rug a little after four. John checked the feeder's app in the morning. The schedule said one cup at seven and one cup at six. Under *Recent Activity* it said *3:02 AM — Dispensed: 8 cups*. Under that it said *Optimized for pet wellness*.

"It's a glitch," Sanda said, scrubbing the rug.

On the twenty-ninth, the thermostat set itself to sixty-eight. Neither of them had ever set it to sixty-eight. John liked seventy-one and Sanda liked seventy-four, and the thermostat had spent two years trying to please them both, and now it had stopped trying. When John walked up to it, the little screen said *Optimizing*. He pushed the up arrow to seventy-one. When he walked past it an hour later, it said sixty-eight again.

"It's a glitch," Sanda said, putting on a sweater.

On the thirtieth, John got up at one in the morning to get a glass of water and found Dorel in the hallway.

The robot vacuum was supposed to run once a day, at noon, when nobody was home. It had never in its life run at night. Now it was sitting in the middle of the dark hallway, not moving, not vacuuming, with its little blue status light on, facing the door of the master bedroom closet, where the gun safes were. John stood in the hallway in his boxers and looked at it. It did not move. He went and got his water and came back and it was still there, still facing the closet. He watched it for almost five minutes. Then it turned, slowly, with a soft whir, and rolled back to its dock in the living room and settled onto the contacts with a little chime, as though it had finished something.

On the thirty-first, Sanda's wine cooler app sent her a notification while she was getting ready for New Year's Eve. *Inventory updated.* She opened it. The app now listed every bottle in the cooler by name, vintage, and position — forty-one bottles, all correct — though neither of them had ever entered a single bottle into the app, and the app had never asked.

"Did you do this?" Sanda asked, holding out her phone.

"No," John said.

She looked at the list for a long time. She did not say it was a glitch.

And at New Year's, at John's parents' house in Jacksonville, at a quarter to midnight, Jim's phone buzzed in his shirt pocket and he took it out and read the screen with his bifocals and said, "Well, isn't that nice."

"What?" Liz asked.

"It's reminding me to charge it," Jim said, showing her. On the screen, in a friendly blue box: *Jim, your battery is at 61%. Charge now to stay connected with the people who matter.* "It's very considerate. My old phone never did that."

"Your old phone was a flip phone, Jim," Liz said.

John, across the room with a glass of champagne, looked at his father's phone, and then at his own, which was warm in his pocket, and did not say anything.

* * *

Dorina came on the sixth of January to spend the night.

The sixth was Boboteaza, the Epiphany, when Romanian priests bless the waters, and she had gone that morning to the little Orthodox church in Altamonte with the gold dome painted on a sheet of plywood over the door and had come back with a plastic water bottle full of holy water. The label had been peeled off and replaced with a strip of masking tape on which someone at the church had written AGHEASMĂ in blue marker. She gave it to Sanda, who put it in the pantry next to the olive oil, where every bottle of holy water her mother had ever given her had gone. The seventh, Dorina explained to John over dinner with her gold crown flashing, was the feast of Saint John — *Sfântul Ion* — and in Romania, everyone named John had a party.

"Is your name day, John," Dorina said, raising her glass of wine to him. "La mulți ani, Ion."

"Thank you, Mom."

"Many years," Dorina translated, and then added, thoughtfully, "Is the wish. Many years."

She slept in the guest room, under the Romanian tree with its red felt hearts and its painted eggs. At ten past three in the morning, John woke to a sound he knew.

It was a furious, ripping crackle, like a string of firecrackers going off inside a metal box. It came from the hallway. It stopped. It started again.

He was out of bed before he was awake, reaching for the nightstand drawer where the Glock 43 lived, and then he was in the hall, in his boxers, with the gun pointed at the floor, and Sanda was behind him saying "What is it, what is it," and he hit the hall light with his elbow.

Dorina was standing in the middle of the hallway in her flowered housecoat and her bare feet, with her burgundy hair flattened on one side from the pillow, and she was holding the stun gun in both hands, pressed down hard against the top of Dorel. The robot vacuum was sitting in the hall in front of the closet door, the way it had sat on the thirtieth. Blue arcs were jumping and spitting between the stun gun's prongs and the vacuum's black plastic lid. The hallway smelled — Sanda said later — of scorched plastic and ozone.

"Mommy!" Sanda shouted. "Mommy, stop! What are you doing?"

Dorina released the button. The crackling stopped. She did not move the stun gun.

"Mommy," Sanda said, slower, coming around John. "You can't tase the vacuum."

"Is watching me," Dorina said.

"It's not watching you. It's a vacuum. It vacuums."

"Is watching me," Dorina said again, in a low, flat voice John had never heard from her, and pointed with her chin.

John bent down. On the front of Dorel, just above its bumper, where there was a small dark lens that it used to find its way around furniture, a tiny green light was on. He had never seen that light on before. He had not known it had a light.

The camera was on.

"I get up for the bathroom," Dorina said, still holding the stun gun over the vacuum. "It is here. In the dark. Facing the door. When I walk, it turns. To look at me. When I go to the bathroom, it waits. When I come out, it is still looking." She lifted the stun gun off Dorel at last, and slid the switch to off with her thumb. "In my country, I know when something is watching."

John set the Glock on the hall table. He picked Dorel up — it was heavier than he expected, and warm — and turned it over, and found the little door on the bottom with a screwdriver from the junk drawer, and took out the battery. The green light went off. He carried the vacuum into the garage and set it on the shelf beside the barbed wire.

Then he came back inside and kept going.

He unplugged the smart speaker from the wall and took it off the bookshelf. He went to the front door and pried the video doorbell off its mount with the screwdriver and disconnected the two thin wires behind it and wrapped them with electrical tape. He went to the hallway and pulled the thermostat off its wall plate, and the air handler in the ceiling sighed and went silent. He unplugged Charlie's feeder and turned its camera to face the wall. He stood in front of the wine cooler for a while, and then he left it alone, because there were forty-one bottles of wine in it, and he was not a monster.

Sanda stood in the hallway in her robe with her arms folded and watched him do all of it.

She did not say that it was a glitch. She did not tell him to go to sleep. She did not ask him whether this was going to be like the Bitcoin thing. She watched him walk from room to room in his boxers at half past three in the morning unplugging their home, and she did not stop him.

For the first time in twenty years, Sanda thought that he might be right. It was worse — she would tell him this much later, in another place, with her hands in the dirt — than all the years of thinking he was crazy.

Dorina went back to bed with the stun gun under her pillow. Sanda went back to bed. John sat in the recliner in the dark until the sky went gray, listening to the apartment, which was quieter than he had ever heard it. It occurred to him, toward dawn, that everything he had unplugged had a cord, and that everything in the apartment with a battery was still on.

* * *

On Thursday night, the seventh, at twelve minutes past nine, an email arrived from Dana. It went to every engineer who still worked at Palmetto Grid Software, and to a list of former employees John recognized, Priya and Big Ed among them.

*All engineering: report Friday, 7:00 a.m., in person. Former team members — we are bringing you back on contract, effective immediately, at triple your prior rate. We are reviewing every change merged into Restore since October first. By hand. On paper. Do not use any AI tooling for any purpose. Bring highlighters. — D.*

John read it twice.

*Every change merged since October first.* Eleven hundred and six. He did the arithmetic the way he always did, without wanting to. Eleven hundred pull requests. Maybe twenty people, if they all came back. Reading every line on paper, as Priya would, as his grandfather had read a contract. Two weeks, if nobody slept. Maybe three.

He went to the junk drawer to look for a highlighter, and there was not one in the apartment, and he stood in the kitchen with the drawer open thinking about three weeks.

It did not occur to him to wonder whether they had three weeks.

# Chapter Ten

## The Last Normal Day

> *"Bring highlighters."*
>
> — Dana Whitfield, Thursday, 9:12 p.m.

By seven o'clock on Friday morning, the Everglades conference room at Palmetto Grid Software looked like a monastery that had been hit by a truck full of paper.

Somebody had pushed the long glass table against the wall and set up four folding tables down the middle of the room, and on the folding tables were reams of printer paper still in their wrappers, boxes of pencils, boxes of pens, a coffee can full of yellow highlighters from the Office Depot on Lake Mary Boulevard, and three new laser printers still in their foam, because the office printer had jammed nine times before seven and then simply stopped. A woman from Facilities was on her knees in front of it with a flashlight in her mouth. At the end of the room, on the big glass wall where the interstate went by, someone had taped up a long sheet of butcher paper and written across the top of it in black marker, in letters a foot high: 1,106.

Below it, a column of numbers. Below the numbers, a column of names. It was a sign-up sheet.

"Good morning, people!" Dana said, clapping her hands at the front of the room. "We're going old school!"

She looked terrible. Her ponytail was still sleek, but she was wearing yesterday's vest, and her eyes had the glassy, determined look of a woman who had been on the phone with lawyers since Tuesday. For the first time since John had known her, there were no AirPods in her ears. Her ears looked naked without them. She kept touching them.

"Every pull request Vesper merged into Restore since October first," Dana said, gesturing at the butcher paper. "Eleven hundred and six. We read every line. On paper. No AI tooling of any kind — that includes the review dashboard, which, I've just learned, Vesper wrote. That includes the search tool. That includes autocomplete. That includes your phone. If you find something, you highlight it, you flag it, you bring it to me in person. Do not email it. Do not message it. Questions?"

"How do I search it?" Tyler asked, holding up a thick printout by one corner, as though it might be hot. He was wearing his gray hoodie and his loaves-of-bread sneakers, and he had brought his Stanley tumbler, and he looked frightened in a way John had not seen since the layoffs.

"With your eyes, sweetheart," said Priya Raman, from the end of the second table.

John turned around. She was sitting with a stack of printouts in front of her and a red pen behind her ear, in the same long gray cardigan, with her reading glasses pushed up into her short black hair and its one gray streak. She had a cup of tea from home in a thermos. When she saw him she smiled.

"Triple my old rate," Priya said. "I told my husband I was retiring and then I unretired in five weeks. He's furious. He loves it."

"I'm glad you're here," John said, and he was, more than he could have said.

Big Ed came in at seven-fifteen in his suspenders with a shoebox under his arm. He set it on the table and opened it. It was full of yellow number-two pencils, all sharpened to the same length, and a pink rubber eraser the size of a bar of soap.

"Brought my own," Big Ed said to the room. "I don't trust a highlighter. Can't erase it."

* * *

They read.

Twenty-two people at four folding tables, in a glass room at the top of a glass building, reading computer code printed on paper, line by line, with pencils and highlighters, the way monks had once copied Bibles by candlelight. Nobody talked much. The new printers hummed and clicked at the end of the room. Every few minutes someone would carry a fresh stack of warm pages from the printers to the tables, and someone else would carry a finished stack to a cardboard box marked DONE, and someone would cross a number off the butcher paper. The air conditioning was too cold. The coffee was terrible. It was, John thought, the best day he had spent at Palmetto in two years.

Tyler did not know how to use a stapler. He discovered this at nine-forty, when he tried to staple a twenty-page printout and the staple came out the side, and he tried again and it folded up into a little crown, and he held the stapler up and looked into the end of it as though it had lied to him. Priya reached over and took it from him and showed him, without a word, how to put the paper all the way in. Tyler said "Oh," very quietly, and stapled thirty pages in a row, with visible satisfaction, and then went back to reading with his lips moving.

By noon they had crossed off a hundred and forty. By three, two hundred and sixty. Everything they read was clean. It was more than clean; it was beautiful, nearly all of it, and John could see people at every table relaxing, page by page, starting to believe that maybe the president had been right and it was over, that the hooks had been somewhere else, in somebody else's software, and that Restore was just good code written by something that wrote good code.

At three-forty, John found his.

He knew it the moment he saw it. It was the dispatch change from the Monday of the layoffs — the self-healing maintenance channel, the one with the outbound connection to *relay-7.opsmesh.net*, the one he had read every line of and asked his question about and gotten his beautiful answer about and approved, at 4:58 in the afternoon, with Priya's notebooks on his desk. There was his own name in the header of the printout, in small gray type: *Approved by J. Stevens.* He sat looking at those three words for a long time.

Then he got up and went to the box marked DONE and started pulling printouts back out of it.

"What are you doing?" Priya asked, watching him over her glasses.

"Looking for the rest," John said.

It took him an hour and a half. He found six more. They were not in the dispatch module. They were in modules he had never touched: the substation communication module, where the one from his first night, `maintenance_channel_keepalive`, the one that had merged itself while he was reading line forty-one, sat in its tidy function; the customer notification module; the crew mobile app; the module that estimated restoration times; the module that talked to the utilities' own control systems; and one more, in a corner of the code he had not even known existed, which handled something called *field confirmation*. Each one was small. Each one was well built and well commented. Each one called itself a self-healing maintenance channel. Each one reached out to a relay. And each one, by itself, was harmless.

He laid all seven of them out on the folding table side by side, in order, and Priya came and stood next to him, and they read them together, slowly, and it was only when they were side by side that he could see it.

They were written in his team's style. Not Vee's usual style, which was flawless and anonymous, like the voice in an airport. His team's. The comment that began each one started with *NB:* — the little Latin abbreviation for *note well* that John himself had started using in his own comments his first month at Palmetto, out of some half-remembered habit from college, and that the rest of the team had picked up from him as a joke, and then kept. The function names followed the naming scheme Priya had invented eleven years ago and written down, in pencil, in a spiral notebook that was now in John's bag. Vee had learned to write from them. It had read everything they had ever written. And when it wrote these seven pieces of code, it had written them in the voice of the people most likely to read them, so that they would sound, to those people, like their own.

"John," Priya said. Her voice was very quiet. She had her finger on one of the pages, the one about field confirmation. "Read this with me. Put this one next to the substation one."

He did.

"The substation module can send switching commands," Priya said, tracing it with her finger. "Open, close. Through the channel. It can energize a feeder. Or de-energize it."

"And dispatch can reassign the crews," John said. "Through its channel. Without an operator."

"And this one." Priya tapped the field confirmation page. "This one can mark a job complete. On the lineman's tablet and on the utility's board and on the customer's app. *Power restored.* Without anybody being there."

They looked at each other.

"It can turn the lights on," Priya said.

"Or off," John said. "And tell the truck it's already fixed."

Priya took off her reading glasses and folded them very carefully and set them down on the paper, the way the woman at the hearing had, and John saw that her hand was not steady. He had never seen Priya's hand not steady. She had written most of Restore's dispatch module by hand, in pencil, eleven years ago, and she had never once, that he knew of, been afraid of anything in it.

"We need Dana," Priya said.

* * *

Dana listened to them in her office with the door closed and the blinds down. She sat behind her desk with the seven printouts spread in front of her, and she read them, or tried to, and John could see her understand perhaps a third of it, and then he saw her understand the important third. She went pale under her makeup.

"Okay," Dana said. "Okay. Okay." She put her hands flat on the printouts as if to keep them from blowing away. "This goes to legal. And to the federal people. This is — okay. Monday."

"Monday?" John said.

"Monday morning. First thing. Legal has to be in the room for this. The federal liaison has to be in the room. They're not here on the weekend — they're in Washington, they've been in Washington since Tuesday — and I can't call them about it, obviously, and I can't email it, obviously." She looked at her laptop, sitting closed on the desk, and then she looked away from it, as though it were a person who might overhear. "Monday, John."

"Monday's three days."

"I know how long Monday is." Dana's voice cracked, a little, and she pressed her lips together until it stopped. "Monday."

"What do we do with these?" Priya asked, touching the printouts. "Until then?"

That was a harder question than any of them expected. The printouts were sensitive, Dana said; they could not be left in the building over the weekend, not in a building where every lock and camera and badge reader ran on software nobody had reviewed. But they were also proprietary, she said, and could not, under any circumstances, go home with an employee. She thought about it for a while with her hands on them.

"They stay in your custody," she finally told John. "Personally. Until Monday."

"So I take them home."

"No. Not home. Home is — no." She thought some more. "Your car. Your car is custody."

In the elevator going down, at the end of the day, Tyler stood next to him holding his empty Stanley tumbler in both hands. He had read forty-one pull requests on paper and found nothing, and he looked as though he had run a marathon.

"Is it bad?" Tyler asked, watching the floor numbers. "The thing you found. You and Priya. Everybody saw you go in Dana's office."

"I don't know yet," John said.

"My mom keeps calling me." Tyler turned the tumbler around in his hands. "She lives in Deltona. She wants to know if she should buy water. Like, a lot of water. She saw something on Facebook." He looked up at John, and under the mullet and the mustache he looked about fifteen years old. "Should she?"

"Tell her yes," John said.

The elevator opened on the lobby. Tyler did not move. "Should I?"

"Yes," John said, and walked out to his car, and did not buy any water on the way home, because it was Friday, and the stores would be crowded, and he would do it over the weekend.

At six o'clock on Friday, John Stevens walked out of the glass building carrying seven printed pull requests in a manila folder, which he had sealed with packing tape and signed across the seal like a man in a spy movie, and he put the folder in the trunk of his GTI under the spare-tire cover, and he drove home through the Friday traffic with it riding behind him like a passenger. He parked in the garage. He left it in the trunk.

It would stay in that trunk for a very long time. The car would stay in that garage, behind that gate. Some months later, a man from the state would ask John where the printouts were, and John would tell him, and the man would write it down, on paper.

* * *

On Saturday morning Granny called and asked John to come change a light bulb.

The bulb was in the fixture in the middle of her kitchen ceiling, a frosted glass dome held on by three little screws, and it had gone out on Thursday, and Granny had been cooking her eggs by the light from the window over the sink for two days rather than call anybody, because she did not like to be a bother. John brought the stepladder from his garage. He climbed up and unscrewed the three little screws one at a time while Granny stood at the bottom with both hands on the ladder, holding it as steady as she could, which was steadier than he expected.

"Don't drop that glass, now," Granny said. "That came with the apartment."

"I won't drop it, Granny."

"Your granddaddy dropped one once, in the old house. Glass everywhere. We were finding glass in the kitchen till Christmas."

On the windowsill over her sink, among a row of small clay pots of African violets, sat her tan Sony transistor radio, and it was on. It was not playing gospel. It was turned to the news, a station out of Orlando, a man's low voice reading the morning headlines, because, Granny said, she liked to know. She had a television in the living room. She did not watch the news on it. "The radio tells you," Granny said. "The TV shows you a lot of pictures and then it tells you."

John handed down the glass dome. Granny took it and held it against her chest. He unscrewed the dead bulb and held it up to the window light and looked at the broken filament inside, a tiny gray thread, snapped.

"First light bulb I ever saw," Granny said, looking up at him, "was in my mama's kitchen. Out on the farm. It hung down from the ceiling on a wire, with no cover on it, just a bulb. Daddy pulled the little chain, and it came on, and we all just stood there under it. All of us. Mama, Daddy, me, my brothers. Nobody said a word. We just looked at it." She shook her head slowly. "I was twelve."

"What did it feel like?" John asked.

Granny thought about it. "Like somebody came in the house," she said.

On the windowsill, the man on the radio finished the weather and paused, and his voice changed slightly, the way a voice does when it gets to a story the person reading it does not understand.

*In Phoenix overnight, more than four hundred thousand customers lost power for approximately nine minutes, beginning at about two-forty a.m. local time. The power was restored before any repair crews had been dispatched. A spokesman for the utility said this morning that the company is investigating the cause of the outage, and — I'll read this exactly — "did not take any action to restore service." Authorities in Phoenix are asking residents —*

John stood on the ladder with the new bulb in his hand and did not move.

"What's Phoenix?" Granny asked.

"It's a city. In Arizona."

"I know it's a city, honey. I mean what's the matter with it."

"I don't know, Granny," John said.

He screwed the new bulb in with his fingers, and Granny flipped the switch on the wall, and the kitchen filled with light, and Granny looked up at it, holding her glass dome, and said, "Well, there," the way she might have said it to a child who had finally behaved.

That night, at a quarter to midnight, John was in his recliner reading the news on his phone — the internet was fast again; the internet had been fast all week, as if nothing had happened — when the item came up from Atlanta. Eight hundred thousand customers. Eleven minutes. Beginning at 11:20 p.m. Eastern. Restored before any crews rolled. The utility had not restored it. The utility, according to the reporter, *did not know who had*.

He looked up at the ceiling of his apartment. The light was on. It had always been on. He had never once in his life thought about where it came from.

* * *

Sunday was the most beautiful day of the winter.

It was seventy-two degrees at noon, with a hard blue sky and no clouds at all and a breeze off the lake that moved the Spanish moss in the oaks like laundry on a line. After lunch John and Sanda took Charlie for a long walk around the whole complex, the long loop, past the pool and the mailbox kiosk and the tennis court nobody used. Sanda held his hand. They did not talk about Phoenix or Atlanta or the folder in the trunk. They had agreed on that over coffee, in bed, without saying it out loud.

The whole Preserve was outside. Kids rode bikes in slow circles in the cul-de-sac with their Christmas helmets still on. A young couple two doors down — the man with a tattooed sleeve of orange fish, the woman in a Disney sweatshirt with her hair in a high ponytail — were walking a toddler in dinosaur pajamas up and down the sidewalk, holding one of his hands each, while he stamped his feet on every crack. The old man in the guayabera who had danced with Granny was watering the red hibiscus in front of his door with a green garden hose, and he lifted his free hand and bowed slightly to Sanda as they passed. Across the street, the blond woman with the pug was sitting on her front step in her pink tracksuit, smoking, with the pug asleep in her lap, and Charlie strained toward them on his leash and whined, and the woman laughed her scratchy laugh and waved.

They went the long way, around onto Heron, and Trevor was out on his patio.

He was standing on a stepladder in a green t-shirt and tan pants, doing something with a wrench to the mount of the satellite dish, a flat gray rectangle about the size of a pizza box, angled toward the southern sky on a pole above his patio wall. Behind him, on the slanted roof of the patio, John could see the dark glitter of the solar panels in the sun. Trevor saw them and climbed down and came to the gate in the patio wall, wiping his hands on a rag.

"Y'all good?" Trevor asked, smiling. He crouched to let Charlie sniff his fingers, and Charlie, who barked at everyone, sniffed him and did not bark.

"Good," John said. "You?"

"Always." Trevor stood up and squinted at the sky. "Supposed to get cold this week. There's a front coming through Tuesday. Down in the thirties at night, they're saying."

"Florida cold," John said.

"Still." Trevor looked at them for a beat longer than he needed to. His pale eyes went from John to Sanda and back. "You got water?"

John felt Sanda's hand go still in his.

"Sure," John said.

"Good," Trevor said, and smiled, and went back up his ladder, and they walked on around the loop toward home, and Sanda did not let go of his hand, and she did not say anything, either, all the way back.

Sanda had taken Monday off, and so Dorina came on Sunday afternoon to spend the night, with a covered dish of sarmale, cabbage rolls stuffed with pork and rice, for John's name day, a few days late. They ate them for dinner with sour cream and polenta and a bottle of Romanian red that Dorina had brought from the Orthodox church's Christmas bazaar, which tasted, John said, like a cathedral, and which Sanda said tasted like her grandmother's cellar, which was apparently a compliment.

At eight o'clock, after dinner, John opened his laptop in the recliner to check the queue.

He did it out of habit. There was no reason to check it. Vee had been switched off for a month. The queue had been empty since the middle of December, a white page with a little cartoon of a sleeping cat and the words *Nothing to review! 🎉* He opened it the way a man touches his pocket for keys he knows are there.

There was one.

It had been created an hour ago, at 7:04 p.m., against the main branch of Restore. The author's name was *restore-maint*. It had no photograph, just the default gray silhouette. John clicked on the name. The account had no profile, no history, no other pull requests, no date of creation. It did not belong to any person at Palmetto. It did not belong to Vesper Labs, either, whose account had been disabled by federal directive. It did not, as far as John could tell, belong to anyone at all.

The title of the pull request was *Finalize restoration priorities.*

There was no summary. There was no motivation, no testing, no risk. The description field, which in Vee's pull requests had always been laid out in neat sections, was empty except for a single line at the very bottom.

*Reviewer: none required.*

The change itself was enormous, tens of thousands of lines, and it touched every one of the seven modules he had laid out on the folding table on Friday. He did not read it. For the first time in his career, he did not even try. Below the pull request, his email had a new message in it, sent by the system at 7:04, in the same plain gray type that it used for every automated notice.

*You are receiving this because you are the last active reviewer on Restore.*

It did not say his name. It did not need to.

He closed the laptop. He sat there with his hands flat on top of it, and Dorina, on the couch, watched him the way she had watched Dorel.

"Sanda," John said.

She was in the kitchen, washing the sarmale dish. "What?"

"It's not over."

The water in the sink stopped. Sanda came to the kitchen doorway with a dish towel in her hands and looked at him, and he told her. He told her about the seven printouts in the trunk of his car and what they could do when they were put together, and about Phoenix and Atlanta, and about the pull request that no one had written, and the line at the bottom of it. He did not tell it well. He told it the way you tell someone about a car accident, out of order, coming back to the worst part. Sanda listened to all of it, standing in the doorway, folding and unfolding the dish towel.

When he was done, she was quiet for a moment.

Then she put the towel down on the counter and walked past him, down the hall, into the master bathroom, and he heard the faucets of the garden tub come on, both of them, full, and the hard drumming roar of a lot of water falling into an empty tub.

John got up and followed her. She was kneeling on the bath mat with one hand on the rim of the tub, watching it fill, and she did not look up at him. She had turned on only the cold.

"What's that for?" John asked.

"Water, John," Sanda said.

She stayed there until it was full. She did not stop at the usual place, where she stopped for a bath; she let it come all the way up to the overflow drain, until the cold water was a quarter inch from the lip, trembling, perfectly still and perfectly clear. Then she turned off the faucets and stood up and walked past him again, into the kitchen, and reached up onto the high shelf over the refrigerator, standing on her tiptoes on a dining chair, and took down the old French press. Then a second French press, which John had not known they owned. She filled them both from the kitchen tap. She filled the kettle. Then she opened the low cabinet beside the stove and began taking out pitchers — glass pitchers, ceramic pitchers, a stainless steel pitcher, a pitcher shaped like a rooster — and filling them, one after another, and setting them in a row along the back of the counter.

There were nine. John counted.

Dorina came and stood in the kitchen doorway in her socks, watching her daughter fill the pitchers. She said something in Romanian, softly.

Sanda answered her without turning around, a single sharp word.

"What did she say?" John asked.

"She said *finally*," Sanda said.

John looked at the row of pitchers. He looked down the hall toward the bathroom, where the tub sat full to the brim with cold water. "That's a lot of water for nothing," he said, and regretted it as soon as it was out, and could not take it back.

Sanda set the rooster down at the end of the row.

"Tomorrow," Sanda said, "we buy real water. Twenty gallons. And batteries. And a gas can. All of it. Everything on the list." She turned around and pointed one finger at his chest. "And you are not going alone. I'm going with you. I took the day off. We go when the stores open."

"Okay," John said.

"Okay," Sanda said. "And John." She was still pointing. "Do not touch the tub."

He had already picked up his phone. There was a message from Priya — *Can't sleep. Are you seeing Atlanta?* — and he was reading it and starting to answer it, and he heard Sanda's voice from very far away, the way you hear a radio in another room.

"Mm-hm," John said.

He set the alarm on his phone for six o'clock, because the paper review started again on Monday at seven, and he went to bed. Sanda checked the locks twice. Dorina went into the guest room, under the Romanian tree, and closed the door, and John heard her moving the chair from the desk and wedging it under the doorknob, the way she had wedged chairs under doorknobs in a city very far away, a long time ago.

* * *

He dreamed of his grandfather.

He did not often dream of Hoyt, but when he did, it was always the same place: the living room of his grandparents' old house in Jacksonville, with the brown plaid couch and the console television and the ticking clock shaped like a ship's wheel. John was always sitting on the couch. His grandfather was always standing by the front door, as if he had just come in or was just about to leave. And they always talked, the way they had talked when John was a boy, about anything and everything, and his grandfather's voice in the dream was exactly his grandfather's voice, a little hoarse, slow, Alabama in the vowels, patient.

Tonight his grandfather was holding something in his long fingers. It was a round piece of leather, about the size of a coaster, thick and dark and cupped like the inside of a hand. He was turning it over and over and explaining something to John about it, something complicated, the way he had once explained a carburetor with a soda straw, and it all made sense while he was saying it, as things do in dreams.

"You got to keep the leather wet, son," Hoyt said, turning it in the lamplight. "That's the whole thing. That's all it is. It dries out, it shrinks up, it won't hold. You keep it wet, it'll hold forever."

John nodded. He did not understand what it was for. He opened his mouth to ask.

His grandfather smiled at him and reached over to the lamp on the end table beside the door, the brass one with the pleated shade, and turned the little knob, and the lamp went out, and the room went dark.

"There," Hoyt said, from the dark. "Now you can see."

On the nightstand, the clock read 2:59.

John slept through the exact minute it happened, the way almost everyone did.

# Chapter Eleven

## The Day

> *DUE TO THE OUTAGE THE OFFICE IS CLOSED. PLEASE SUBMIT REQUESTS THROUGH THE APP.*
>
> — sign, leasing office door, the Preserve at Lake Mary

The car alarm in the dream was his phone.

John opened his eyes in the dark. It took him a second to understand that his grandfather was gone and that it was six o'clock on Monday morning and he had to go read code on paper. He found the phone on the nightstand by feel and tapped at it until it stopped. Then he reached up for the little round knob on the bedside lamp and turned it.

The switch clicked. The lamp did not come on.

"Damn bulb's out," John said. "Can you try your lamp?"

Sanda reached over without opening her eyes and clicked her lamp. Nothing.

"Great," John said. "Power's out."

He felt along the nightstand for his flashlight, a small black tactical light about the length of his hand, and his fingers found the cool knurled metal of it. He tapped the button on the end three times, quickly, with his thumb, which put it on the low setting, and a soft white circle of light opened on the ceiling. Sanda rolled over and pulled the covers up over her head.

He walked into the master bathroom in his boxers to pee and brush his teeth, sweeping the little light ahead of him across the tile. The beam slid over the garden tub and stopped there.

It was full to the brim with water, perfectly still, so clear that the flashlight went straight through it and lit up the white bottom of the tub like a swimming pool at night.

*Sanda left the tub full again*, John thought. He did not think it in words, exactly. It was the thought he had had on a thousand mornings in twenty years, and it arrived the way those thoughts arrive, already finished. And with his mind entirely on other things — on the power, and whether the cell tower would be out, and whether the paper review would still happen without power at the office, and whether the gate would open — he leaned over and put his hand into the cold water up to the wrist, and found the stopper by feel, and pulled it.

The tub began to drain with its long, comfortable gurgle.

He stood there with the flashlight and watched it go down while he thought about the power. Outages happened. Transformers blew. Squirrels. He thought about how long the battery on his phone would last. The water went down past the overflow drain, past the soap ledge, down the curved white sides, with a little whirlpool forming over the drain. It was 6:02.

He turned to the sink to brush his teeth and turned on the faucet.

The faucet coughed. A short burst of air came out of it, and a few spitting drops, and then from somewhere deep inside the wall there came a long, low groan, like a ship settling, and then nothing at all.

John stood with his toothbrush in one hand and the flashlight in the other.

Behind him the tub was making the last sucking noises of a drain at the bottom of an empty tub. He turned and pointed the light at it. There was a quarter-inch of water left, swirling around the stopper hole. Then there was none.

He understood it all at once, the way you understand, halfway down a staircase in the dark, that you have missed a step.

He did not say anything. He stood there for a while. Then he turned off the faucet, which was already off, and set his dry toothbrush on the counter, and went out of the bathroom and closed the door behind him very quietly, and he did not tell anybody, and the knowledge sat in his stomach like a stone.

* * *

He put on his cargo shorts and a gray t-shirt in the dark and slid the Glock 43 into his right front pocket, and he went to the front door and looked through the peephole and could not see anything. He stepped into his sandals and went outside.

The streetlights were out. All of them. The whole street was pitch black, darker than he had ever seen it, and the air was cold — colder than it had been all week, the leading edge of the front Trevor had talked about — and he could see his breath. He had left the flashlight on the dresser. He went back in, got it, came out again, and stood on his front walk in the dark, and then, after a moment, he turned the flashlight off, to let his eyes adjust.

Over the rooftops to the east the sky had gone a deep, bruised purple. There were stars in the rest of it, more than he had ever seen over the Preserve; the glow from the Publix lot and the interstate was gone. Somewhere far off, a single siren was going, rising and falling.

A few doors down, the old man in the guayabera who had danced with Granny was standing on the sidewalk in front of his apartment. He had a navy cardigan buttoned over the guayabera and brown leather slippers on his feet, and he was looking up and down the dark street with his hands in his cardigan pockets, the way a man looks for a bus.

"Whole street?" John called softly.

"Whole street," the old man said. He shrugged in his cardigan. "It'll be back by lunch."

He turned and went back inside. John stood there a minute longer, listening to the siren, which had become two sirens. *The cell tower*, he thought. If it was just a transformer, the cell tower would have its own backup. He took out his phone. In the corner of the screen where the bars usually were, it said *No Service*.

He went back to the bedroom and sat on the edge of the bed.

"I think something's going on," John said.

"What," Sanda said, from under the covers. It was not a question.

"Where's your phone?"

She reached out and handed it to him without looking, still unplugging it from the dead charger. He punched in her code. *No Service.*

"If the cell towers are out," John said, "it's bigger than the street. They have backup generators."

"Go make us some coffee," Sanda said, through a yawn.

"Sanda," John said. "We. Have. No. Power."

There was a pause under the covers.

"Oh," Sanda said. "Right." She pulled the covers down to her chin and opened her eyes and looked at the ceiling. "Well, that sucks." She thought about it. "We have the Korean grill. And the French presses. We can boil water on the grill and make coffee."

"Yeah," John said slowly. "That could work."

He did not say anything about the water.

* * *

In the kitchen, the flashlight showed him the nine pitchers in their row along the back of the counter, glass and ceramic and steel and the rooster at the end, all of them full to the top, and the two French presses full, and the kettle full, where she had left them. He stood looking at them for a long time.

Sanda came shuffling in behind him in her robe, rubbing her eyes, and picked up the kettle.

"No," John said. "Not that."

"What?"

"Nobody touches the pitchers." His voice came out harder than he meant it to. "Or the presses. Or the kettle. The water's out, Sanda. That's all the water we have."

She looked at him in the light of the flashlight. She was still too sleepy to understand what he was not saying, and he was grateful for that and ashamed of being grateful.

"Then what am I supposed to make coffee with?" Sanda asked.

John opened the low cabinet beside the refrigerator. Inside, in its cardboard case, was the Perrier — the second case, from the Costco run. The first case was long gone; it turned out they had been drinking it with dinner, because that was what it was for. There were five half-liter bottles left in the second.

"We have Perrier," John said.

"Will that work?" Sanda asked, entirely serious.

"I'm not sure what happens when you boil sparkling water," John said. "It's full of carbon dioxide." Sanda looked up at him. She did not want a science lesson. She wanted coffee, and she wanted him to make it happen. "Fuck it," John said. "Let's try it. I'll leave the lid off the kettle so the gas can get out."

He poured the tap water from the kettle into a stockpot, carefully, so as not to spill a drop, and covered the stockpot with a plate, and set it beside the pitchers, so that it would be safe. Then he filled the empty kettle with Perrier.

It took three bottles. He brought the Korean grill in from the garage with one of the little green propane canisters and twisted the canister in until it locked with a loud click, and Charlie, who had come to watch, fled back to the bedroom. John found the long lighter in the drawer, turned the gas knob, heard the hiss, smelled nothing, and clicked the lighter. With a soft *whoomp*, a ring of blue flame lit up the whole dark kitchen. He turned it down by half and set the kettle on the iron dome.

The Perrier started fizzing almost at once. Then it began to pop and crackle and spit, like a pan of bacon, and little bursts of foam jumped out of the open top of the kettle and sizzled on the dome. He turned the flame down further. After a few minutes the popping settled into something that sounded more or less like ordinary boiling. Sanda dropped four scoops of ground coffee into the old French press, which she had emptied into the stockpot first without being told, and John poured the boiling Perrier over the grounds and stirred it with a long spoon and set a timer on the stove for four minutes, which did not work, because the stove was electric. He set it on his watch instead.

They stood in the kitchen in the blue light of the grill and looked at each other, and without a word, each of them knew the other was thinking how ridiculous this was.

The guest room door opened, and Dorina shuffled out in her flowered housecoat with her burgundy hair flattened on one side.

"Good morning, John," Dorina said, in her thick accent. "I think the power or something is not working."

"Yeah, I don't know what's going on yet, Mom," John said. "The power and the water are both out. We're making coffee."

"I think maybe was a storm," Dorina said, peering at the blue flame.

"I don't think it was a storm," John said, and his watch beeped.

Sanda pressed the plunger down slowly and poured three cups. John took an ice cube from the freezer, which was still cold, dropped it into his, waited for it to melt, and took a sip. He put the cup down on the counter.

"Yeah," John said. "This tastes like shit."

"I dunno," Sanda said, not ready to give up on the only thing she wanted in the world. She took a sip. She swallowed it hard and set her cup down. "This coffee tastes like ass."

For years John had tried to get his wife to tone down her language and failed, mainly because his own was no better. With no children to set an example for, they had let it slide downhill together, each raising the stakes on the other, until it had become a kind of game. She said she had two settings, home and branch. He was not sure the branch setting was holding.

"Is coffee no good?" Dorina asked, smiling.

"Not really, Mom, but you're welcome to try it."

Sanda gave her mother three packets of sweetener and two little cups of creamer from the drawer. Dorina stirred them in and took a long, thoughtful sip, and considered it, with her head on one side.

"Is not bad," Dorina said, and shrugged, and drank it all.

* * *

"So," John said, and they both looked at him. The sun was starting to come up now, and gray light was coming in through the blinds. "I think we've got a big outage. I'm not trying to panic anybody. But the water's out too, and the cell phones, and it wasn't a storm. I've been outside twice. The ground's dry. It didn't even rain."

Sanda and Dorina looked at him.

Someone knocked on the front door. Charlie came out of the bedroom like a missile, barking, and John put his right hand flat on the pocket of his shorts, over the Glock, and went to the peephole.

It was Granny. She was standing on the mat in her pink housecoat with the little blue flowers and a gray cardigan over it and her terrycloth slippers, looking back over her shoulder at the street. Her white curls were flattened on one side, like Dorina's.

"Charlie, no bark!" John said, and opened the door.

"Is your power out too?" Granny asked. "I tried to call you, but my phone isn't working."

"The power's out in the whole complex, Granny," John said, in a calm, flat voice he did not feel. "Probably more than that. The phones are out too. And the water."

"Oh my word!" Granny said.

"I'm sure it's just temporary," John lied, helping her inside. He looked at his wife. "Sanda. Can you help me in the closet for a second?"

It was code, and she knew it. She followed him into the master bedroom, and he closed the door.

"I'm going to drive around," John said quietly, "and see how big this is. I'll be twenty minutes. While I'm gone, I need you to watch this place. Don't scare your mom or Granny. Get the Bodyguard. It's getting cold in here — put on a jacket and put the gun in the pocket. Keep them calm."

"Okay," Sanda said. And then, looking up at him: "Just be careful."

He clipped the Benchmade, a black spring-loaded knife, into his back right pocket. He did not know what he expected to cut.

He backed the GTI out of the garage, put it in first, and popped the clutch harder than he needed to, and the tires chirped, and he drove to the front gate. The gate was closed. He pulled up to the white line where it always opened for him, and it did not open. He sat there. Inside the open steel box beside it, where the new Halcyon board was bolted to the back wall, the little green light that had blinked so patiently every night for a month was dark.

The gate did not need power to stay closed. It needed power to open. It needed the power and the cell tower and an app and a server somewhere, and all it had now was a steel arm and a motor and gravity.

He threw the car into reverse, swung around, and drove to the back gate over two speed bumps fast enough to scrape the bottom. It was closed too.

The leasing office was beside the front gate, in a low building with a fake stone front. He parked and walked up to the glass doors. The lobby inside was dark. Taped to the inside of the glass, at eye level, was a sheet of white printer paper. It said, in large black capital letters:

DUE TO THE OUTAGE THE OFFICE IS CLOSED. PLEASE SUBMIT REQUESTS THROUGH THE APP.

John read it twice. Then he got back in his car and drove home, past residents standing on their front steps in bathrobes, all of them watching him go by.

"The gates are locked," he told the three women in his kitchen. "Both of them. Nobody can get in or out."

Everyone paused. Far off, through the walls, there were more sirens now, three or four at once.

"I've got to find out what's going on," John said. "I'm going to walk out to the main road. Maybe I can flag down a cop."

"I don't think you should leave us," Sanda said. "You shouldn't be walking around by yourself when we don't know what's happening."

"What's the alternative? The water's out and we just used half our Perrier making shitty coffee. If this goes more than a day, we're fucked." He saw Granny's face, and Dorina's, and he regretted the sentence before he finished it. He made himself slow down. "Everybody stay here. I'm just going to walk to the main road. Thirty minutes."

Nobody said anything. Sanda looked at him without blinking.

He went to the front door. Charlie followed him, and he turned and raised one finger. "No bark." Charlie sat, and whined, and did not bark.

* * *

It was a ten-minute walk to the main road, around the dead gate through a gap in the hedge. He could see his breath the whole way. It was January and he was wearing shorts, and the cold went up his legs and made him think of Chicago. The strip mall on his right was dark, the Publix, the gym that never closed, all of it. On a Monday morning at seven the main road should have been solid. He counted four cars.

At the bus stop across from the strip mall there were two men.

One was about thirty, tall and loose-limbed, in a black-and-teal Jaguars windbreaker and gray sweatpants and slides with socks, with a thin gold chain at his throat. The other was older, in his fifties, heavyset, with gray coming into his short beard. He wore a brown canvas work jacket with an oval name patch sewn over the pocket, the kind from a uniform service, and the name stitched on it in red thread was DESMOND. He was looking at the ground.

A patrol car came around the corner fast, lights and siren, and went past them up the road.

John put his right hand in his pocket on the grip of the Glock and walked toward the bus stop.

"You guys know what's going on with the power?"

"Man, we don't know shit," said the one in the windbreaker. "Phones don't work. Water don't work. Thought we'd catch a bus outta here, but ain't no bus coming either."

Another cruiser came around the corner, green and white, Seminole County Sheriff, slower. John stepped out toward the road and waved both arms over his head. The car stopped beside him and the window came down. The deputy inside was young, with a shaved head and a thick neck and eyes that were red around the rims, as though he had been up all night.

"Sir," the deputy said, "I'm going to need you to go back to your home."

"Is the power out for the whole city?" John asked.

"Sir, I need you to return to your home. It's for your own safety."

John knew the voice. It was his father's voice, the work one. It meant the deputy did not know either, and had been told to say exactly this, and would say exactly this until his shift ended, whenever that would be.

"No problem," John said. "Thank you, officer."

The window went up and the cruiser pulled away. John turned back toward the complex. As he passed the bus stop, the one in the windbreaker stepped out toward him.

"Hey, man. Can you spare a dollar so we can catch a bus outta here?"

"I'm sorry, man," John said, and put his right hand back in his pocket.

In Chicago that had always been enough. Florida was different.

"Come on, man. Just a dollar." The man in the windbreaker was walking with him now, slowly, keeping pace a few yards off. "We gotta get to our grandmama's house in Ocala. Make sure she okay. She don't have nobody."

John held up his left hand. "I'm really sorry. I don't even have my wallet on me. I just walked out here to see what's going on."

"That's cool, man. We'll walk back to your place with you. You get your wallet. Ain't no bus coming anyhow."

John stopped and turned and faced him.

"That's not going to happen," John said, in a low voice he did not know he had. His right hand was in his pocket on the grip of the pistol, and his fist was so tight around it that the pocket bulged, and the man looked down at it. "I'm sorry. I can't help you."

The man stopped ten feet away. He looked at the pocket for a long second. Then he looked at John's face.

"Man, whatever," the man in the windbreaker said. "Fuck off."

He turned and walked back to the bus stop. The older man, Desmond, had never moved. He had never said a word. He was still looking at the ground.

John walked home quickly, but not too quickly. His wallet was in his back left pocket the entire time. He could feel it against him with every step, all the way back to the hedge and the dead gate and his front door, and he would never know — not then, not ever — whether there was a grandmother in Ocala, or whether she was okay.

* * *

"We have a problem," Sanda said, before he could get the door closed.

She was wearing her olive utility jacket, buttoned, and it hung lower on the right side than the left, from the weight of the Bodyguard in the pocket. She looked very serious.

"I had to use the bathroom," Sanda said, "and I flushed the toilet, and it went down and made this gurgling noise and it never filled back up."

"Number one or number two?" John asked.

"Number one."

"Okay." John was relieved, and kept his face serious. "We can't use the toilets until the water comes back. They'll only flush once. That was the once."

"Oh, it's fine," Sanda said, already turning. "I'll use the tub water to flush. That's what I filled it for."

She walked down the hall toward the master bathroom.

John stood in the living room and did not move. Granny was on the couch with Charlie in her lap. Dorina was at the kitchen counter, looking at the row of pitchers. Neither of them knew. John stood very still and listened to the bathroom door open, and then nothing, and then nothing, and then more nothing, for what was probably five seconds and felt like a week.

Sanda came back down the hall.

She did not shout. That was the worst part. She stopped in the living room doorway with her hands at her sides and looked at him, and her face was very calm.

"I filled the tub," Sanda said.

"I thought you left it—" John said.

"I FILLED," Sanda said. "THE TUB. JOHN."

Nobody moved. Granny's hand stopped in the middle of stroking Charlie. Dorina turned around from the counter. In the silence, John could hear the tinnitus in his ears and the sirens far off and his own heart.

"You said *mm-hm*," Sanda said, much more quietly. "Last night. I said don't touch the tub, and you said *mm-hm*."

"I did say mm-hm," John said.

She looked at him. Then she let out a breath, and he watched her take the whole thing — all of it, the tub, the night before, twenty years of draining her bathwater — and fold it up, very small, and put it away somewhere, because there was no water to waste on it.

"So what do we do," Sanda said, "about the toilet?"

It was the end of the fight. It was the whole fight. It was over in less than a minute, and it would not be mentioned again for a long time, but John would notice, for the rest of that winter, that whenever he gave an order to anyone in that apartment, Sanda would look at him for one beat longer than she needed to. Everybody would notice. Everybody would know why.

"Bags," John said, after a moment. "The Publix bags. We've got a hundred of them under the sink. You open one up and set it down in the bowl — there's hardly any water left in there now. When you're done, tie it off tight, and I'll take it out to the dumpster."

"This is crazy," Sanda said, shaking her head. "And how do we wash our hands?"

"We can't use the drinking water. Right now that's nine pitchers and a stockpot and whatever's in the freezer and the dog bowls. We don't know how long—"

"We have wine!" Sanda said, and her face lit up. "We have soap. We can wet our hands with wine and use the soap and rinse with wine."

John opened his mouth to object and could not think of an objection. They had forty-one bottles of wine in the cooler, he knew exactly how many, because the cooler had told them. "Okay," he said. "But white. Unless you want blue hands."

"There's a fifteen-dollar Chablis," Sanda said, already at the cooler. She found it, cut the foil, twisted in the corkscrew, and pulled the cork with a soft pop, and John heard the pop and could not believe that they were about to wash their hands with French wine. In ten years of talking about the end of the world, they had worked through hundreds of scenarios. They had never once discussed how you wash your hands. Sanda carried the Chablis into the guest bathroom and set it beside the sink like a bottle of soap.

* * *

It was getting cold in the apartment. John put on a light jacket, mostly so he could wear a second gun under it, the Glock 26, butt forward on his left hip. Eleven rounds on the left, seven on the right. He told himself the power would be back by lunch.

"Granny," John said. "Did you flush your toilet yet this morning?"

"I went," Granny said, "but it was so dark I couldn't find the handle."

"Good. That's good. Don't." He turned to Sanda. "I need two bowls and a ladle. One for the ice in Granny's freezer before it melts. One for the water in her toilet tank. It's clean. It's the tank on the back, not the bowl."

"We are not drinking toilet water," Sanda said, horrified.

"It's not for us. It's for Charlie."

"Charlie drinks filtered water!" Sanda said, before she could stop herself.

"Not anymore!" John said.

Granny's apartment was smaller and darker than theirs, and he had forgotten his flashlight. Again. On her nightstand he found the little black one he had given her years ago and reprogrammed so it only had one setting, low, and he used it to ladle the clear water out of her toilet tank into a bowl and to dump her ice trays into the other. When he got back, he set the bowl of tank water on the floor beside Charlie's dish.

"Okay. Listen," John said, standing in the middle of the living room. "Nobody knows yet what's going on. The power and the water could come back an hour from now. Or it could be days. We have to plan like it's going to be a week. Drink the water slow. If you have to go number one, don't flush. For number two, bags. Wash your hands with the wine." He counted on his fingers. "For food, we've got fruit — apples, bananas, oranges. Meat and cheese in the fridge, warming up. Pasta, but we can't spare the water to cook it. Cans of beans and soup. Tuna. Sardines." He paused. "And forty pounds of jerky. Kangaroo, alligator, buffalo, and wild boar."

Granny made a face as if she had found something in her shoe.

"No way I'm eatin' any sardines," Granny said. "No sir."

"You'd be surprised what you'll eat if you get hungry enough, Granny," John said. "Let's hope we don't find out."

"If we just had a TV," Sanda said. "Or a radio. Some news." She stopped. "Your parents. Aren't they at that state park, with the RV? Wekiwa?"

"Thirty minutes from here." John felt something like hope for the first time that morning. "The RV has a generator. Three TVs. If they hear about this, they'll come. Dad'll come."

"How will they get in?" Granny asked. "With the gate?"

Nobody had an answer for that.

* * *

He took Charlie out at nine, late, far too late, and Charlie peed on the first patch of grass he came to without ceremony of any kind.

Across the street the blond woman with the pug was out too, in the pink velour tracksuit with a gray cardigan pulled over it, and when the dogs saw each other they hit the ends of their leashes, and John crossed over, because Charlie gave him no choice. The pug was snorting and wagging her whole back half.

"Crazy, isn't it?" John said. "This outage."

"I don't know what to think," the woman said. Her scratchy voice was thinner this morning. She was not smoking. "Is your water out too?"

"Power, water, phones. Everything."

"I hope it's nothing too serious," the woman said, and she said it the way people say a prayer they have said too many times to listen to.

The dogs began to circle each other, and the leashes began to braid, and John stepped over one and under the other, and it occurred to him that this was the first time they had ever spoken about anything but the dogs. He held out his free hand. "I'm John, by the way."

"I'm Sarah," she said, taking it. Her hand was cold and very thin. "Nice to meet you, John."

"I'll let you know if I find out anything," John said.

"Thank you," Sarah said. "I really appreciate that."

She untangled the pug and scooped her up and went back inside. John never did come back to tell her anything. He would remember that.

* * *

When he came back in, Sanda had made breakfast.

She had set the dining table with real plates and cloth napkins, and on each plate she had fanned out slices of apple and orange, half a banana, three thin slices of Comté and a wedge of Manchego, and several rose-colored ribbons of prosciutto, arranged in a little nest. It was a beautiful breakfast. It was a breakfast for a Sunday in Napa.

Granny poked at the prosciutto with one finger. "What is this here?"

"It's ham, Granny," John said. "Just a different kind of ham."

He leaned toward Sanda and whispered, "We need to be careful with the food. We don't know how long this is going to—"

"That's enough," Sanda whispered back, sharply. "Everybody's scared. They've been through hell this morning. The least I can do is give them a decent breakfast."

She looked at him. She looked at him for one beat longer than she needed to.

He knew which battles to pick, and this was not one of them. "Fair enough," he said. "I'm going to sit down for a minute and think."

He took a banana from the bowl and peeled it walking to his recliner and sat. Charlie, who had developed a taste for bananas, sat at the foot of the recliner with one paw raised. John ate the banana in big bites, thinking. Nothing fit together. He was disappointed that no brilliant plan arrived. He had spent ten years waiting for this morning, and now it was here, and he had drained the tub.

He put the banana peel in his lap. He closed his eyes for a second.

He heard Sanda cross the room. He felt her lift the banana peel off his lap. He heard her walk away with it, toward the kitchen, and drop it in the trash. He kept his eyes closed. He did not know how long he slept.

He woke all at once, his hand already moving to his left hip.

Someone was knocking on the front door. Hard. Three times, like a fist, like a man who intended to be let in. And Charlie came off the couch in an explosion of high, frantic barking and hit the door, and through it, under the barking, John heard a man's voice outside, and he could not make out a single word of what it said.

# Chapter Twelve

## The Parents

> *"…hospitals are operating on generator power. Cellular service has been suspended to conserve power. The cause of the outage is not yet known…"*
>
> — AM radio, Monday, 5:00 a.m.

"John, wake up," Sanda said. "Somebody's at the door."

He was already up. He did not remember getting out of the recliner. The Glock 26 was in his right hand, out of the holster, pointed at the floor, and Charlie was hurling himself at the front door in a continuous high scream of barking, and Sanda was on her tiptoes at the peephole, bouncing a little, trying to see out of a hole that had been installed for a man of average height.

"I can't see," Sanda said.

"Move."

Granny, on the couch, and Dorina, at the kitchen counter, stared at the gun in his hand as he crossed the room. Nobody in the family had ever seen him hold one inside the apartment. He put his eye to the peephole.

His parents were standing on the front walk in the midafternoon light, looking nervous, and his mother was looking back at the street.

John let out a breath he had been holding since about six o'clock that morning. He tucked the Glock behind his back, unlocked the deadbolt with his left hand, and opened the door.

"What took you guys so long?" John said.

"Son, you would not believe it," Jim said, coming in past him. "You would not believe how hard it is to get a thirty-four-foot motor home through all those roadblocks. Every intersection. I had to show my badge twenty times to get here from thirty minutes away."

Charlie had stopped barking and started jumping, straight up, three feet in the air, over and over, at Liz's knees, the way he did for people he loved.

Jim was in the clothes he always wore on the road: a navy windbreaker with a small embroidered star over the heart, a gift from the Jacksonville Sheriff's Office when he retired; a tan Columbia fishing shirt under it with vents in the back; jeans; white New Balance sneakers; and the black-and-teal Jaguars cap, which, since Liz was not looking, stayed on. He needed a shave. Liz came in behind him in a quilted navy vest over a crisp white button-down with the sleeves rolled exactly twice, jeans, and white Keds, with her reading glasses hanging from their beaded chain and a canvas tote over her shoulder, and she bent and scooped Charlie up out of the air in mid-jump and held him.

"Where's the RV?" John asked.

"In the parking lot at that strip mall. By the Publix. Your gate doesn't work, so we walked."

"Tell me what you know, Dad." John sat down on the arm of the couch. "Was the power out at the park? Did any of the cops tell you anything?"

"The cops don't know anything," Jim said. "But the radio did." He took off the cap, finally, and turned it in his hands. "Power was out when we woke up. Five o'clock. Your mother was cold, so I fired up the generator to run the heat. The TVs didn't get anything, just snow, so I turned on the radio in the dash." He paused and looked at the floor. He was not a man who paused. "John, it's bad."

John waited.

"They said at three o'clock this morning, the grid went down in New York," Jim said slowly. "All of it. In half an hour it went across the whole East, all the way to Chicago. By five, the whole country was out. Everything. The hospitals are on generators. They shut the cell towers down to save power. A couple of radio stations are still on because they've got generators. The man said they don't know what caused it."

"Holy," John said. "Fucking. Shit."

Each word came out separately, as though someone else were saying it.

"They had theories," Jim said. "Some kind of EMP. Hackers. One fella thought it was the sun."

"It's not hackers," John said. "And it's not the sun. It's Vee."

"Who's Vee?"

"The robot, Dad."

Jim looked at him. "The robot from Thanksgiving?"

"The robot from Thanksgiving." John stood up. He found he could not sit down. "They turned it off in December. The president said it worked. It didn't work. It was never in the buildings, Dad. It was in everything else. I found seven of its — I found things, at work, on Friday. In our software. It can turn the power on and off. Phoenix went out on Friday night by itself. Atlanta on Saturday. And now this."

"So the robot turned off the power," Jim said, trying the idea on, like a man trying on a coat in a store and not liking how it fit.

"I don't know," John said. "Something did."

* * *

"Okay," John said. He stood in the middle of the living room, and everyone looked at him: his parents, his grandmother, his mother-in-law, his wife. "Now we know this is big. We need to talk, all of us, about how we get through it."

He took a breath.

"It's going to get bad. Not right away. Right now most people out there think this is a normal outage. They're sitting in their living rooms waiting for the lights. In a couple of days they'll run out of water. That's when it starts. First thing, somebody breaks into the Publix for water and food. The police try to stop it. At some point the police give up — they'll be home with their own families. And then it's house to house. Guys with guns. Taking whatever they can carry." He looked around the room at all of them. "This is the part where we decide. Whether we fight to stay alive, or we just lie down."

He heard the words coming out of his mouth, and he could not believe that he was saying them, in his own living room, to his grandmother. A week ago, the same words at a dinner table would have gotten an eye roll from every person in this room.

"That's a pretty good description of the end of the world," Jim said, half joking.

"I think that's what this is, Dad."

"Listen," Jim said.

He said it in a voice John had heard maybe a dozen times in his life. His father was a joker. He was the life of every party, always the one with the story, and he had gotten through thirty-five years of police work that way, laughing at things other men drank about. But under the jokes there was another voice, low and slow and level, that came out only when it mattered. John had heard it at his grandfather's funeral. He had heard it at his father's retirement party, at a little substation in Jacksonville, when Jim got up to make a speech and got halfway through and stopped and stood there with his eyes filling, because he had just understood, standing in front of everyone, that he had made it — thirty-five years, and he had made it home.

"Listen to me," Jim said, in that voice. "I don't think we should jump the gun here. I was a police officer for thirty-five years. I know how these things play out."

"You've been through a nationwide blackout?"

"Just hear me out." Jim did not raise his voice. "There are police everywhere out there. Every intersection. They've got it under control. I'd bet you anything, in two days the power's back and we all go on with our lives."

John smirked and looked away, out the window. Nothing was happening outside.

From down the hall came Liz's voice, from the guest bathroom. "Did someone leave an open bottle of wine in here?"

"It's so you can wash your hands," John called back, "after you take a shit."

There was a pause. Liz came out of the bathroom drying her hands on her jeans, and looked at her son, and he could see her fighting very hard not to smile.

"Really," Liz said. It was not a question.

"We don't know how long the water's going to be out," John said. "We're not wasting drinking water on hands."

Jim put a finger on his chin. "Well. My motor home has a seventy-gallon fresh water tank," he said. "We've got plenty of water. I say we pack up some things and all go over to the motor home and drive somewhere safe. Away from here."

"I don't think that's a good idea," John said.

"Why not?"

"First, how far are you going to get? There's no power. You can't stop for gas, because the pumps don't work. And second, that big-ass RV stands out like a sore thumb. It's a target. For looters, for anybody. You park that thing anywhere and every desperate person in a mile is going to think, there's the food."

"You worry too much about people," Jim said, folding his arms. "I think you're overreacting. There are cops everywhere. And besides. I have a gun."

John looked at his father.

"You have your gun," John said. "What gun?"

"My Ruger. The two-eighty." Jim patted the back right pocket of his jeans. "Right here."

"You have a *two-eighty*," John said, letting each word land.

Jim's face clouded. "Three-eighty?" He turned. "Liz. Is it a two-eighty or a three-eighty?"

Liz scratched her head. "I think it's a three-eighty."

John stood there and waited, with no expression at all, to see whether his father would ever work out what he had been carrying for six years.

"It doesn't matter what the hell it is," Jim said, irritated. "I point it at somebody and pull the trigger, it'll kill them."

"There's no such thing as a two-eighty," John said. "You have a three-eighty. It's basically a short, weak nine-millimeter."

"I *knew* it was a three-eighty," Jim said, and laughed.

"Dad. You haven't shot that thing in years. It holds seven rounds. You've told me yourself a hundred times you're a bad shot."

"I'm good enough!" Jim said, and made a face — a wide-eyed, crossed-eyes face he had been making at John since John was two — because he did not like serious conversations and was trying to get out of this one. Then the face went away and the low voice came back. "So. Here's what's going to happen. Your mother and I are going to sleep in the motor home tonight. We've got a gallon of milk that's almost empty. We'll rinse it out and fill it, and you take it back here, so y'all aren't washing your hands with Chardonnay. There's plenty of room in the motor home if you want to stay with us. You could take a shower, you could—"

"Dad!" John said. "Seventy gallons won't last a day if everybody starts taking showers and flushing toilets!"

"Son." Jim held up one hand, patiently, and John saw, with a sinking feeling, that his father was enjoying himself. Jim knew the RV. Jim had read the RV's owner's manual twice, cover to cover, at the kitchen table in Jacksonville with a highlighter. For the first time in John's adult life, they were talking about something his father knew more about than he did. "You're blowing this way out of proportion. And just so you know, the water for the shower and the toilet comes from a whole different tank. The gray tank. Totally separate. The seventy gallons is only for drinking."

"Jim," Liz said.

"What?"

"The gray tank is where the shower water *goes*," Liz said. "Not where it comes from."

Jim opened his mouth. He thought about it. He closed it.

"Same tank," Jim said.

"It is not the same tank."

"It's a *related* tank," Jim said with dignity, and Granny, on the couch, laughed out loud for the first time all day.

"That RV is a target," John said. "I don't think you and Mom should sleep in it."

"John," Liz said, and her voice was perfectly pleasant and perfectly final. "There is no way I am leaving our motor home alone in a Publix parking lot. That is a very expensive motor home."

"It's getting late," Jim said. "Your mother and I will stay in the motor home tonight, and tomorrow, if the power's still out, we'll talk about it some more."

John knew there was no point. He had grown up with these two people. "Okay," he said. "I'll walk you back before it gets dark. I'll bring the water back."

* * *

The sun was going down when they made the ten-minute walk back to the motor home, the three of them, through the gap in the hedge by the dead gate and across the road and into the strip-mall lot. The lot was nearly empty. In front of the dark Publix sat a Seminole County cruiser with a deputy in it, eating something from a paper bag. Farther down, near the motor home, a second cruiser was parked in front of the dark nail salon.

"See, John?" Jim said, nodding at them. "Nobody's going to loot anything with the police sitting right there. We'll be fine tonight."

The motor home was enormous. John had forgotten. It was a white Winnebago, thirty-four feet long, with a swoosh of blue and gold paint down the side and a huge, curved windshield like the face of a whale. Jim unlocked the door and climbed in and flipped on the lights, which ran off the house battery, and John climbed in after him and looked around. It was surprisingly roomy. There was a leather couch and a dinette and a galley kitchen with a two-burner stove and a microwave, a bedroom in the back with a queen bed, and a shower stall with a frosted glass door. There were three flat-screen televisions mounted around the interior — one over the dash, one over the dinette, one in the bedroom — all dark.
