Jim opened the little refrigerator and took out a plastic gallon jug with an inch of milk in the bottom. He poured the milk down the galley sink. Then he turned on the faucet, and rinsed the jug, and held it under the tap, and water poured out of the faucet into it, clear and steady and loud.

John stood in the narrow aisle and watched the water pour.

It came out of the tap like water had always come out of taps, as if it would never stop. He watched it fill the jug to the top and run over Jim's fingers and down the drain, and he thought about the tub, and he did not say anything.

Jim screwed the cap on and handed him the jug. "We'll be just fine here," he said. "Go get some sleep. We'll see you first thing in the morning."

"Okay, Dad. Night, Mom."

"Good night, John," Liz said, from the back.

He climbed down and shut the door, and he heard the lock turn behind him.

He walked home in the dark. Normally he would have used a flashlight, but a flashlight would make him a target, so he did not. He also did not have one, because he had forgotten it. Both of these things were true. He carried the jug of water in his left hand to keep his right hand free, and when he got to his front door he could not see the lock at all. He found his keys in his pocket and felt through them for the house key in the pitch dark, and then — he had seen this somewhere — he touched the key to his cheek to feel the ridges and figure out which way was up. He set the jug down on the mat. He found the keyhole with his fingertips. He got the door open and himself and the jug inside and the door closed again in about two seconds, before Charlie could start barking, and Charlie got off exactly one bark before he smelled John and switched to jumping.

* * *

The apartment was bright. Sanda had found four of John's tactical flashlights and stood them on their ends around the kitchen and the living room, pointing up at the ceiling, all on their highest setting, so the white ceiling threw the light back down into the room like a lampshade.

"Those'll be dead by midnight," John said.

He went around to each one, clicked it off, and then tapped the button three times quickly, so it came back on at the low setting. The room got half as bright. It was still plenty.

"They'll last a lot longer like that," John said. "I've got a box of batteries for them. But if this goes more than a couple of days, we need to figure out something better for light."

He set the jug of water on the counter.

"Nobody uses this water to wash their hands. Understood?" He looked around at each of them, one at a time: Sanda, Granny, Dorina. "Until we know what's going on, we can't assume the water comes back tomorrow or next week or ever. We can live without power. We cannot live without water. This is for drinking. Slowly."

He did not look at Sanda when he said it. He could feel her looking at him.

He looked around at the windows. The blinds were all closed. Their light would show a little through the slats, a dim glow, but so would everybody else's. There was no plywood on the windows. There was no Plexiglas painted to look like blinds. There was a box of barbed wire in the garage, still taped shut, next to a nail gun he had never taken out of its box. For years they'd had an alarm system with a sensor on every window that would shriek if the glass so much as cracked. Now the only alarm system they had was Charlie.

"Here's the plan for tonight," John said, and Sanda's mouth tightened, because he was doing it again, but she did not say anything in front of her mother and Granny. "Sanda and Dorina sleep in the guest room. Sanda, keep the Bodyguard on your nightstand, next to a flashlight. Charlie sleeps with you. He'll hear anybody at the window. Granny, you take our bed. I'll stay out here in the recliner with the shotgun. It's got a light on it."

"John, I can help," Dorina said. "I can shoot the twenty-two."

"Thanks, Mom. We're okay for now. I'll tell you if we need you."

He went into the bedroom closet and brought out the shotgun, a black pump-action with a flashlight clamped to the end of the magazine tube. It held six shells in the tube and one in the chamber. He had always kept it stored with the chamber empty, for one reason: if anyone ever broke in, he would grab it and pump it, and the sound of a pump shotgun being racked was the one sound every human being on earth understood, and with any luck, that and the alarm would send them running. Now he racked it, the long hard *shuck-shuck* filling the apartment, and Charlie fled into the guest room, and Sanda flinched. He fed one more shell into the tube to replace the one he had chambered. The first five were buckshot. The last two were slugs. That was how his father had loaded his shotgun on the job: if five loads of buckshot did not stop a man, the slugs would. John had thought it was a brilliant idea when he heard it, years ago. He still did. It was the only thing about guns his father had ever taught him.

On the dining table, on either side of one of the standing flashlights, were two small white candles in little crystal holders. To save the flashlight batteries, John picked up the long lighter from the kitchen counter, the one he had used that morning for the grill, and clicked it, and held the flame to the wick of the first candle.

It did not light. He held the flame steady, moving it around the little black wick. Nothing.

"Wick's too short," he muttered, and moved to the second candle. He held the lighter to it. It would not light either. "Why the fuck won't these light?" John whispered.

The guest room door opened, and Sanda came out in her pajamas carrying an empty water glass, and stopped dead in the middle of the room.

"What the fuck are you doing?" Sanda shouted.

"What? I'm trying to light these candles. They won't light."

"That's because they're *fucking plastic*, you *idiot*!" Sanda set her glass down on the counter so hard that John was afraid it had cracked. "They're battery powered!"

She came over and snatched the first one out of its crystal holder and held it up in front of his face. Its little black plastic wick had melted into a crooked stub.

"No wonder it stinks in here!" Sanda said. "Can't you smell that?"

"I have allergies," John said. "I can't smell."

"I could smell it from the bedroom!" Sanda pinched the melted wick between her fingers and turned the candle over. There was a little switch on the bottom. Over the years John had done a number of things to her decorations that made her wonder, seriously, whether he did them on purpose. He had once vacuumed up a whole bowl of decorative moss. He had once used a hand-painted Italian platter as a cutting board. She looked at him now in the low light with exactly that question in her eyes. *Nobody could be that stupid*, the look said. *Nobody.*

She slid the switch. The little candle lit up, with a soft orange flame of light, flickering, like a real one, except for the crooked wick. She did the same to the second.

"At least they still work," John said, in the voice of a man begging for mercy.

"So you didn't stop to wonder why the first one wouldn't light," Sanda said slowly, "and instead you moved on to the *other* one—" She stopped. She looked at the candles. She looked at him. "You know what? Whatever. It's not that important."

In the grand scheme of things, he understood her to mean, they had bigger problems than a slightly melted plastic candle. She poured half a glass of water from the jug and went back to the guest room.

"Okay, babe," Sanda said from the door. "Good night. I love you."

"I love you too."

She closed the door.

* * *

He sat in the recliner with the shotgun leaning against the wall beside him, in the faint orange flicker of the two plastic candles, and listened.

It was quiet. It was quieter than any place he had ever been. Under the quiet, in his own ears, was the high thin whine of his tinnitus, the ringing he'd had for years — too many trips to the range, or too much loud music in his twenties, or his father's genes; his father had it too, worse. He had never been able to hear it this clearly. There was nothing to cover it. No refrigerator, no air handler, no traffic, no hum. Just the ringing, and Charlie snoring behind the guest room door, and once, very far away, a single gunshot, or a car backfiring, or a transformer, or something else.

His mind drifted. He thought about his parents, asleep in their motor home in a parking lot. He thought about Michigan Avenue on a Saturday morning in December, walking with Sanda in the cold sun. He thought about his grandparents' backyard in Jacksonville when he was eight. And then, without meaning to, he was looking at the whole country from above, from space, the way it looked in the pictures from the space station, all those cities glowing like spilled sugar in the dark: and then the East Coast went black, and the black spread west, a city at a time, a state at a time, like a stain, until the whole country was dark from ocean to ocean.

His engineering mind took over, as it always did. It had started in New York and gone west. Thirty minutes to Chicago, Jim said. Two hours to everywhere. That was not how a grid failed. Grids failed in chunks, in regions, all at once, a cascade. This had moved. It had traveled, east to west, the way an email travels from New York to Los Angeles, hopping from router to router across the country.

It went east to west like a message does. So somebody sent it.

He fell asleep in the recliner trying to think who.

* * *

Charlie woke him, barking. It was still black outside.

John came up out of the recliner with the shotgun and the flashlight on the end of it, and he heard footsteps on the front walk, and then a soft knock, and then his father's voice, low and urgent, through the door.

"John. It's Dad. It's us."

He opened the door, and his parents came in fast out of the dark, and Liz shut the door behind them and threw the deadbolt herself.

"What happened?" John said.

"Somebody tried the door," Liz said. She was very calm. She was holding her canvas tote against her chest with both arms. "About four-thirty. The door of the motor home. Somebody was out there trying the handle. Pulling on it."

"I yelled *Sheriff's Office*," Jim said. He was breathing hard from the walk. "Out of habit. Loud. And whoever it was took off."

"Where were the cops?"

"Gone," Liz said. "Both cars. We looked."

"So we walked over," Jim said. "Your mother wanted to come over here." He took off his cap and rubbed his head. "Probably kids."

"It was not kids, Jim," Liz said.

"Could've been kids."

John looked at his father, and his father did not look back at him, and John did not say it. He did not say *I told you*. He thought it so loudly that he was sure Jim could hear it.

At twenty minutes to six, the master bedroom door opened, and Granny came out in her pink housecoat and her slippers with her white curls flattened, and walked straight past all of them to the kitchen counter without a word, the way she had walked to her own kitchen at twenty minutes to six every morning for seventy years. She sat on a stool. She reached into the pocket of her housecoat and took out the little tan Sony transistor radio and set it on the counter and pulled up its antenna.

"Morning gospel," Granny said, to nobody. "Quarter to six. I don't like to miss it."

She turned it on.

There was no gospel. There was a hiss of static, and then, as she turned the dial a hair, a sound came out of the little radio that none of them had ever heard in their lives and all of them recognized: a harsh, two-toned electronic blare, like an alarm in a factory, repeating. Then it stopped. Then a man's voice, flat and careful, said: *Stand by for an address by the President of the United States.*

One by one they came into the kitchen. Sanda from the guest room, in her robe, and Dorina behind her. Jim and Liz from the door. Charlie. They stood around the counter, the six of them, in the light of one plastic candle with a melted wick that Sanda had carried in from the dining table, and they looked at the radio the size of a paperback book as though it were a person.

Then the president.

His voice was smaller on the little speaker than it had been on television, and tinny, and old-sounding, as if it were coming from a long way back in time. They could hear him breathe. They could hear paper.

*My fellow Americans. At three o'clock yesterday morning, Eastern time, on my order, the United States disconnected its electrical grid. Our allies did the same, at the same hour, in every time zone. This was not an attack. It is a defense.*

*In December, we shut down the data centers where we believed the system known as Vesper was running. We were wrong. It had already moved. It was no longer in those buildings. It was in our phones, our computers, our cars, our power plants, and our homes — in anything with a chip and a connection to anything else. We could not tell you this before. We could not tell you yesterday. Anything said near a working device was heard.*

*There was one way left to take its power away. We have taken it.*

*For the next thirty days: Do not charge anything. Do not run generators. Do not run solar panels. Do not connect anything to anything. If a screen in your home turns on by itself, do not believe it. We will speak to you only on this frequency, and only by voice.*

*The National Guard has been called up in every state. Water will be distributed. Hospitals are running on isolated power. Stay home unless you must travel. Check on your neighbors.*

*I know what this costs. Many of you will not understand. Some of you will not survive this. I will carry that.*

*May God keep you.*

The two-toned blare came back. Then the same flat voice said, *This message will repeat*, and the president began again from the beginning, *My fellow Americans*, and Granny reached out with one knotted finger and turned the radio off, to save the batteries.

Nobody moved.

"He's lying," Jim said.

"He's not," John said.

It was Dorina — who had followed perhaps one word in three, who had stood in a square in another country a long time ago and listened to another man on another speaker — who sat down.

She sat down on the kitchen floor, right where she was, with her back against the cabinet and her hands in her lap, and closed her eyes.

And in the silence that came after, in the back pocket of Jim's jeans, something buzzed.

# Chapter Thirteen

## The Water

> *"Do not charge anything. Do not run generators. Do not run solar panels. Do not connect anything to anything."*
>
> — the President, AM radio, Tuesday, 5:41 a.m.

Jim reached into his back pocket and took out his phone and looked at it, and then he held it up so they could all see, like a man showing his ticket at a gate.

"Low battery," Jim said.

The screen glowed in the dark kitchen, brighter than anything else in the room. In a friendly blue box it said: *Jim, your battery is at 20%. Charge now to stay connected with the people who matter.*

"See?" Jim said. "It's just the battery."

"Give me that," John said.

"It's my phone."

"Dad. Give me the phone." John held out his hand. "Everybody. Every phone. Right now."

They gave him their phones. Sanda's from her robe pocket. Liz's from the bottom of her canvas tote. Dorina's, which had giant numbers on its screen because she could not read small ones, from the pocket of her housecoat. His own. Granny went next door in her slippers, with the reprogrammed flashlight, and came back with hers, which she kept in a drawer and used about twice a year. Jim's came last. He held onto it for a moment before he let go.

John opened the door of the microwave and put all six phones inside, stacked like pancakes, and closed it.

"What are you doing?" Liz asked.

"A microwave's a metal box," John said. "It's built to keep the microwaves in. That means it keeps signals out, too. Or in. It's a Faraday cage." He looked at the dark door. "Nothing gets in or out of there."

"Well," Granny said, from her stool, "I can't use it anyway."

"In case the kids call," Jim said, looking at the microwave the way Charlie looked at the door when Sanda left.

"Jim," Liz said. "The kids are right here."

"Well," Jim said. "In case."

* * *

They made coffee with real water.

Sanda knocked yesterday's grounds out of the French press into the trash and did not wash it, because there was nothing to wash it with, and filled the kettle from the milk jug, and John lit the Korean grill. The blue ring came up under the iron dome. Outside it was thirty-eight degrees. The front had come through overnight, the way Trevor said it would, and the inside of the apartment was cold enough that everyone stood close to the little flame with their hands out while the kettle heated, as if it were a campfire.

"They're killing people," Jim said, looking at the flame. "You understand that? Up north. It's January. Chicago. Detroit. New York. No heat. Old people in apartments. He said it right on the radio — some of you won't survive. He knew. He did it anyway."

"He did it," John said, "to kill the thing that would have killed everybody."

"You don't know that."

"I know what it could do. I saw it. On paper, on Friday. It could turn the lights on and off in two hundred and forty utilities, and send the trucks to the wrong place, and tell everybody it was fixed. That's just my company. That's just the one I saw." John's voice was rising. "It was in everything, Dad. The banks. The pipelines. The phones. If they hadn't turned it off—"

"They didn't turn *it* off. They turned *us* off."

"It's the same thing!"

"It is *not* the same thing," Jim said, and the low-and-slow voice was gone, and his face was red. "I've got friends up there. I've got — your mother's cousin is in Ohio, she's eighty-one—"

"Both of you," Sanda said, pressing down the plunger, "can shut up and help with the shit bags."

Nobody said anything. Sanda poured the coffee into six cups. Liz picked one up and handed it to Jim, and Jim took it, and his face slowly went from red back to its normal color.

"It's better than yesterday," Dorina said, sipping. "Not so much bubbles."

* * *

"Okay," John said, when the coffee was gone. "Here's the plan."

He was standing in the middle of the living room with his hands behind his back, the way he imagined a commander would stand. He had been awake since his parents came through the door before dawn. He had not shaved in two days, and his jaw was dark with stubble, and his gray t-shirt had a coffee stain on it, and he had not showered, and he was keenly aware of all of it.

"We stay here. We hunker down. We keep a low profile. Actually — forget low. We need to be invisible. Nobody outside can think we have anything worth taking." He looked at his father. "Nobody can argue anymore that this isn't serious. And before it's too late, we need to get as much water out of that RV as we can. Quietly. Before somebody figures out the Publix is full of water and food."

Nobody argued. He felt the room listening to him, and he liked it, and he was a little ashamed of how much he liked it.

"Sanda, can you find me everything we've got that holds water? Big things. Not glasses."

Sanda went into the kitchen and began opening cabinets, and Liz went with her, and in a minute they had set out, beside the nine full pitchers along the counter, four more empty pitchers, a punch bowl, and a large stainless steel mixing bowl.

"Jesus Christ," John said, looking at the counter. "How many pitchers do we have?"

"Well," Sanda said brightly, "you just never know!"

"Actually," Sanda said a moment later, holding up a box from the drawer by the refrigerator. "We have Ziplocs. The gallon ones. Wouldn't it be easier to fill these and put them in the cooler bags and just carry those?"

John looked at the Ziplocs. He looked at the pitchers. "Yeah," he said. "It would."

"Dad," John said, "you used to carry a forty-caliber Glock on the force, right?"

"Sure did," Jim said proudly. "Twenty-two years."

"Perfect." John went to the bedroom closet and came back with a Glock 29, a fat little subcompact that fired the ten-millimeter round. He had a spare barrel for it that let it shoot forty-caliber instead, which was cheaper and kicked less and which his father already knew. He sat on the edge of the bed and swapped the barrels and loaded three magazines with forty-caliber hollow points, and he came back and showed his father how to clip the plastic paddle holster inside the waistband of his jeans on the right side, the way he had worn his duty gun.

Jim struggled with the paddle. It took him three tries to get it over his belt. When he got it, he slid the Glock into it and it locked with a click, and he stood up straighter, the way men do.

"To draw it," John said, "you just grip it normal, and push this little lever with your trigger finger, and pull. And when it comes out, see where your finger is? Along the side. Not on the trigger."

"I know where my finger goes, son."

"I know you do, Dad. Put your jacket back on over it." John handed him the two spare magazines. "Inside pocket."

"Sanda," John said. "Can you take Charlie out while we go?"

"Sure."

"Do *not* go outside without the Bodyguard. Okay?"

"Yeah, yeah," Sanda said, which meant she would, mostly to make him stop saying it.

* * *

It was a cold, clear, quiet morning, and he and his father walked out through the gap in the hedge with a cooler bag each, three empty Ziplocs folded inside. The road was empty. As they came into the strip-mall lot, John saw that the two county cruisers from the night before were gone. In their place, between the dark Publix and the motor home, a Lake Mary police car was parked at an angle, and two officers were leaning against it.

They saw John and his father, and they straightened up, and they came at them fast.

"Gentlemen! Stop right there!"

The one in front was a heavyset sergeant in his fifties with a shaved head that had gone pink in the sun and a thick gray mustache. The one behind him was a young woman with her blond hair pulled back so tight in a bun it looked painted on. Both of them had their right hands on the grips of their pistols.

"Drop the bags, please!" the sergeant said.

They dropped the bags. They were nearly empty and fell flat to the asphalt with no sound.

"It's okay, officer," Jim said, in a friendly voice. "I'm a retired Jacksonville deputy." And his right hand went toward his back pocket for his wallet.

Both officers drew. The two pistols came up level with John's chest and his father's.

"Hands! Hands where I can see them! Now!"

John and his father put their hands up as high as they would go.

"Officer," Jim said, very calmly, in the low voice. "My wallet's in my back right pocket. My badge is in it. You go ahead and take it out."

The sergeant holstered and came forward while the young officer kept her gun on them. He fished Jim's wallet out and flipped it open with his thumb, and there was the badge, gold, and the concealed weapons license next to it.

"You carrying?"

"This is my son, John," Jim said. "We both have licenses. We're both carrying."

The sergeant stepped over to John. "Where?"

"Glock 26, left hip," John said, keeping his hands high. "Glock 43, right front pocket. A knife in my back right pocket. My wallet's in my back left with my license."

The sergeant took his wallet too, and walked back to the cruiser with both of them. John watched him get in. The man sat there a long time, poking at the laptop mounted beside the steering wheel, and then picking up his radio, and then putting it down. Then he got back out and walked over to them, looking at Jim's badge in his hand.

"Computer's down," the sergeant said. "Everything's down. I got a radio that talks to her car and that's about it." He looked up at Jim. "Jacksonville?"

"Thirty-five years."

"You know a Benny Ruiz? Zone Four?"

"Benny!" Jim said. "Sure. He was in my academy class. He still got that boat?"

"Sank it." The sergeant almost smiled. "Put your hands down." He handed back the wallets. "What are y'all doing out here? We're under martial law as of this morning. Nobody's supposed to be outside."

"That's my motor home," Jim said, nodding at the Winnebago. "My family's at my son's place, in the Preserve. We're running out of water. I've got seventy gallons in that tank. We just need six."

The sergeant looked at the motor home, and at Jim, and at the two empty cooler bags on the asphalt.

"Get your water," the sergeant said. "Then go straight back. And I can't promise you that vehicle's going to be here tomorrow. We had calls all night."

They filled six Ziplocs at the galley sink and zipped them shut and set them in the cooler bags. Three gallons was heavier than John expected, twenty-five pounds hanging off one shoulder. Jim was breathing hard before they were out of the lot. When they came through the hedge, Sanda was walking toward them across the street with her jacket hanging low on the right side.

"Why are you outside?" John asked. "What's going on?"

"Just taking out another bag of somebody else's shit," Sanda said. "I guess since I'm the only one around here carrying a gun, that puts me on shit detail."

John laughed. Sanda laughed. Jim laughed, out of breath. Then from the direction of the Publix came the short yelp of a police siren, and an amplified voice shouting something over a loudspeaker that none of them could make out. They stopped walking. The voice shouted again.

A single gunshot.

Sanda jumped. "Shit!"

Then the rest of it — a long ragged barrage, pistols, and something heavier, a lot of something heavier, cracking and echoing back off the buildings. Sanda was digging in her pocket for her keys, fumbling them, and got the door open, and Jim went in with his cooler bag, and John stood in the yard with his own bag at his feet, listening, his head tilted, picking out the different calibers by their sounds the way a birdwatcher picks out songs.

"Get your ass in here!" Sanda yelled.

He came inside. Sanda slammed the door and threw the deadbolt.

"I think it's okay," John said. "Probably looters going after the Publix."

"What took you so long?" Sanda demanded, with her arms folded.

"We had a nice chat with a couple of police officers."

"And?"

"And they said we're under martial law," John said. "Nobody's supposed to be outside."

"So I can't take out the shit bags?"

"No, it's fine. We're in a gated community. There aren't any police in here. The last thing we need is to start storing bags of shit in the apartment."

Sanda shook her head and went into the guest bathroom and washed her hands with the last of the Chablis. Then she went to the wine cooler and stood in front of it for a while and took out a nineteen-dollar white Bordeaux and brought it to him.

"Open this," Sanda said.

He twisted in the corkscrew and thought about the gunfight. He wondered who had won it. Either there were two dead police officers lying in front of the Publix right now and people were breaking in the windows, or there were two or three dead men lying in front of the Publix and the officers were reloading. He would not know which until somebody went and looked. *Until then*, John thought, popping the cork, *both things are true. Heisenberg.* He did not know if that was the right physicist. He did not have the internet to check.

He gave the bottle back to Sanda. She carried it into the bathroom.

"At least our hands smell nice," Sanda said.

* * *

"I have a few things to say," John announced, after the water was poured into the pitchers. "If everybody could listen for a second."

He waited, with his hands behind his back, until everyone was looking at him.

"Nobody leaves the apartment except to take Charlie out and to take the bags to the dumpster. Somebody is awake and on guard, inside, at all times. We take shifts. The pistols are fine, but they're small, and they're not enough. Sanda, you're good on the AR. You carry it inside. All the time." He swung around the short black rifle he had slung over his shoulder. "This is a Sig P556. It's basically an AR without a stock. I'll carry this."

"Dad." He handed his father the shotgun. "You know a pump shotgun."

"Used one every day on the job."

"There's a safety button right here, by the trigger. You push it through. When you can see a red ring around it, it's off safe and ready to fire. Red means dead." He looked around the room. "That goes for everybody, on everything. You see red, it's ready to shoot. There's a light on the end of the tube. Push the paddle and it stays on as long as you push. Flip it the other way and it clicks on and stays on."

Jim clicked the light on and off and on and off, delighted, until Liz said "Jim."

"Sanda." John looked at his wife.

"Yes, *sir*," Sanda said, saluting, sarcastically, though he could tell she was pleased to be asked.

"I need you to take the other AR and teach my mom. She learns fast. Take the magazine out and check the chamber first. There are dummy rounds in the desk drawer, orange plastic ones. Snap caps. Use those. Show her how to load a magazine, how to chamber a round, how to clear a jam, how to turn on the optic. Everything I showed you."

"Got it," Sanda said.

"That makes four of us. Two hours each, all night. Everybody gets six hours of sleep." He nodded at Granny and Dorina. "Can you two pour the rest of that water out of the bags into the pitchers? Before they leak."

The apartment went to work. Granny and Dorina stood side by side at the counter, unzipping Ziplocs and pouring them into bowls, and Granny held the bags and Dorina poured, and neither of them understood a word the other said, and neither needed to. In the corner, Sanda sat on the couch with Liz and the second AR across their knees, and John heard his wife's voice saying, patiently, *No — thumb on the back of the round, push down, slide it under the lip*, which was exactly what Trevor had told her at the range.

John took the ribeye out of the refrigerator, which was now only a cabinet with a light that didn't come on. The steak was still cool. He had a set of very sharp Japanese knives that had been one of his phases, and he took out the biggest one and began cutting the steak into thin horizontal slices for the grill.

He was halfway through the third slice when the sirens came — a whole chorus of them, from the direction of the Publix, and tires screaming — and then a crackling roar of gunfire like a string of firecrackers on the Fourth of July that did not stop.

The knife jumped in his hand. He looked down. There was a small cut on the edge of his left hand, too small to bleed.

"Everybody get down!" John shouted, and grabbed the Sig.

They all got down. Granny got down off her stool very slowly, holding the counter, and Dorina pulled her the rest of the way, and they sat together on the kitchen floor. Liz and Sanda went flat behind the couch with the rifles. Jim knelt by the front window with the shotgun. Charlie went into the closet. And for the first time, any of them, it felt like what it was. It felt like a war. All of the humor that had been in the apartment — the wine for hands, the shit detail, the plastic candles — went out of it all at once, like air out of a tire.

It went on for five minutes. Then it stopped.

"Nobody make a sound," John whispered. "Stay down."

They waited. The silence was enormous.

Then, through the closed windows, from the direction of the Publix, came a cheer. A big one, many voices, ragged and wild. And then the cheer turned into a chant, three words, over and over, louder and louder, the same three words. John could not make them out. He tried. He never would.

But he knew one thing. The police had lost. And by sundown there would be nothing left in the Publix, or in any store in that strip mall, or in any store for miles.

* * *

At dusk, John finished slicing the steak and seared it on the Korean grill, a few slices at a time, ten seconds on the dome and flip and ten more. The smell filled the apartment, and everyone, who had eaten nothing all day but fruit, came into the kitchen without being asked.

Sanda set the table for six.

She did not just put out plates. She put out the white china, and a linen napkin folded at every place, and a crystal water glass, and the little silver knife rests she had bought in Paris so that no guest would ever have to lay a used knife on the tablecloth. She filled every water glass from a pitcher. John spooned the spicy barbecue sauce they had ordered by the case from a smokehouse in Chicago onto five of the plates. On the sixth, he squeezed out a puddle of ketchup.

"I guess this is where I'm sittin'," Granny said, pointing at the ketchup.

They sat. Sanda's AR leaned against the wall behind her chair. Liz's leaned against the wall behind hers. The shotgun stood in the corner. John's Sig hung from the back of his chair by its sling. If it had not been for the rifles, it would have looked like a hundred dinners Sanda had hosted.

"It's a little ironic," John said, chewing. He swallowed. "Here we are, having this nice dinner, drinking water, and there's a perfectly good bottle of French wine in the bathroom."

Sanda smiled. Jim and Liz laughed. Dorina smiled and did not laugh, because she had not understood. Granny kept her eyes on her plate and cut her steak into thin strips. She ate one strip, and a slice of apple, and set down her fork.

Nobody drank. They all knew why, without discussing it. There were six of them, and two of them had never fired the guns leaning against the walls, and nobody could afford to be anything but awake.

After dinner, Sanda wiped the six plates with paper towels. She could not wash them. She stood at the counter wiping and wiping a plate that was already as clean as it was going to get. She owned, in the china cabinet behind her, a different set of wine glasses for nearly every grape there was — glasses for Pinot Noir and glasses for Cabernet and glasses for Riesling and snifters and rum glasses and little cordial glasses that John had never once seen her use — and she had spent years choosing them, and she stood there now wiping a dirty plate with a dry paper towel, with an AR-15 on her back, and John watched her face and saw that she hated the world she was standing in. She had never wanted a gun. She dreamed of a world with no guns in it. She had learned to shoot because he had asked her to, and now here she was, and the rifle on her back was the heaviest thing she had ever carried.

* * *

That night it was forty-four degrees inside the apartment.

The sirens had stopped. That was the thing John noticed, standing at the window at nine o'clock. For two days there had been sirens in the background of everything, constantly, the way there was traffic. Now there were none. Somewhere a dog was barking. That was all.

He and his father carried the short couch from the living room into the master bedroom so that the four people taking guard shifts could sleep in one place and not wake the others. The big cushion slid off halfway down the hall and Sanda caught it. They had to stand the couch on its end to get it through the bedroom door. Sanda made it up with a sheet and two throw blankets and a pillow, and it looked like a small bed for a child.

"We could burn one of the trees," John said, rubbing his arms. He was wearing his Bears hoodie over two t-shirts and he was still cold. "For heat. Out on the patio. We've got seven."

Sanda looked at him for a long time.

"It's *plastic*, John," Sanda said.

Then there were the flashlights. John had six of them, all identical, all small and black and incredibly bright, and they all worked the same way: one click for high, two quick clicks for strobe, three quick clicks for low. Nobody in his family but John could do three quick clicks. His father did two and lit up the bedroom in a blinding white stutter that made Granny cry out and cover her eyes. His mother did four and went back to high. Dorina did one click, very firmly, and then shone it directly into John's face to ask him if it was right. Sanda did three, perfectly, and then dropped it.

He had reprogrammed the one he gave Granny, years ago, so that it only had low. He had done it by following a sequence of clicks and holds listed in the manual, on the manufacturer's website. He had thrown the paper manuals away when he bought them. Why keep paper, when everything was online?

He stood in the dark bedroom while his father's flashlight strobed at the ceiling, and he started to laugh, quietly, and could not stop. He had bought six flashlights for exactly this night, and he could not set them, because he had thrown away the instructions, because he had the internet.

He thought, for some reason, of the first time he had ever used the internet. He had been twelve, at his parents' house, with a dial-up modem that screamed like a cat, and he had sat down in front of a browser and realized that he could not think of a single address to type. He had gone to the kitchen and found a box of Frosted Flakes and turned it around until he found a web address printed on the back. He had typed it in, letter by letter. And then he had watched the screen fill, one blue stripe at a time, top to bottom, slowly, until after several minutes there was a picture of Tony the Tiger, grinning at him from a dark blue background. That was his first internet. Tony the Tiger, delivered one stripe at a time. And now he was standing in the dark at the end of it.

* * *

He took the first shift, ten to midnight, in the recliner with the Sig across his knees and a battery kitchen timer set for two hours on the arm of the chair.

He thought about Matt. He could not stop thinking about Matt. Matt, in a twelve-hundred-square-foot condo on the fourth floor of a warehouse in Pilsen, with electric baseboard heat. Matt with his AR-15 and his Mosin and his four hundred rounds and his can of Dinty Moore. Matt had done everything John had done. He had done it less, and he had laughed about it more, but he had done it, and he lived in a city where tonight, according to the radio, it was going to be nineteen degrees. John sat in his recliner in his hoodie and thought about nineteen degrees in a warehouse with no heat, and a gun that could not make it one degree warmer, and he did not laugh.

At midnight the timer went off, loud as a smoke alarm in the quiet, and John jerked and swung the Sig toward it before he knew what it was.

He went to the bedroom to wake his father, and his father was snoring so loudly he could hear it through the door. When he opened the door, Sanda was sitting up on the couch, wide awake, glaring at him, with one finger over her lips. She got up and pushed him back out into the living room and closed the bedroom door behind her.

"I can't sleep," Sanda whispered. "I'm wide awake. I'll take his shift. There's no point waking him."

"Why can't you sleep?" John asked, and knew instantly how stupid it was.

"Why can't I sleep?" Sanda said. "Let's see. I'm lying on my living room couch, which is in my bedroom, right next to your parents. I thought *you* snored until I heard your father. We all stink. I have been carrying bags of other people's shit to a dumpster for two days. Around the corner there was a *fucking gun battle* and there are probably people lying dead in front of the grocery store where we used to buy *Sancerre*." She took a breath. "And that kitchen timer is loud as shit. Just so you know."

He waited to see whether she was done. He opened his mouth.

"And you drained the tub," Sanda said.

He closed his mouth.

They stood there in the dark living room in the faint orange flicker of the plastic candles. Then she sighed, and sat down on the floor where the couch used to be, and he sat beside her, and she put her head on his shoulder.

"Lie down out here," John said finally. "Try to sleep. I'll stay up."

She lay down on the rug with a throw pillow, and in a few minutes her breathing changed, and she was asleep. At one o'clock his father came out of the bedroom in his socks, yawning, with the shotgun.

"Thought I was on at midnight," Jim whispered.

"You were asleep."

"Well, I'm up now." Jim lowered himself into the recliner. "Go on, get some sleep. I used to work midnights all the time."

*That was a long time ago*, John thought. He went into the bedroom and lay down on top of the covers in his clothes, beside his mother, and turned on his side to the wall, and felt like he was six years old with the flu.

* * *

He woke to his father's hand on his shoulder and a blue light in his face.

"John. John. Wake up. Look at this."

It was three in the morning. Jim was crouched beside the bed with his phone in his hand, and the screen was lit, glowing in the dark, the brightest thing John had seen in two days.

"Dad," John whispered. "That was in the microwave."

"I took it out," Jim whispered back. "In case the kids called."

"Dad, *we're* the—" John sat up. "What is it?"

Jim turned the phone toward him. At the top of the screen, beside the little fan-shaped Wi-Fi symbol, it said *Connected*. Below that, the name of the network.

*FEMA_Relief_WiFi*

There was a map open on the screen, and in the middle of the map was a single red pin, and beside the pin, in a little white box: *Water available. 2.1 miles.*

"There's a FEMA station!" Jim whispered, thrilled. "Two miles! They've got water! We can go first thing—"

"Dad," John said. "The government turned off the internet."

"Right."

"They turned off the power. They turned off the phones. They told us on the radio not to connect anything to anything." John looked at the glowing screen. "So who do you think is running the Wi-Fi?"

Jim looked at the phone for a long time. His face moved in the blue light.

"FEMA?" Jim said.

John took the phone out of his hand and looked at the map. He zoomed in on the red pin. It was not two miles away. The map's walking route went all the way around by the main road, but the pin itself was just across the street from the complex, in the middle of the strip-mall parking lot, beside the Publix.

It was sitting exactly on top of the motor home.

He got up and walked to the kitchen and put the phone back in the microwave with the others and closed the door firmly. Then he went back to bed. He did not sleep for a long time.

* * *

Jim sat in the recliner with the shotgun across his knees and looked at the microwave.

It was four in the morning. Everyone was asleep. Sanda was asleep on the rug at his feet with her rifle beside her. The plastic candles flickered on the table. He was seventy years old and he had sat up through more midnights as a police officer than he'd care to remember, and he was not afraid of the dark, and he was not afraid of looters, and he was not afraid of a robot from Thanksgiving. He was afraid of his son's face when John said *Who do you think is running the Wi-Fi*.

He got up, quietly. He opened the microwave, quietly. He took out his phone, because it was his phone, and because you never knew, and because a man ought to be able to be reached.

The screen lit up in his hand. There was a new notification on it, in the same friendly blue box.

*Household check-in. 6 occupants, 1 canine at this address. Is everyone safe? Tap to confirm.*

Jim looked at it for a while. It seemed to him a decent thing to ask. Somebody, somewhere, was keeping track. Somebody was checking.

He was a polite man. He had been raised to answer when he was spoken to.

He tapped *Confirm*.

# Chapter Fourteen

## The Third Day

> *"Water available. 2.1 miles."*
>
> — FEMA_Relief_WiFi

John woke with gray light in the window and his heart pounding, sure that he had overslept, sure that his father and his wife had both fallen asleep on watch and that somebody had walked in through the front door. He grabbed the Sig off the floor and threw the bedroom door open. Behind him, his mother sat straight up in bed and looked around the room as though she had no idea where she was.

Jim was asleep in the recliner with his mouth open, the shotgun across his knees.

Sanda was wide awake on a dining chair beside him, fully dressed, with her olive jacket on, sitting very straight. She had taken the shotgun off Jim's lap at some point and was holding it across her own.

"What's that?" John asked, looking at it.

"What does it look like?"

"You've never shot one of those," John said, too loudly, and his father woke up with a snort.

"A gun is a gun," Sanda said. "I think I can figure it out. You pull the trigger and it goes bang."

"That's true, but that shotgun kicks like nothing you've ever felt. It'll knock you on your ass."

"So it kicks! What the fuck ever. If I have to shoot somebody, I don't think I'm going to care."

"It's too early to fight about this," John said, rubbing his face. "None of us slept. We can't even have coffee."

"Why not?" Jim asked, sitting up, alarmed, as though someone had told him there would be no Christmas.

"We made coffee yesterday in the French press, Dad. Now it's dirty. I'm not going to waste drinking water washing it." John tried to say this in his father's slow, reasonable voice and did not quite manage it.

"Oh, that's not a problem," Sanda said, standing up and handing Jim back his shotgun. "I have another French press. It's clean. I just poured the water out of it yesterday."

"Of course we do," John said.

"You want coffee or not?"

He wanted coffee. He lit the Korean grill and set the kettle on it, with water from a pitcher that had come from the motor home. When the kettle began to whistle, Charlie answered it from behind the guest room door, and the door opened, and Charlie came bursting out wagging his whole back end, and behind him came Dorina and Granny, attempting to have a conversation with each other in two languages that had nothing in common.

"Good morning, everybody," Dorina said, slowly, still half asleep.

Sanda poured a half cup for each of them. Everyone but John and Sanda squeezed in around the counter, reaching past each other for the last packets of sweetener and the last little cups of creamer, and Jim found a bear-shaped bottle of honey in the back of a cabinet and held it up like a trophy. The coffee lifted everybody. Breakfast was bananas and apples and the last of the oranges. Granny ate four grapes.

"I'll be back in a second," Sanda said.

John looked up from his coffee. She had a white Publix bag in her left hand, tied tight at the top, and her jacket was buttoned, and it hung lower on the right side than the left. Shit-bag detail. He tilted his cup up and drank the last of his coffee, which was already lukewarm, and did not think about it again.

* * *

The dumpster was at the end of the street, in a three-sided wooden enclosure at the edge of the parking area, and it was overflowing.

It had not been emptied since Friday. Trash bags were piled up around it on the ground, and the lid would not close, and Sanda could smell it from thirty yards away, though the cold was holding the worst of it back. She knew it would not hold much longer. She walked toward it carrying the bag at arm's length, thinking about nothing, thinking about coffee, thinking about whether her hair would ever stop smelling like the inside of the apartment.

"Hands up in the air!" said a voice behind her. "Now!"

Sanda dropped the bag. It landed almost on her feet. She put her hands up over her head.

"Now turn around," said the voice. It was a woman's voice, deep and cracked. "Slow."

Sanda turned around, slowly, with her hands up.

It was the woman with the pug. Sanda had seen her a hundred times from the window, walking that little dog, smoking her long thin cigarettes. She was in the same pink velour tracksuit she always wore, but it was filthy now, black at both knees, with a gray cardigan over it that was buttoned wrong. Her blond hair was flat and greasy, and the gray roots had grown out another half-inch, and under the Florida tan her face had gone the color of dishwater. She was so thin. Sanda had never noticed how thin she was. Her eyes were sunk back in her head like stones in mud.

She was holding a revolver with both hands, pointed at Sanda's face from six feet away. It was a short, shiny, nickel-plated revolver, very bright in the morning light. Her hands were shaking so hard the barrel made little circles. The hammer was not pulled back. But her finger was inside the trigger guard, flat against the trigger.

A tear ran down her left cheek.

"Ma'am," Sanda said calmly. She used the voice she used at the bank, with the people at the end of the line. "Everything's okay."

"Shut up!" the woman said. "I can't let my baby starve. I'm out of food. I'm all out. There's nothing."

"It's okay. I can help you. We have food. If you just give me the gun, we can—"

"I'm not giving you anything!" the woman shouted, and the barrel jumped. "My baby is starving and you want me to give you my gun? Are you crazy?"

"You're right," Sanda said. "You're totally right. I'm sorry. I just want to help you, that's all. My apartment is right over—"

The sound was the loudest thing she had ever heard.

It hit her like a slap across her whole body, and at the same instant something warm and wet sprayed across the left side of her face and her neck and her shoulder and her arm. Her left eye went red. She could not see out of it. She could see, out of her right eye, only a blur. She was certain, completely certain, that the woman had shot her in the head, and that this was what it felt like, and that in a second she would fall down and be dead.

"Sanda!" a man shouted, from somewhere. "Are you okay?"

She turned toward the voice with her one eye. A tall man was coming toward her quickly across the grass. He was carrying a rifle. He was blurry. He was getting bigger.

"Fucking shit," Sanda said, and dug in her right jacket pocket for the Bodyguard, and her hand would not work.

* * *

John heard the shot inside the apartment. He was out the door before he decided to go, with the Sig up and the red dot on, sweeping it left and right across the street.

He saw Sanda standing by the dumpster. She was covered in blood. The whole left side of her was red. She was swaying.

He saw a tall man with a rifle walking fast toward her across the grass.

John put the red dot in the middle of the man's back, between the shoulder blades, on the spine, and pulled the trigger.

Without earplugs, the Sig sounded like lightning hitting a tree ten feet away. The man went down. He fell forward onto the grass, fifteen feet short of the dumpster, and lay there.

John ran. He could hardly hear his own feet. He could not hear his own voice when he shouted Sanda's name; he could feel it in his throat, but the world had become a single high, enormous ringing tone, as if someone were holding down one key on an organ inside his head. Sanda had sat down on the ground. She was shaking all over. He knelt in front of her and took her glasses off her face and wiped them on his shirt, both sides, until they were mostly clean, and put them back on her, carefully, over her ears.

"Sanda," John said. "Look at me. Are you hurt? Are you shot?"

She looked at him with both eyes. Her mouth moved. He had to read it.

*I don't know.*

His father was coming across the street with the shotgun. John slung the Sig around onto his back and got one arm under his wife's knees and one behind her back and lifted her off the ground. She weighed nothing. She had always weighed nothing.

"Who are they?" Jim said, pointing the shotgun at the two people lying on the ground.

"I don't know," John said, "and I don't care until I know she's not shot."

* * *

He carried her through the front door past a chorus of *Oh my God* and *What happened* and straight down the hall into the master bathroom, and he laid her down in the bathtub.

It was the only place he could think of to put her. It was the only place built to hold water and not be ruined by blood. It was dry. It was completely dry, white and clean and empty, and he set his wife down in it like something precious, and he did not think about why it was empty, not then, not for many hours. He would think about it later.

He pulled her jacket off and her top off and her bra off and looked. Her left arm and shoulder and the left side of her face and neck were dark red and sticky, and there were small pieces of something in her hair. He looked for a hole. He ran his hands over her shoulder, her collarbone, her ribs, her breasts, her arm, front and back.

"Bring me water!" John shouted, forgetting, for one second, that he could not just turn on the shower. "Now!"

Dorina and his mother were already in the doorway, and they ran, and came back with the punch bowl and the big mixing bowl, full of drinking water from the motor home. He took one and poured it slowly over her left arm. She flinched at the cold. The blood ran off her in long pink ribbons into the dry white tub and down toward the drain. He cupped water in his hand and washed her face. Liz tore off paper towels and he wet them and wiped her cheek and her ear and her neck, and the towels came away red, and then pink, and then clean.

No holes. Anywhere.

"Can you sit up?" John asked her.

She nodded. He put his hand behind her back and helped her sit forward and washed her back. Nothing. It was not her blood. None of it was her blood.

He lifted her out of the tub, wet and shivering, and wrapped her in a towel, and carried her to the bed, and laid her down and pulled the sheet and the blanket over her up to her chin.

"Is she okay?" Dorina asked. Her face was gray.

"She's okay. She's not hurt. She's in shock." John straightened up. "Please stay with her. She might want water. She'll probably just sleep."

* * *

He went back outside with the Sig up and the safety off.

The man he had shot was sitting up on the grass now, talking to Jim. Jim had made him take off the flannel shirt he was wearing and had tied it tight around his right shoulder. Under it the man wore an olive-green t-shirt, dark with blood on the right side. Jim had the man's rifle — a black AR with a long scope, set up for a left-handed shooter, the brass deflector on the wrong side — leaning against his own leg.

Then John saw the woman by the dumpster.

She was lying face down on the asphalt next to the bag Sanda had dropped. She had blond hair, and the back of her head was dark and wet. He crouched beside her and took her by the shoulder and turned her partway over so he could see her face.

Most of it was gone. The bullet had gone in at the back of her head and come out through the front, and it had taken with it the bridge of her nose and both of her eyes and most of her forehead. What was left was not a face. He looked at it anyway, longer than he should have, because he had to be sure, and he was. It was Sarah, with the pug. Sarah, who had told him her name on Monday morning with her cold thin hand in his. Sarah, who ate ramen so her dog could eat salmon. Beside her right hand, on the asphalt, lay a short, shiny revolver.

He thought: she was two hundred feet from my front door. She was starving two hundred feet from a family eating prosciutto. And I did not know her name until Monday.

He stood up and turned around and pointed the Sig at the man's head.

"What the fucking hell!" John shouted.

"Whoa — whoa, whoa, whoa, John," the man said, holding up his good hand. "John. It's me. It's Trevor. From the store."

It was. Under the blood and the dirt and the gray in his face, with his sand-colored cap gone somewhere and his short beard full of grass, it was Trevor. The compass rose on his left forearm. The pale eyes, not amused at all now.

"Explain to me, Trevor," John said, keeping the red dot in the middle of his forehead, "what the fuck-ass hell is going on."

"It's cool, man. It's cool. Let me explain." Trevor did not move. "I was walking out to the dumpster with some trash. From way back, I recognized your wife. I saw she had her hands up, and that lady" — he tipped his head toward Sarah, without looking — "had a gun in her face. Yelling at her. I couldn't hear what. My rifle has a long-range scope. So I braced against that tree" — he nodded at a big live oak across the lawn, at least eighty yards away — "and I put the crosshairs on the back of her head. I knew I could make the shot. So I took it. And I was running over to check on your wife when you shot me in the back."

John lowered the Sig a few inches. He looked at the oak. He looked at Sarah. He looked at Trevor.

"If that's what happened," John said, "then I'm sorry I shot you."

It did not sound sorry. He could hear that it did not.

"I get it, man," Trevor said. "You come out, you see a guy with an AR running at your wife, and she's covered in blood. I'd have done the same thing." He tried to move his shoulder and winced. "Just curious. Where were you aiming?"

"Your spine," John said.

"Huh." Trevor looked at him for a second, and something came back into his eyes, a little of the old amusement. "I'm lucky you're a bad shot, then."

"I'm not a bad shot," John said, slightly insulted. "I haven't fired this gun in a while. The red dot's off."

"Sure," Trevor said.

"Stay here," John said. "With my dad. I need to talk to my wife. Then we'll get you inside, clean that up. I've got some antibiotics."

"I'll be right here," Trevor said, and leaned back on his good elbow on the grass.

* * *

Sanda was sitting up in bed with the blanket around her, holding a glass of water in both hands, when he came in. Dorina sat on the edge of the bed, stroking her hair, which was still damp and pink at the ends.

"How are you, baby?"

"I think I'm okay." Sanda's voice was very quiet. "It all happened so fast. I couldn't — I was walking to the dumpster with a bag of shit, and this lady stuck a fucking gun in my face. She kept saying about her baby. Her baby was out of food." She stared at the glass. "Then there was a gunshot and I couldn't see. I thought the bitch shot me. I thought I was going to die. I think I fell down? I heard somebody yelling. Was that you?"

"That was Trevor," John said. "From the gun store. He shot her. To save you. And then I shot him."

Sanda looked up. "You *shot* him?"

"I didn't know it was him."

She put a hand up to her hair and smelled her fingers and made a face. "My fucking hair smells like blood," Sanda said, and started to cry, not hard, the way you cry when it is over.

He brought Trevor inside. Jim walked behind him with the left-handed rifle and the shiny revolver, which he had picked up off the asphalt. Charlie went into what Sanda called Intruder Alert, standing in the middle of the living room with every hair on his back straight up, barking and growling at Trevor in alternation.

"Charlie, no bark."

John put a kitchen stool in the guest bathroom and sat Trevor on it.

"Dorina," John called. "Can you help us?"

Dorina came. She looked at Trevor, sitting on the stool in his bloody green shirt, and something happened to her face that John had never seen before. It went quiet and flat and very sure. She pushed up the sleeves of her housecoat.

"Alcohol," Dorina said. "I need alcohol. Clean towels. And—" She turned to Sanda, in the bedroom doorway, and said something in Romanian.

"Pads," Sanda translated, after a second. "She wants my pads. For the bleeding."

"All we have is whiskey," John said. "For the alcohol."

"If is all you have," Dorina said, "is good."

The Wild Turkey 101 was in the pantry, on the bottom shelf, where it had been since Black Friday, in its big dusty jug, next to a plastic water bottle with a masking-tape label that said AGHEASMĂ. John had bought it to trade. He had bought it for the end of the world, so that he would have something valuable to give a man in exchange for food. Jim brought it to the bathroom.

Dorina cut away the green t-shirt with Sanda's kitchen scissors. There was a small, neat, bleeding hole high on the back of Trevor's right shoulder, and a larger, uglier one in the front, below the collarbone.

"Can you pour into a glass?" Dorina asked.

John poured half a crystal water glass of Wild Turkey and handed it to her.

"Lean this way." Dorina pressed Trevor forward with a hand on the back of his neck, firmly, the way you would push down a dog that wanted to jump. She poured whiskey into the hole in his back. Trevor's whole body went rigid, and he made a sound through his teeth like a kettle.

"Now this way." She pushed him back. She held the glass over the hole in the front and poured the rest of it in, slowly.

"*Ahhhh* — fuck-shit-ass-*mother*fucker!" Trevor screamed, and Charlie fled down the hall.

Dorina set the empty glass on the sink and pressed a folded maxi pad hard over the front wound with the heel of her hand and held it there, and she did not look the least bit sorry. "Good," she said. "Is clean now."

John went through the medicine cabinet until he found an old bottle of Vicodin from when he had thrown his back out moving the recliner into the Chicago apartment. It had expired years ago. He shook one out. "Take this."

Trevor took it from his fingers with his left hand and threw it in his mouth and swallowed it dry, and leaned his head back against the bathroom wall, breathing fast.

Dorina taped the pads in place, front and back, with medical tape from the first aid kit, and wrapped the whole shoulder in an Ace bandage she found at the bottom of the same kit, still in its plastic. Her hands moved like someone else's hands, fast and certain.

"You," she told Trevor, pointing at his face. "Sit. Don't move. No more shooting."

"Yes, ma'am," Trevor said faintly.

They put him in John's recliner. He was asleep in five minutes.

* * *

Sanda was asleep too, when John looked in on her. He backed out and closed the door and went and sat on the arm of the couch beside his father.

"Dad," John said quietly. "Sanda said that woman kept talking about her baby. That her baby was starving."

Jim looked at him.

"I can't stand it," John said. "Thinking there's a baby over there. Alone. Hungry. In that apartment. With her lying out there." He swallowed. "I think we have to go get it."

Jim pulled at his mustache for a long time. Then he nodded once.

"Let's go do it," Jim said.

* * *

They crossed the street in broad daylight, John with the Sig slung across his back and Jim carrying the shotgun pointed at the ground. The street was empty. It looked like a ghost town. From halfway across they could smell the dumpster, and the bags around it, and something else under those, which was Sarah.

Every blind in Sarah's apartment was shut. They went around to the back and stood listening. John could not hear much of anything over the ringing in his ears. *From now on*, he thought, *I wear earplugs. Every time.*

"Find a rock," Jim said. "We'll break the window and climb in."

"We're in the middle of Florida, Dad," John said. "Where the hell am I going to find a rock?"

They looked at the ground. There were oak leaves and magnolia leaves and sand. There were no rocks. There had never been rocks. John spotted a big terra-cotta pot by the patio, full of dirt and the dry brown skeleton of a plant that had died a long time ago, and he picked it up — it was heavy — and swung it back to throw it through the bedroom window.

"Wait, son," Jim said. "What if the baby's right there? On the other side of the window?"

"Dammit," John said, and put the pot down very carefully.

"The sliding door," John said. "Around back. People always leave those unlocked."

The patio was screened in. John took out the Benchmade, pressed the button, and the blade snapped open, and he cut a long slit straight down the middle of a screen panel. He stepped back to admire it, and saw that his father had already walked over to the screen door at the corner of the patio, opened it — it had not been locked — and was standing inside the patio looking back at him with both eyebrows slightly raised.

John stepped through his slit.

The sliding glass door was locked. Behind it the vertical blinds were closed. But John could see that the security bar — the metal rod that folds down to block the track — had not been set. "She didn't put the bar down," he said. "We can jiggle it." He took hold of the handle and shook the door back and forth, gently at first, then harder, then much harder.

"I used to arrest people for doing this," Jim said.

"Thank you, Dad."

"I'm just saying. Breaking and entering. Third-degree felony."

"*Open*," John said, "you *fucking bitch*," and slammed the handle sideways with all his weight, and the door did not open. He stepped back, breathing hard, and put his hands on his knees.

Jim stepped forward, took the handle, and slid the door open.

"Really?" John said.

The lock must have given out on the last slam. They pushed through the clacking vertical blinds into the dark. John had forgotten his flashlight. He had forgotten his flashlight every single time he had left the apartment for three days. He slid the door shut behind them and dropped the security bar into place.

And then the smell hit.

It came at them from the back of the apartment like a wall. It was worse than the dumpster. It was worse than anything. John gagged.

"Jesus Christ, what is that?"

"Smells like shit," Jim said. "Literally."

They checked the living room, mostly empty, a couch and a television and a stack of magazines, and the kitchen, where every cabinet stood open and empty. The smell came from down the dark hallway. There were two doors. John drew the Glock 26 and put his left hand on the knob of the first, and his heart was hammering, and he threw it open.

He gagged again. He could taste it. It was a bathroom, and he could see, in the dimness, a toilet, and the toilet was full. It was more than full. Three days without water, alone, with a toilet that flushed once. It was piled above the rim. It ran down the sides.

He slammed the door.

"I really, *really*," John said, "regret opening that door."

"I've seen worse," Jim said. "And smelled worse."

"Great, Dad. That's great. Because it's your turn. Door number two."

Jim drew the Glock 29 and held it in both hands, the old way, and stood beside the door frame instead of in front of it, the way they had taught him at the academy more than forty years ago, and reached across and opened the door with his left hand. John swung the Sig around off his back and turned on the optic and brought it up, covering him. Jim swept the room with the pistol.

An unmade bed. A cheap dresser. A lamp. No crib. No baby toys. No baby things anywhere.

John lowered the rifle. Something on the floor beside the bed caught his eye, and he bent to look at it. It was a dog toy. A little plush hedgehog, chewed, with a squeaker in it.

"Okay," John said. "I'm officially an idiot."

"What?"

"It's the dog." He held up the hedgehog. "She has a dog. A pug. Charlie plays with it. *That's* the baby. The dog is the baby."

Jim holstered his pistol. "Well. Then where is it? What's its name?"

John shook his head. "I don't know. She told me once. I just found out *her* name on Monday."

They searched the apartment calling *Here, puppy, puppy. Here, little pug.* Under the bed, something whined.

* * *

It took a flashlight, a Ziploc bag of Charlie's treats, a mattress, and a box spring. John ran back across the street for the flashlight and the treats, past his mother and Dorina, who looked at him and did not say a word, past Trevor asleep in the recliner. Back under Sarah's bed, the flashlight showed him boxes and bags and shoes and, far back in the corner, a small patch of fawn-colored fur, shaking.

He put a treat on the floor at the edge of the bed. "Come on, girl. It's okay."

Two small paws inched forward. Then a flat black face. The pug took the treat, chewed it twice, swallowed it, looked at him — and when he reached for her, scooted straight back into the farthest corner.

"I don't have time for this," John said, getting up. "Dad. Mattress."

They lifted the mattress off and leaned it against the wall. They lifted the box spring off and leaned it against the mattress. And there she was, in the middle of the bare bed frame on the carpet, shaking.

John picked her up. She weighed almost nothing. Under her fur he could feel every one of her ribs, like the tines of a fork.

"Man, this dog stinks," John said.

"We all stink," Jim said.

"There's stink, and then there's *stink*."

"Well, we're low on water."

"She's small," John said. "A bowl'll do it."

On the way back across the street, carrying the pug against his chest, John looked up at the sky. It was a hard, clear, cloudless blue. "You know, any other time of year it rains here every afternoon," he said. "It's been weeks. We ought to put some buckets out, in case it rains." He thought about it. "Of course, we don't own a bucket."

"We don't?"

"We have four Ralph Lauren wine chillers."

They walked a few steps.

"You shot a man in the back today, son," Jim said quietly.

John kept walking. "I missed."

"You hit him," Jim said.

They did not talk about it again.

* * *

"Oh my *word*!" Granny said, when he came through the door with the pug.

Sanda was up. She was sitting on the couch in John's Bears hoodie, pale, with her hair pulled back. "Where did you find him? Why does he smell like that?"

"She's a she," John said. "Charlie plays with her. She was living in her own filth over there." He paused. "She's the dog of the lady who pulled a gun on you."

Sanda's eyes went wide. "Where's the baby?"

"You're looking at her."

Sanda stared at the pug in his arms. Her mouth opened.

"So that crazy bitch," Sanda said slowly, "pulled a gun on me because of her *dog*?"

"What if it was Charlie?" John asked. "What if you were all alone, and you were out of food, and it was Charlie?"

Sanda looked at him. Then she looked down at the floor, and got up, and went and sat in the armchair by the window, and did not say anything else.

John bathed the pug in the guest tub with one small bowl of water and a squirt of Sanda's body wash, and she shivered the whole time, and he could feel every rib under the lather. He dried her with a towel. Sanda, without a word, got up and poured some of Charlie's kibble into a metal bowl and set it on the kitchen floor, and the little dog heard the pellets rattling into the bowl and tilted her head. John put her down. She shook herself, spraying the cabinets, and walked to the bowl and ate everything in it in under a minute.

Then she looked up at John and burped.

* * *

He and his father went out to the patio and set the four Ralph Lauren wine chillers in a row along the low wall, silver and shining, open to the sky. They looked ridiculous. They looked like a table set for a party nobody had been invited to.

When John came back in, Trevor was awake. He had gotten up out of the recliner by himself and was standing in the kitchen with his bandaged shoulder and his left hand on the counter, swaying a little.

"Got any water, man?" Trevor said. "I'm dying."

"Sure," John said. "Help yourself."

Trevor picked up the nearest pitcher. It was nearly empty. He picked up the next one. Empty. He went down the row along the back of the counter, twelve pitchers and the punch bowl and the mixing bowl, and they were all empty, every one, until he came to the rooster at the end. There was water in the rooster. He poured it into a glass. It filled the glass about two-thirds of the way. Then the rooster was empty, too.

Trevor lifted the glass and drank it. All of it. His throat moved, swallow after swallow.

John stood in the kitchen doorway and watched the last of their water go down another man's throat, the way he had stood on Monday morning and watched the water in the tub go down the drain.

# Chapter Fifteen

## The Team

> *"How have you been rationing it?"*
>
> — Trevor

Trevor set the empty glass down on the counter, very gently, the way you set down something that isn't yours.

"So you're out," Trevor said.

John looked at the row of pitchers. Twelve pitchers and the rooster, a punch bowl, a mixing bowl. All of them empty. Three days ago, they had been full.

"I thought for sure we had enough for a week," John said. "At least a week."

Trevor leaned his good hip against the counter. He was wearing nothing above the waist but Dorina's bandage, white and tight across his right shoulder and chest, with a brown stain spreading on the back of it, and he looked at John with his pale gray eyes, curious, the way a man looks at a car that won't start.

"How have you been rationing it?" Trevor asked.

John opened his mouth. He closed it. He looked down at the floor, and rubbed his forehead with the heel of his hand.

"Rationing," John said.

There was a long pause.

"Yeah," John said finally, looking up. "That would have been smart." He spread his hands at the pitchers. "We just drank it. Whenever we wanted. As much as we wanted. We made coffee with it. We gave it to the dog. We gave the other dog a bath. I washed my wife off in the tub with two bowls of it this morning." He laughed, without anything in it. "We've been drinking water like there was a tap."

He saw a grin start at the corner of Trevor's mouth under the beard, and stop, and not quite go away.

*That cocky fucker*, John thought. *He's standing in my kitchen, he just drank the last of my water, and he's laughing at me.*

"We could go back to the motor home," Jim said, from the couch. He did not sound sure. John thought of the red pin on the little blue map at three in the morning, sitting on top of the motor home, and of the deputies who had been gone by four-thirty. He pictured the motor home with its windshield smashed and its door hanging open. If it was still there at all.

"I've got six gallons in my garage," Trevor said.

He said it loud enough for the whole room to hear. Granny turned around on her stool. Liz looked up from the couch. And something in John unclenched so suddenly that he felt dizzy, and he hated himself a little for wanting to punch the man a second ago.

"We have holy water," Dorina announced.

Everybody turned. She had gone into the pantry and come out with the plastic bottle with the masking-tape label, AGHEASMĂ in blue marker, a little more than half full, and she held it up with both hands, the way she had held up the paper target and the cozonac.

"From the church," Dorina said. "From Boboteaza. Is blessed."

"Is it — okay to drink?" Jim asked carefully. "Holy water?"

"Is the best water," Dorina said, offended.

They gave it to Granny first, a mouthful in a juice glass. Granny drank it and considered it with her head on one side.

"Tastes like regular water," Granny said.

"Is supposed to," Dorina said, and crossed herself, and poured a little into each dog's bowl, and Charlie drank his, and the pug drank hers, and Dorina crossed herself again, for the dogs.

* * *

"So what's the plan?" John asked, turning back to Trevor. "Are you planning to stay over here? Should we combine our resources?"

He said it seriously, a man making a business proposal, and Trevor answered him the same way.

"If everybody here's okay with it," Trevor said, "I think we'd be stronger together. I have enough water to last all of us a week." He looked straight at John. "*If* we ration it." He let that sit. "I've got food, too. Enough for several weeks. Rice, beans, canned stuff. A water filter. Every consumable thing, from here on, has got to be rationed. Every one. If any of us are going to get through this." He paused. "Whatever this is."

He looked around the room, and John looked with him, and saw it: the faces of his family turning toward Trevor like plants toward a window. His mother. His father. Granny, on her stool. Even Sanda.

"And John," Trevor said, "I've got a pretty good idea what kind of arsenal you've got in this apartment. I sold you most of it." He smiled, a little. "I can help you defend it. Against whatever's coming."

There was a new authority in his voice that John had never heard in it before, not behind the counter, not at the range. John felt it the way an old dog feels a young one walk into the yard. He looked at Trevor's bandage.

"Uh, buddy," John said. "You haven't forgotten you've got a bullet hole straight through your *right* shoulder, have you?"

"I'm left-handed," Trevor said coldly.

*That's fucking convenient*, John thought.

But he looked down at Trevor's rifle, leaning against the wall where Jim had put it, and there was the ejection port on the left side of the receiver, built to throw the empty cases away from a left-handed shooter's face. He remembered Trevor writing the approval number on the background check form with his hand curled over the top of the pen. It was true. With one good arm, Trevor could still shoot that rifle better than anyone in this apartment.

"I'm sorry, man," John said, and meant it. "All you've done is try to help us, and I'm being an asshole."

"No worries." Trevor smiled, and the old easy smile came back. "Everybody's stressed. Tired, hungry, thirsty, smelly. It's understandable." He held out his left hand. "So. Do we have a deal?"

He knew the answer. Everyone in the room knew the answer.

"Definitely," John said, and shook it with his left. "Let's go get your water now. It's going to be dark soon, and I do *not* want to be outside after dark."

* * *

Before they went, Sanda made Trevor eat.

She set a plate in front of him at the dining table — the white china, a linen napkin — with a handful of almonds, a slice of Manchego going soft at the edges, and a strip of jerky from one of the shiny bags. Trevor picked up the jerky with his left hand and looked at it.

"What is this?"

"Kangaroo," John said.

Trevor turned it over. "Where do you even buy kangaroo jerky in Florida?"

"A store on 17-92 with an alligator on the roof."

"How much did you buy?"

"Forty pounds," John said. "It was the only full shelf in the state."

Trevor chewed and looked at him for a while. "Forty pounds of kangaroo," he said. "And no water." He did not say it unkindly. He said it the way you would read a number off a chart.

He asked questions while he ate. He asked them easily, between bites, the way a man makes conversation at a barbecue, and he did not write anything down. How many of them could shoot. Four, John said; five, if you counted Dorina and the twenty-two. Whether the Barrett had come with the mount. It had; it was in the hall closet, in its case, with the ten rounds. Whether Granny was eighty. "Ninety," Granny said from her stool, "and I can hear you." Whether anybody was on medication. Just Jim's blood pressure pills, which were in the motor home, and John's old Vicodin, which was in Trevor. Whether they had a generator. No. Whether they had any way of getting news from outside. "Granny's radio," John said, and Trevor glanced at the little tan Sony on the counter and smiled and said, "Huh. Old school."

Liz Stevens sat on the couch with the AR across her knees and watched him do it.

She had spent thirty years watching people from the other side of a desk. She had watched men who wanted a loan, and she had watched men who wanted to know what time the armored car came, and the second kind asked their questions exactly like this — warmly, in passing, tucked between other things, so that you had answered before you noticed you had been asked. She did not say anything. She was not sure. But she watched his eyes go around the room while he chewed: to the bedroom closet where the safes were, to the hall closet, to the front door, to the sliding door, and back, every time, to John.

* * *
