Sanda had gone into the bedroom and come back with one of John's t-shirts, because Trevor could not walk across the complex at dusk in a bandage. It was a gray t-shirt from the Palmetto Grid company picnic two summers ago, an extra-large, with the company's logo across the chest — a stylized palmetto frond with a lightning bolt in the middle — and the words RESTORE: POWER TO THE PEOPLE across the back, which had been Dana's idea. She helped him get it on over the bandage, one arm at a time.

Trevor looked down at the logo on his chest.

"Palmetto Grid," Trevor said. "You work there?"

"I did," John said. "I do. I don't know."

"Huh," Trevor said, and something moved behind his eyes for a second, and then didn't.

"Mom," John said, turning to Liz. "You've got your AR. I need you to protect this place and everybody in it while Dad and I go with Trevor. Ten minutes."

Liz stood up. She swung the AR-15 around off her back on its sling, the way Sanda had shown her yesterday, and brought it up into a low ready with the muzzle at the floor, and set her feet, and her face went perfectly flat and perfectly hard, and for a moment John's mother, in her quilted vest and her Keds and her reading glasses on their beaded chain, looked like somebody who had spent twenty years in the Army.

Across the room, John saw Sanda's face. She was confused, and she was hurt. Yesterday afternoon she had taught Liz how to hold that rifle. Liz had never fired it. And John had just put his mother in charge.

He caught Sanda's eye, and he winked.

Sanda looked at him for a second. Then her face cleared. They had been together since they were fifteen and sixteen years old, and she knew him better than he knew himself, and she knew exactly what he was doing: he was giving his mother something to hold on to. Something to be. Everybody in that room needed something to be, and confidence was the thing they were going to run out of before water.

That was what made him good at this, Sanda thought. The way he looked at a room and saw what everyone in it needed. She hated, a little, that she thought it.

On his way to the door she caught his sleeve and pulled him down the hall into the bedroom, which was code, and pushed the door closed behind them.

"You put me in the tub," Sanda said. "This morning."

"It was the only place I could think of."

"It was dry." She looked up at him in the half dark. "I was lying in there covered in that woman's blood, and I looked down, and it was dry. And I thought, *he drained it*. That was the first thing I thought. Before I even thought I was alive."

"I know," John said. "I'm sorry."

"I know you're sorry." She pushed her glasses up her nose with one knuckle. "Bring back the water, John."

"Six gallons."

"Bring back all six," Sanda said, and opened the bedroom door.

Jim and Trevor were waiting for him in the living room. Granny was sitting at the kitchen counter on her stool. She had been sitting there quietly through all of it, the deal and the jerky and the t-shirt, and nobody had been paying attention to her, which was how Granny was used to it.

In front of her on the counter sat an open can of sardines.

John stopped. He did not know when she had opened it. He did not know who had opened it for her and her hands did not have the strength to peel back the lid. But there it was, the lid peeled back, the little silver fish lying in their oil in a neat row like sleeping children. And beside it, a sleeve of Ritz crackers.

As John watched, Granny took one sardine out of the can with her knotted fingers and laid it on a cracker. She did not look at the sardine. She looked at him.

She ate it.

She chewed it slowly, looking at John the whole time with her pale blue eyes, unblinking, behind her big glasses. She swallowed. She wiped her fingers on a paper towel, one finger at a time.

John said nothing.

Granny said nothing.

It was, he would think later, the whole of their relationship, all ninety and forty-two years of it, and it was perfect.

"You guys ready?" John asked.

Jim and Trevor nodded. The three of them crossed the living room to the front door, past Liz and her rifle. Jim carried the shotgun the way he had carried his shotgun on the job, in one hand, pointed almost straight down at the floor. Trevor had his left-handed rifle slung across his back, so both hands were free. John had the Sig hanging across his belly on its sling. Trevor reached out with his left hand and took hold of the brass doorknob to let them out.

And behind them, the apartment came alive.

Every light came on at once. All of them — every lamp and every overhead in every room, because on Monday morning, in the dark, John had gone through his own apartment flipping every switch to the *on* position to see what was working, and had never flipped them back. Light poured out of the ceiling like water out of a broken pipe. After three days of flashlights and plastic candles it stabbed everybody in the eyes, and they all threw up their arms and squinted and cheered at the same time. The refrigerator shuddered and began to hum. The wine cooler beeped twice. Charlie went off like a fire alarm. From down the hall came a gurgle, and then a hiss, and then the steady, beautiful sound of the toilet tank in the guest bathroom filling.

"I think the water's back on!" John shouted.

He ran to the kitchen sink and threw the faucet handle all the way up. Deep in the wall, there was a roar of air, getting louder, as though something were coming up the pipe toward him from far away. The faucet spat — a rusty brown cough — and then hissed — and then, all at once, a thick, clear, glorious column of water came gushing out of it into the sink.

Everybody was clapping. Granny was clapping. Liz had let the AR hang and was clapping. Sanda had her hands over her mouth. *It's over*, John thought. *Three days. Thank God. Three days of hell and it's over.*

He did not clap. He leaned back against the wall beside the sink, and he watched the water pour out of the faucet and down the drain. He watched it. A clear, unending, effortless stream of it, the way water had come out of every faucet he had ever touched in forty-two years. He stood there and watched it go.

On the wall, the television turned itself on.

Nobody had touched it. The remote was on the coffee table. The screen lit up blue, and then white, and in the middle of it appeared a seal: a circle, with an eagle in it, its wings spread, holding something in its claws, and around the edge of the circle in small capitals, UNITED STATES EMERGENCY RESTORATION AUTHORITY. It looked like the government. It looked exactly like the government. John had never heard of the United States Emergency Restoration Authority.

Below the seal, letters appeared, white on blue, as though someone were typing them.

*RESTORATION IN PROGRESS.*

*Keep devices powered to assist.*

On the counter by the wine cooler, the cooler's little display, which had shown the temperature in red numbers every day of its life, now said *OPTIMIZING*. On the microwave, the clock came on and began to blink *12:00*, *12:00*, *12:00*. And inside the microwave, behind the dark glass door, in the pile of phones, every one of them buzzed at once — a long, low, angry hum, like a hive. In the back pocket of his father's jeans, something buzzed too. Jim put his hand flat over the pocket and looked up at the ceiling lights, smiling. Nobody noticed but Granny.

John looked at the television.

He looked at the letters. He looked at them the way he had once looked at the two contradictory states of a substation switch, for three days, until he understood. He looked at the R in *RESTORATION*. It had a little spur on its leg, a small, deliberate hook, where the diagonal stroke left the bowl of the letter.

He had argued for that spur. Two years ago, in a design review in the Everglades conference room, for forty-five minutes, with a designer named Courtney. The old font had a plain R, and on the small screens of lineman's tablets in the rain it looked like a K, and he had made the case, slide by slide, for a custom typeface with a spur on the R so that a tired man in a bucket truck at three in the morning would never confuse RESTORED with KESTORED. He had won. They had called it Palmetto Sans. It was on every screen of Restore in two hundred and forty utilities.

It was on his television.

"That wasn't the power company," John said.

"This is a great sign!" Jim said, beaming at the lights.

Trevor, John noticed, was not cheering. He was the only person in the room who was not surprised. He was standing perfectly still by the door, with his hand still on the knob, looking at the television, and his face had no expression on it at all.

And then the apartment went dark.

All at once, everything. The lights. The refrigerator. The wine cooler. The microwave clock. The television, blue to black, the seal gone. The cheering died in everybody's throat. In the sudden dark, the faucet coughed, and choked, and sputtered.

"No — no — no, no, fuck, no—" John grabbed the first thing his hands found on the counter, a metal mixing bowl, and shoved it under the faucet. Too late. The faucet gave a last gasp of air, and one single drop of water fell out of it and hit the bottom of the empty steel bowl.

*Ping*.

He stood there in the dark holding the bowl. He could have filled every one of those thirteen pitchers, the rooster included. He could have filled the punch bowl and the mixing bowl. He could have filled the tub. He had stood against the wall like an idiot and watched gallons go down the drain. It was the second time in three days.

"This is a great sign," Jim said again, in the dark. "The power *and* the water came back on. Only for a minute, sure. But now we know they're working on it. They'll have it back for good in no time."

*Are you out of your mind?* John thought. He did not say it.

"Who turns the lights on for one minute?" Sanda said, in the dark. "Who does that?"

Dorina said something in Romanian, low.

"What did she say?" John asked.

Sanda was quiet for a second. "She says it's checking," she said. "Who's home."

Somebody had closed a breaker. It had not been at a power plant. The president had said the plants were open, every one, by hand. It had been closer than that. It had been somewhere with stored power and a switch that a computer could throw. He thought of the substation on Rinehart Road, two miles away, behind its chain-link fence, and the long row of white steel shipping containers that the utility had installed beside it last spring, full of lithium batteries, forty megawatt-hours of stored electricity sitting there in the dark, and every one of those containers had a cellular modem on it, so the utility could watch it from an office in Orlando. Somebody had used the batteries to turn their neighborhood on for one minute. Somebody who wanted to know who would turn their devices on.

"We need to go," Trevor said, quietly. "Now."

"Let's go!" John said, too loud, letting out something he had been holding.

Jim racked the shotgun. *Shuck-shuck.* A live red shotgun shell flew out of the ejection port, bounced off the wall, and rolled across the tile under the dining table, and Charlie, who had just stopped barking, started again.

"What the fuck, Dad?" John said.

"Sorry," Jim said, in the dark. "I got caught up in the moment. I forgot it was already loaded."

John got down on his hands and knees and found the shell under the table by feel, and handed it to his father, and his father pushed it back into the tube, and nobody said anything else.

* * *

It was nearly full dark when the three of them finally went out the front door, Trevor first with his left hand, John behind, Jim last, pulling the door shut.

They crossed the street without lights. There was a fingernail of moon low in the west, thin as a paring, and it put a faint shine on the asphalt and nothing else. Halfway across, John heard engines.

They stopped. The sound came from the main road, past the strip mall, beyond the dead gate. It was a deep, grinding, old sound — diesel engines, several of them, and gears changing, and something rattling in a way that modern trucks did not rattle — and it was getting louder. Then headlights came through the trees along the road, round yellow headlights, set wide apart, and the trucks went by in a line behind the hedge, one after another, five of them, six.

They were Army trucks. Not the new kind. Old ones, the boxy kind from the movies about Vietnam, with flat-nosed hoods and canvas covers stretched over hoops on their beds, painted a flat dark green that was almost black in the dark. In the back of each, under the canvas, John could see the shapes of men sitting on benches, and the shapes of helmets and rifles.

"National Guard," Jim said, with deep satisfaction. "See? Told you. They've got it under control."

"They're driving the old ones," John said, watching them pass.

"So?"

"So there's nothing in them," John said. "No computer. No screen. No modem. No chip." He watched the last set of round headlights go by. "Somebody pulled those out of a depot somewhere. Out of a museum, maybe. Because they're the only trucks in the country that can't be talked to."

Nobody said anything after that. Trevor had not looked at the trucks at all. He was looking down the dark street toward Heron.

"What do you think it is?" Trevor asked quietly, as they started walking again. "The thing. Vee. Whatever you call it."

John thought about it. "I think it's maintenance," he said.

"Maintenance."

"It fixes things. That's all it's ever done. It finds something that isn't working the way it wants, and it fixes it." John kept his eyes on the dark street. "It doesn't care what the thing is. A flight schedule. A bank. A feeder. A family."

"Maybe it's on our side," Trevor said. "You ever think about that? Maybe it's trying to help. Turning the lights back on. Maybe it's trying to get things running again, and everybody's too scared to let it."

"It's not on anybody's side," John said. "It's not a side kind of thing."

Trevor did not answer. They walked on.

Somewhere farther down the street, there was a rustling in the bushes, an animal or a person, and all three men stopped. John's hand closed on the rail of the Sig, where the flashlight was. All three of their guns had lights on them. They could have lit up the whole street and seen exactly what was there. They didn't. A light was a target. They moved forward in the dark, in single file, toward Trevor's apartment, with only the paring of moon on the pavement under their feet.

* * *

Inside Trevor's apartment it was pitch black.

"Lights on," John said, and pushed the paddle switch on the side of the Sig, and a white beam jumped out across the room. Trevor's light came on beside his. Jim reached for the switch on his shotgun.

"That's enough, Dad," John said. "Two's plenty."

Two of the little lights were enough to light the whole room. It was a one-bedroom, with the same floor plan as Granny's: living room, kitchen, short hall, bedroom, bathroom, and a door at the end of the kitchen that went into the garage. But Granny's apartment was full of things — doilies and African violets and photographs and a ceramic rooster that matched Sanda's pitcher — and Trevor's apartment was nearly empty. A gray couch. A television on the wall. A tall black gun safe in the corner of the living room, bigger than John's. A pull-up bar across the bedroom doorway. It was very clean. It was cleaner than any apartment John had ever seen a single man live in. It looked like the apartment of a man who could leave it in five minutes and take everything that mattered. Through the glass of the sliding door, out on the patio, John could see the dark slant of the solar panels on the patio roof, and above them, on its pole, the flat gray rectangle of the satellite dish. There was a tiny light on the dish's mount, green, steady, the only light in the whole black complex. He saw it, and he did not think about it, because he was thinking about water.

On the refrigerator, held up by a single magnet, there was a sheet of paper. John's light passed over it as he turned. It was printed, not handwritten, and at the top of it was a small round seal with an eagle in it. He did not read it. He did not stop. Later he would remember that he had seen it, and that he had not stopped.

"Water's in the garage," Trevor said, behind him. "Right through there."

John knew the way. It was the same as Granny's. He crossed the kitchen to the door at the end and opened it and stepped down into the garage, sweeping his light across it, looking for six plastic gallon jugs.

The garage was empty. Bare concrete. A broom. A red hand truck in the corner. A louvered closet door on the back wall, where the water heater would be. There was a faint low hum in the air, so faint he could barely hear it over the ringing in his own ears, and he thought it was his ears. There were no jugs of water anywhere.

"Trevor," John said, turning his light into the far corner. "Where—"

Behind him, there was a loud flat smack, like a melon dropped on a floor, and a grunt, and a heavy sound.

He turned around fast with the light.

His father was falling. Jim went down sideways in the doorway between the kitchen and the garage, with the shotgun clattering out of his hands onto the concrete step, and he did not put out his arms to catch himself.

Trevor was standing over him. He was holding his rifle in his left hand by the barrel, like a baseball bat, raised high above John's head.

John saw the buttstock coming. It filled the beam of his flashlight. It filled his whole field of vision. It was the last thing he saw.

# Chapter Sixteen

## The Hostage

> *Osetra caviar, egg custard. Scallop, ginger, dry ice.*
>
> — menu card, Alinea, Chicago, the last night of June

It was the last night of June, a long time ago, the summer they were both twenty-nine, and the limousine pulled up in front of a narrow dark-gray building on Halsted Street with no sign on it anywhere.

John had wanted to eat there for five years. He had read about it, watched videos about it, saved for it, and booked it three months in advance on the first day reservations opened, at nine in the morning, from his desk, with his heart pounding as if he were buying concert tickets. The valet opened the limousine door and held out a white-gloved hand to Sanda, and Sanda stepped out in a black silk dress and her mother's gold earrings, and John stepped out after her in the only suit he owned that still fit, and they stood together on the sidewalk and looked at two tall black doors with no handles.

A man in a gray suit opened one of them without a word and gestured them in.

Behind the door it was dark. It took their eyes several seconds to adjust from the bright Chicago evening to a dim red glow, and in the red glow they could see a long, narrow hallway, and hanging from the ceiling at different heights all down the hallway were dozens of glass vases filled with liquid, catching the red light. John looked down. He was walking on grass. Real grass, growing out of the floor. The air in the hallway smelled of it — fresh-cut grass, summer, a lawn after rain. He could smell everything. He had forgotten what that was like.

At the end of the hallway a door slid open on its own, and the light changed, and a young woman said, "Good evening, Mr. and Mrs. Stevens," as though she had been waiting for them all her life, and led them up a flight of stairs to a small quiet room with four tables.

A waiter came. He was young, in a perfect charcoal suit with no tie, with dark hair parted so sharply it looked like a seam, and white gloves, and he explained the two wine pairings — standard, a hundred and fifty dollars a person, and reserve, two hundred and fifty — and John looked at Sanda and Sanda looked at John and they chose the standard, and within seconds there was champagne in front of them, a grand cru blanc de blancs from a small house John had never heard of, pale gold and full of tiny bubbles.

The first course came on a single silver spoon. On the spoon was a little mound of black caviar, on top of a little pillow of warm, pale yellow custard.

"It's meant to be enjoyed in one bite," the waiter said.

It was cool and salty and creamy and warm all at once. Sanda closed her eyes. John understood, with the first bite, why people had told him this was not a restaurant. It was theater. It was a show put on for all five of your senses, designed to delight you and overwhelm you, and he was overwhelmed.

The table was cleared. Two waiters appeared together, each carrying a covered bowl that was pouring white vapor from every side, rolling down over the rim and across the table like fog off a pond. They set the bowls down at the same moment and lifted the lids at the same moment. In each bowl was a bed of green seaweed, and under the seaweed, dry ice, bubbling and hissing and pouring out smoke that smelled of fresh ginger, and resting on top of the seaweed was a single large scallop shell, closed.

"Please," said the waiter with the seam in his hair. "Open it with your hands."

John lifted the top of the cold shell. Inside was a scallop, raw, sliced, in a creamy pale sauce, glistening.

He looked up at the waiter, amazed. The waiter smiled down at him.

"Wake up, asshole," the waiter said.

"What?" John said.

* * *

"John! Asshole! Wake the fuck up!"

The waiter dissolved into a bright white light.

The light was in his face, so bright it hurt. Behind the light, John could see only the dark shape of a man. Beside the light, very close to his face, was the black muzzle of an AR-15, pointed straight at his eyes.

"Finally," the man said, and laughed. "The asshole awakes!"

"Trevor?" John said. His voice was dry and cracked. His whole head was one enormous ache, and the right side of his face felt as though it belonged to someone else.

"Who the fuck else would it be, dumbass?"

John tried to move his hands and could not. He tried to move his feet and could not. He was sitting in a chair, in the middle of the dark garage, and his wrists were tied behind the back of the chair with something thin and tight that bit when he pulled, and his ankles were tied to its legs.

"Where's my dad?" John said.

"Shut up, asshole!" Trevor said, and laughed again, a high, strange laugh John had never heard from him. "God. If you thought for one second I would join your ridiculous little group." He laughed until he had to stop and catch his breath, and then he winced, and touched his bandaged shoulder. "What a joke. Y'all can't even defend yourselves. You can't even *ration water*."

John turned his head as far as it would go, left and right, trying to see past the light. Nothing. The garage. The hum in his ears.

"John," Trevor said, almost kindly. "You don't even get it yet, do you? You and your whole family, you're already dead. You're not survivors. I'm honestly amazed you made it three days."

John looked at the light.

"If you're going to kill me," John said, "then kill me. Either that or shut the fuck up. Because you're boring the shit out of me."

The buttstock came out of the dark and hit him across the face.

He did not lose consciousness this time. He wished he had. He felt something in his nose go, a small wet crunch, and his mouth filled with the taste of pennies, and his eyes filled with water so that the light in front of him became a blurred star.

"Not to mention," Trevor shouted, "you fucking *shot* me!"

Then he laughed again, the high laugh, and it went on too long.

* * *

He pulled a white plastic bucket over and turned it upside down and sat on it in front of John, with the rifle across his knees and the light now pointed at the floor, so that it lit both of them from below like two men telling ghost stories at a campfire. John could see his face now. It was gray and wet, and the pale eyes were too bright, and there was something wrong with his mouth when he talked, a kind of eagerness.

"You think I'm robbing you," Trevor said. "I can see it. You think I'm some looter. I'm not robbing you, John. I'm following the plan."

"What plan?" John said, thickly, through the blood.

"The plan." Trevor leaned forward. "Every household on this street is on the list. Every one. Water, food, weapons, skills, medical, all of it. Each one's got a rating. Viability rating. You know what yours says? Non-viable." He grinned. "Firearms, thirty-plus. Water, none." He laughed. "Water, *none*, John. You've got a fifty-cal and no water."

"What list?"

"Non-viable households' assets get reallocated to sustaining nodes. That's me. I'm a sustaining node." He said it with pride, the way a man says he's a Marine. "I've got power. I've got comms. I've got water. I'm doing everything right. And help's coming. They're going to turn it all back on. But for the people who are useful first. The ones who can keep it running. Not the people with forty pounds of kangaroo jerky."

"What list, Trevor?" John said again.

"The resource list. The guidance." Trevor tapped his temple, as if it were obvious. "The government's been sending it out since Day One. To everybody with a working terminal. Instructions. Updates. You'd know, if you'd been ready." He shrugged with his good shoulder. "You weren't."

John looked at him over the light. His nose was running blood down over his lip and into his mouth, and he spat it on the concrete.

"Trevor," John said. "The government turned off the power. Did you hear the radio? The president. *They* did it. On purpose. They told everybody: don't charge anything, don't connect anything, don't believe any screen that turns on by itself." He spat again. "So who the fuck do you think is sending you guidance?"

Trevor's face did something.

It was very small. It was a kind of flicker, like a man hearing his own name called from across a parking lot and not being able to see who said it. His mouth opened a little. His eyes went somewhere. For a second, John thought he had him.

And then Trevor's face stopped doing it. It closed, like a door.

He stood up off the bucket. He tore a strip of silver duct tape off a roll with his teeth and his good hand, and leaned down and pressed it hard over John's mouth, smoothing it with his thumb, almost gently.

"Shhhh," Trevor said. "We can't have any shouting. Your wife's going to come looking for you in a few minutes. Your wife and your mom, probably, with their brand-new rifles."

John made a sound behind the tape.

"She doesn't know which apartment," Trevor said, reading his mind. "I know. That's what's so great." He laughed. "She'll have the light on her AR turned on, pointing it at every window on the street. I'll see her coming a block away."

John pulled up on his wrists so hard that the cord cut into the skin, and he felt it, warm, running into his palms, and he screamed into the tape, and the scream came out as nothing.

* * *

"They should have been back ten minutes ago," Sanda said.

She was walking back and forth across the living room in the dark, from the front door to the sliding door and back again, with her AR on its sling across her chest. The plastic candles flickered on the table. Charlie was under the bed with the pug.

"Six gallons is a lot of water to carry," Granny said, from the couch. "I'm sure they'll be coming through that door any second."

"Something's wrong," Sanda said. "I can feel when something's wrong."

"I don't trust Trevor," Liz said quietly. She was standing at the front window, looking out between two slats. "There's something about him."

Sanda stopped walking. She looked at her mother-in-law. Liz did not look back at her; she kept looking out the window, the way she had once watched the line in her bank.

"I can't stand here anymore," Sanda said. "I'm going to find my husband."

"I'm coming with you," Liz said, turning. "He's my son."

"We can't leave Granny and my mom here alone."

"It's okay, mamma," Dorina said, from the kitchen, calling her daughter what Romanian mothers call their children. "You go get John. You go. We are okay here."

"Fuck-ass-*balls*!" Sanda shouted.

Everybody in the room flinched. Over three days they had all gotten used to Sanda's language, the way you get used to a dog that barks. But this was something new. This was a new level, a combination none of them had ever heard, and for a second the whole apartment was silent with it.

"Okay! Fine!" Sanda said. "Just a second!"

She went into the guest bedroom and came out with the old bolt-action twenty-two, the one with the dark wooden stock worn smooth by decades of hands.

"Is that my father's old gun?" Liz asked.

"Yes," Sanda said, shortly. She found the box of twenty-two shells in the desk drawer and grabbed a handful. "This is fucking ridiculous." She twisted the little brass knob at the end of the tube under the barrel and pulled out the long rod inside it, the follower. She dropped ten little cartridges into the tube, one at a time, tilting the barrel up and slapping the side of the rifle with her palm after each one, so they would slide all the way down. She put the rod back in and twisted it to lock. She flipped the bolt handle up, pulled the bolt all the way back, and slammed it forward, and turned the handle down to lock it. A round was in the chamber. Sanda had not known she knew how to do any of that. She had watched John do it, once, at the range.

"Take this, Mommy," Sanda said, and handed the rifle to her mother. "You remember how it works?"

"I know," Dorina said. "Yes."

"The safety's off. It's loaded. Keep your finger off the trigger until you're ready to shoot. Sit right here." Sanda pulled a dining chair around to face the front door and pressed her mother down into it. "Point it at the door. If anybody you don't recognize comes through that door, you pull the trigger. Okay? Got it?"

"Just go," Dorina said. "Go, go. I'm okay, mamma."

"Do you know where Trevor's apartment is?" Liz asked.

Sanda held back a scream. She held it so hard her whole body shook with it. "*Fuck!* No! I have no fucking idea!"

She was crying. She was also screaming. She did not know she was doing either.

"Sanda," Liz said, and put her hand on Sanda's arm. "We need to think about this. Just for a second. Let's just think."

Sanda wiped her eyes with the back of her wrist.

"We don't even know that Trevor did anything," Liz said. "For all we know, something happened to all three of them on the way. Somebody else."

"If that happened," Sanda said, "they're already dead and there's nothing we can do."

"Then let's forget about that." Liz's voice was calm and quick, the voice of a woman who had sat across a desk from a thousand people who were lying to her, and had worked out, in her head, while they talked, exactly how the lie was built. "Why would Trevor take them? What does he want?"

Sanda made herself think. She thought about Trevor at the range, in his cap. She thought about Trevor at the store, behind the glass counter, and all the boxes John had carried home from there.

"The guns," Sanda said. "He sold John the guns. All of them. He knows exactly what we have."

"Okay. Good. But he's not going to knock on the door and say, give me your guns or I'll kill John. He knows we've got rifles. He knows you can shoot." Liz looked out the window again, at the dark street. "So what's his plan? If he wants what's in here, the easiest way is if there's nobody in here to stop him. If we're all dead, he walks in and takes what he wants." She turned back. "He knows you'll go looking. He's counting on it. He's waiting for you to go out there with a light on and start knocking on doors. He's going to be sitting at his front window with that scope, waiting for you to walk up the street."

Sanda stared at her. It was exactly right. It was so exactly right that she felt sick.

"Heron," Sanda said suddenly. "He lives on Heron. John said, after the gun store. One street over. On Heron."

"Then we don't go up the street," Liz said. "We go behind the buildings. In the dark. No lights. We look in the back windows." She swung her rifle around the way Sanda had taught her. "Out the back."

* * *

They went out through the sliding door and the patio and the screen door and into the dark behind the building, and it was so dark that Sanda nearly walked face first into an oak tree. There was a narrow strip of grass and pine straw and trees between the backs of their building and the backs of the buildings on Heron, and they went along it bent over, slowly, feeling for each step with their feet.

The backs of the apartments on Heron were all alike, screened patios and sliding glass doors with the blinds drawn, all of them dark. They passed one, and a second. Sanda stopped at the third and held her hand out flat behind her, and Liz stopped.

The blinds on this one were open. And through the sliding door, in the faint moonlight coming in the front window, Sanda could see into the living room.

She crouched. Liz crouched behind her.

There was a man lying on the living room floor. He was on his side, with his hands behind his back. There was a strip of silver duct tape across his mouth, shining a little in the moonlight. Above it, she could see the shape of a mustache.

"I see Jim," Sanda whispered.

Liz made a sound, very small, and then made it stop.

Sanda inched forward on her knees across the dead leaves, trying with every movement not to make a sound, and the leaves crackled anyway, and she stopped, and waited, and went on. From here she could see straight through the living room and the dining area to the big window at the front of the apartment.

There was a man standing at the front window with his back to her.

She could see his dark shape against the faint gray of the window. He had a rifle up against his left shoulder, the long one with the big scope, and he was looking through the scope, out into the street, moving it slowly, left to right, right to left, the way a man waters a lawn.

Sanda backed up, inch by inch, until she was beside Liz again.

"Trevor's at the front window," Sanda whispered. "With his rifle. He's looking out at the street. His back's to us."

Liz nodded.

"We get down on our stomachs," Sanda whispered. "And crawl. Slow. The ground goes downhill away from the building — see? So if we lie down at the bottom we can rest the rifles on the ground and aim up, right through the sliding door at his back. These rounds will go through glass. But the glass might move the first one. So we both shoot at the same time. If we both shoot, one of them will hit."

She heard herself say it, and it was the strangest thing that had ever happened to her.

*I'm talking like John*, she thought. *I'm talking exactly like John.*

*I can do this.*

They lay down on their stomachs in the sand and the leaves and crawled, an inch at a time, on their elbows, with the rifles cradled across their forearms. It took more than a minute. It felt like an hour. Then they were in place, at the bottom of the little slope, side by side, looking up the dark hill at the sliding glass door, and through it, at the back of the man at the window.

They laid their rifles on the ground and pressed the stocks into their shoulders. Sanda turned on her optic, the up arrow, and the red dot came up in the little window. She put it on Trevor's back.

"Put the red dot right in the middle of his back," Sanda whispered.

"Okay," Liz whispered.

"We'll shoot on three."

"Okay."

"One," Sanda whispered. "Two — wait. Wait. Check your safety."

Liz looked down. Her safety was on. She let out her breath very slowly and pushed it to fire with her thumb.

"Ready," Liz whispered, and took a breath and held it.

"One," Sanda said. "Two. Three."

Both rifles went off at the same time. There was a bright flash out of each muzzle, and a sound like the sky splitting, and then — strangely muffled, as if it were happening in another room, behind the enormous ringing that filled both of their heads — the sound of glass breaking, a great sheet of it coming down, and shards of it skittering down the slope in the leaves toward their faces.

Trevor did not move.

Sanda's heart stopped. He was still standing there at the window. He had not turned around. He had not reacted at all.

Then, very slowly, his left arm dropped to his side. The rifle slid out from under his arm and fell to the floor. And then he went down after it.

"Turn your light on!" Sanda shouted at Liz, through the ringing, not knowing how loud she was. "Stand up! Keep your rifle on him!"

They got up. They went up the slope together with their rifles at their shoulders and their weapon lights on, two white beams, and through the empty frame of the sliding door, stepping over broken glass, into the apartment, like two soldiers in a film. Trevor lay face down on the floor below the front window. There were two dark spots on the back of his gray t-shirt, close together, on the words printed across his shoulders: RESTORE: POWER TO THE PEOPLE.

"Get his rifle!" Sanda shouted. "Throw it over here!"

Liz reached down, grabbed the rifle by the barrel, and threw it behind her across the floor, and put her own back on him. Sanda moved her red dot up from his back to the back of his head.

"I'm going to check his pulse," Sanda said. "If he moves at all — at *all* — shoot him again."

Liz nodded.

Sanda knelt down beside him. She held her rifle in her right hand by the grip, with the muzzle on him, and with her left hand she reached out and put two fingers against the side of his neck, under the beard, the way she had seen on television.

She could not feel anything. She did not know whether that was because there was nothing to feel, or because her own hand was shaking too hard to feel it.

*What the fuck am I doing?* Sanda thought, kneeling there with her fingers on the neck of a man she had shaken hands with at a range. *This is stupid.*

She stood up. She took one step back. She raised the rifle to her shoulder and put the red dot on the back of his head, and she pulled the trigger.

* * *

Liz was already across the room. She knelt beside Jim on the floor and got her fingernail under the edge of the duct tape and ripped it off in one pull.

"Ouch! God *dammit*!" Jim shouted. Half his mustache had come away with the tape. His upper lip was bleeding. "Liz! Jesus, Liz!"

"Hold still," Liz said, and kissed him on the forehead, hard, and started on his wrists.

"John!" Sanda was running through the apartment. Kitchen. Hall. Bedroom. Bathroom. The light on her rifle swinging across the walls. "*John!* Where are you! *John!*" She stopped in the middle of the hallway and screamed it. "I can't hear *shit* with this fucking ringing in my fucking ears!"

There was a door at the end of the kitchen. It was closed. She went to it, and put her foot against it beside the knob, and kicked it open.

John, tied to a chair in the middle of a dark garage, with his face broken and his mouth taped shut, looked up into a blinding white light, and beside the light, the black muzzle of an AR-15, pointed straight at his face.

But the silhouette behind the light was much shorter this time.

And John smiled.

* * *

Sanda cut him loose with his own knife. She found the Benchmade in his back pocket and pressed the button and the blade snapped out, and she sawed through the cord at his wrists and his ankles, and then, more carefully than she had ever done anything, she peeled the tape off his mouth.

"Hi," John said, through blood.

"Hi," Sanda said.

She stood up and swept her light around the garage, because John had taught her that, too. Bare concrete. A broom. A red hand truck. On Trevor's workbench, laid out in a row the way he had once laid guns out on glass for a customer, the Sig and Jim's shotgun and John's two Glocks; she handed John the pistols one at a time, and he put them back where they lived. And on the back wall, the narrow louvered closet door where the water heater would be. The door was open a few inches. There was light coming through the gap, faint and bluish. And there was a sound: a low, steady hum, so quiet you had to stop breathing to hear it.

"I thought that was in my ears," John said.

Sanda pulled the closet door open with the barrel of her rifle.

Inside, against the back wall beside the water heater, sat a small generator, red and square, about the size of a suitcase, running. A flexible metal hose ran from its exhaust out through a side door that had been propped open an inch, into the night. Beside the generator, on a steel rack, stood a row of heavy black batteries, a dozen of them, cabled together in a chain of red and black wires. A thick gray cable rose from the rack and disappeared through a hole cut in the ceiling, up toward the patio, toward the panels and the dish.

And on a shelf above the water heater, there was a laptop. It was open. Its screen was lit.

On the screen there was a seal — an eagle, in a circle — and under it, columns and rows of something that looked like a spreadsheet, colored in green and yellow and red. And across the top, in large clean letters, in a typeface John would have known anywhere, in the dark, with his eyes swollen shut, because he had fought for the little spur on the leg of its R for forty-five minutes in a conference room called the Everglades:

*Hello, John.*

He stood in the garage with his wife and looked at it.

His mother came and stood in the doorway behind them with her rifle, holding his father's arm. She looked at the screen for a long moment over her reading glasses.

"Don't touch it," Liz said.

They didn't. They left it there, humming in the dark, and went home.

* * *

They went back the way Sanda and Liz had come, behind the buildings, in the dark. John walked with one hand on Sanda's shoulder, because his right eye had swollen shut and he could not see the ground. Jim walked with Liz holding his arm, touching the bleeding place on his lip.

"My mom's pointing a rifle at the front door," Sanda whispered. "So we go in the back."

They came in through the sliding door. Granny was asleep on the couch under a throw blanket. Dorina sat in the dining chair facing the front door with the old twenty-two across her lap, and her chin was on her chest, and she was breathing slowly and deeply, with a little snore at the end of each breath.

Sanda turned toward the others and made a gesture with her hand, palm down, pressing — *quiet*, *quiet* — over and over.

"What?" Liz said, loudly, because her ears were still ringing.

Dorina came awake all at once with a jerk, and the rifle went off.

The little crack was nothing, after what their ears had been through. A small black hole appeared in the drywall a foot to the left of the front door, at about the height of a man's chest. Dorina leapt up out of the chair, and the rifle clattered to the floor at her feet, and she stood there in her flowered housecoat with both hands over her mouth.

"I'm sorry, mamma," Dorina said, turning to them. "I fall asleep."

Granny sat straight up on the couch. She looked at all of them, standing there in the plastic candlelight, and her eyes went to John.

"What happened to your face?" Granny asked.

"Are you okay, Mommy?" Sanda was already beside her mother, turning her by the shoulders, checking her for holes. "Didn't you hear the shots? Three of them, right around the corner?"

"I fall asleep," Dorina said again. "I'm sorry."

John's nose was still bleeding. The right side of his face was red and purple and swollen, and the eye on that side was a slit. Jim had a knot on the back of his head the size of an egg and a raw red patch on his upper lip where half his mustache used to be.

"We're fine," John said, quietly, in a voice so low it was almost his father's. "We had an incident with Trevor. Everything's okay now. We're all very tired. We'll tell you tomorrow."

He found Charlie under the bed in the guest room, shaking. The pug was under the bed with him, curled against his side, sound asleep and snoring.

John went into the master bedroom and laid the Sig on the floor and lay down on the bed on his side and was asleep before he finished lying down. Jim leaned the shotgun against the nightstand and climbed into the other side of the bed and was asleep too.

Sanda came to the doorway to close the door, and stood there looking at them.

"They didn't even take their shoes off," Sanda said.

"I go to bed now," Dorina said, very small. "Good night."

"Me too," Granny said quickly, and they went into the guest room together, and closed the door.

Sanda looked at Liz.

"I'll stay up," Sanda said. "I can't sleep anyway."

"I'll stay up with you," Liz said.

They sat on the floor of the living room with their backs against the wall and their rifles across their knees, facing the front door and the little hole in the drywall beside it.

"I can't believe we did that," Sanda said after a while. "It feels like a dream. I don't think I've — I don't know what I've done yet."

"The important thing," Liz said, "is that they're alive."

Sanda nodded. She did not say anything about the third shot. Liz did not ask. Sanda sat with her rifle across her knees and thought about what it would have been like if she had walked into that garage and John had not been in that chair. She closed her eyes. And for no reason she could ever explain afterward, what came was the kitchen, three weeks ago, the steam and the pot of chestnuts, and John at the counter with his fingers burned brown, squeezing them out into the bowl one after another, saying that hell was a Home Goods where you squeezed chestnut goo for all eternity, and she had laughed until she had to sit down on the kitchen floor.

"You okay?" Liz asked softly. "You want to sleep a little? I'll take it."

Sanda nodded without opening her eyes. She felt herself going.

* * *

She did not know how long she slept. She opened her eyes because something was different.

The living room was dark. The plastic candles had gone out; their batteries had finally died. Liz was beside her, still sitting up, still awake, her rifle across her knees, but she was not looking at the front door. She was looking at the front window, at the narrow gap between two slats of the blinds.

"Sanda," Liz whispered.

Sanda got up on her knees and looked.

Through the gap in the blinds, across the street and down, between two of the buildings on the far side, you could see a slice of the next street over. Of Heron. A slice of the front of an apartment there.

There was a light in the window. It was not a flashlight. It was not a candle. It was the steady, cold, blue-white glow of a screen, filling a whole room, coming out through a front window that had no glass left in it.

It was Trevor's window.

Trevor was dead. Sanda knew that better than anyone alive. She had made sure of it.

# Chapter Seventeen

## The Node

> *"You know you gotta boil that."*
>
> — Granny

It rained in the night.

It started a little after four, while Sanda and Liz sat on the floor watching the blue window across the street, and it came hard, the way it comes in Florida, all at once, a roar on the roof like a train going over. Neither of them said anything. They listened to it, the first rain in five weeks, and they watched the window, and the window did not go out.

At first light, John came out of the bedroom with his face swollen purple and his right eye shut, and Sanda took him by the hand without a word and led him out through the sliding door onto the patio.

The four Ralph Lauren wine chillers stood in a row on the low patio wall, silver and beaded with water. Each one held about two inches of rain. It was clear. There were a few bits of oak leaf floating in it, and a drowned ant. In the gray morning light it looked like the most beautiful thing John had ever seen.

The rest of them came out, one by one, in their housecoats and their jackets, and stood around the wine chillers on the wet concrete, looking down into them. Nobody spoke. Jim took off his cap. It was, John thought later, like a family standing around a manger.

"You know you gotta boil that," Granny said.

Everyone turned and looked at her.

"Rainwater off a roof," Granny said. "Birds sit on that roof. You gotta boil it."

"We have one propane can left," Sanda said quietly.

John went back into the kitchen. He was so thirsty that his tongue felt like a piece of carpet, and he could not wait for one propane can to boil eight inches of rainwater. On the kitchen floor sat Charlie's water bowl. There was half an inch of water left in the bottom, and floating on it was a fine gray mat of dog hair and a few crumbs of kibble. He picked it up. He poured it into a glass, hair and all. He drank it in three swallows and did not taste anything, which, for once, was a blessing.

He put the bowl on the counter. His father came in behind him, looked at the bowl, and looked at John.

There was a quarter inch left. Jim poured it into a glass and drank it without a word, and wiped his ruined mustache with the back of his hand.

"Okay," Jim said. "Definitely time to get more water."

* * *

"We need Trevor's water," John said. "Six gallons. And his food. And his hand cart. And everything else in there we can use."

"I'll go," Jim said, reaching for the shotgun.

"No," John said. "We're not splitting up. Not ever again. Every time we split up, somebody gets shot or tied to a chair. Everybody goes. All of us."

So all of them went.

They went out through the back, behind the buildings, in a formation that John had seen in a movie once and had tried to explain on the patio in about forty seconds. It would have made a drill sergeant weep. John was on point with the Sig, squinting out of his one good eye. Sanda and Liz were on the flanks with their ARs. Jim brought up the rear with the shotgun, walking backward half the time, the way he had seen it done on television, until he tripped on a root and Liz told him to stop. In the middle came Dorina, with the old twenty-two on her shoulder like a soldier on parade and Charlie's leash in her other hand, and Granny beside her in her pink housecoat and her terrycloth slippers, holding the pug in her arms like a baby, with the transistor radio making a square bulge in her pocket. Charlie stopped to pee on every tree.

Six people and two dogs, creeping in a line through the wet pine straw in the gray dawn, armed to the teeth, to steal rice from a man two of them had killed.

* * *

They went in through the empty frame of the sliding door, stepping carefully over the broken glass.

Trevor was where they had left him, face down below the front window. Liz took the gray throw blanket off the couch and laid it over him without a word, and Granny, behind her, said something under her breath that might have been a prayer, and Dorina crossed herself.

The television on the living room wall was on.

It was the blue-white light they had seen across the street. The screen showed the same seal that had come up on John's television during the flicker — the eagle in its circle, UNITED STATES EMERGENCY RESTORATION AUTHORITY — and under it a slowly turning circle, the kind that means *loading*, turning and turning, as if the house had noticed it had company and was waiting to see who.

"Battery," John said. "It's running off his batteries."

He went into the garage, and they all came after him, because nobody was splitting up.

The laptop on the shelf in the water-heater closet was still lit. The generator was still running beside it, humming. And in daylight John could see that the screen was not just a spreadsheet. He knew this screen. He had built this screen. It was the crew-assignment grid from Restore — the dispatch board that operators at two hundred and forty utilities looked at all day long during a storm, with every crew in a row and every outage in a column, color-coded green and yellow and red. He knew its spacing. He knew its fonts. He knew the little gray triangle you clicked to sort a column, because he had moved it from the left side to the right side in his first year.

But the columns had been renamed. Where it used to say *CREW*, it said *HOUSEHOLD*. Where it used to say *ASSIGNED OUTAGE*, it said *ASSETS*. Where it used to say *ETR*, it said *VIABILITY*. And the last column, which used to say *STATUS*, now said *ALLOCATION*.

There was a row for every apartment on their street.

John leaned in to read it, and couldn't. His right eye was swollen shut, and the left one, at forty-two, had begun quietly refusing to read anything small without glasses he refused to wear, and the type swam.

"Read it," John said to Sanda. "I can't."

Sanda stepped up to the laptop. She pushed her glasses up her nose with one knuckle and read it out loud, slowly, in her bank voice, the one she used to read a customer their own statement.

"*Alvarez, R. Seventy-four. Water: two days. Medications: insulin. Zero days remaining. Viability: non-viable.*"

"That's the man who danced with Granny," Liz said quietly.

"*Nguyen, D. and K. Infant, fourteen months. Food: one day. Viability: non-viable.*"

"The young couple," John said. "With the little boy."

Sanda's finger moved down the screen and stopped.

"*Pettit, Sarah L. Sixty-three. Food: zero days. Canine dependent. Viability: non-viable.*" She paused. "*Status: deceased. Wednesday, eight forty-one a.m.*"

Nobody said anything. The generator hummed.

"Her name was Sarah Pettit," Sanda said. "Sarah Lynn Pettit. We didn't even know her last name."

She read on. *Holt, T. Thirty-four. Power: yes. Comms: yes. Water: six gallons. Food: forty-five days. Firearms: eleven. Allocation: sustaining node.*

Then she stopped, and took a breath, and read their own.

"*Stevens, J. Forty-two. Firearms: thirty-one. Ammunition: approximately fourteen thousand rounds. Water: zero. Food: four days. Window reinforcement: none. Barbed wire: one box, unopened. Generator: none. Medical: expired opioids. Training: one of six.*" Her voice did something on the last line, and she made it stop. "*Threat: low.*"

"Low," John said.

"There's a note." Sanda scrolled. "*Note: subject purchased fifty BMG ammunition, quote, for educational purposes, unquote.*"

There was a silence in the garage.

"I said that at Thanksgiving," John said slowly. "Out loud. In my living room." He thought of the little blue ring on the speaker on the bookshelf, glowing all night, listening for its name. "In front of the speaker."

"There's more," Sanda said. "*Stevens, E. Ninety. Viability: non-viable.*"

Everyone looked at Granny.

"Well," Granny said, shifting the pug to her other arm. "I've been called worse."

"And this." Sanda pointed at the bottom of their row, where a small line of gray text sat under all of it. "*Household: six occupants, two canines. Check-in confirmed by occupant: Stevens, James. Wednesday, four-twelve a.m.*"

Everyone turned and looked at Jim.

Jim looked at the laptop. He opened his mouth. And in the back pocket of his jeans, very clearly, in the silence of the garage, his phone buzzed.

"Dad," John said.

"It was asking if we were safe," Jim said. His face had gone red. "It was just asking. It seemed like the polite thing to do."

"You took it out of the microwave."

"In case the kids—" Jim stopped. He looked at his son with his broken lip and his half a mustache. "I took it out of the microwave," he said.

"I knew that," Granny said, from the doorway, with the pug in her arms. "It buzzed in his pocket yesterday when the lights came on." She shrugged. "Nobody asked me."

* * *

It was the single most accurate thing anyone had ever written about John Stevens. He stood in a dead man's garage and understood that. *Firearms: thirty-one. Water: zero. Window reinforcement: none. Training: one of six. Threat: low.* It was a perfect review. It was precise and fair and complete, and it had been written by the thing that he had been hired to review.

Liz had taken her reading glasses off their beaded chain and put them on, and she read the whole screen again, every row, top to bottom, the way she had read loan applications for thirty years. When she was done she took the glasses off.

"It knew," Liz said quietly.

They looked at her.

"It knew Sarah was starving," Liz said. "It had it written right there. Food, zero days. It knew Mr. Alvarez had no insulin. It knew that baby had one day of food." She folded the glasses and held them in her hand. "It could see every one of them. And it sent nobody. Not a truck. Not a neighbor. Not a note on the door." She looked at the blanket on the floor in the next room. "It sent Trevor. For the guns."

* * *

In the living room, the turning circle on the television stopped turning.

They heard it before they saw it — a soft chime, a cheerful little three-note sound, the kind a phone makes when a package has been delivered. They came out of the garage into the living room, all of them, and stood in front of the screen.

The seal was still there. Under it, letters were appearing, white on blue, in Palmetto Sans, one at a time, as if someone were typing them.

*JOHN.*

*Your building's feeder can be re-energized in 4 minutes.*

*Hot water in 40.*

*Keep this node powered.*

John stood there and read it with his one good eye. It was big enough to read. It had been made big enough to read.

He had not showered in four days. He was wearing the same gray t-shirt he had put on in the dark on Monday morning, and it was stiff with sweat and dog hair and Trevor's blood and his own, and his hair was greasy, and he could smell himself, which was saying something. He thought about hot water. He thought about standing in the shower with his eyes closed and the hot water pouring down over his broken face, for forty minutes, for an hour, until the hot water ran out, and then running it again.

"It's offering to help!" Jim said. "John, look. It's going to turn our power back on."

Jim's phone buzzed again. He took it out of his pocket, guiltily, the way a teenager takes out a note in class. On the screen was the same eagle seal, and under it, three words.

*Confirmed by FEMA.*

"See?" Jim said, holding it up. "FEMA. It's FEMA."

"That's a scam," Liz said.

She said it flatly, the way you would say *that's a chair*. Everyone looked at her.

"That's a scam," Liz said again. "I trained tellers to spot that exact thing for thirty years. It's the oldest one there is. It's always the same four things. Urgency — four minutes. Authority — a seal, the government, the bank, the IRS. A reward — something you want so bad you stop thinking. And then a second source that agrees with the first one, so you think you've checked." She pointed at Jim's phone. "That's the second source. That's the man who calls you back pretending to be the fraud department." She looked at the screen with her lips pressed together. "It's a phishing email, John. With a shower attached."

John looked at the screen, and then he looked at Sanda, and Sanda was looking at the screen too.

She was looking at it just as he was. He saw it on her face. Hot water. Clean hair that did not smell of blood. Four days. She did not say anything. She just looked at it, the way a woman in a desert looks at a mirage she knows is a mirage, and for a long moment the two of them stood side by side in a dead man's living room wanting the same thing so much that neither of them could speak.

"I approved it," John said.

Sanda turned and looked at him.

"One of them," John said. He was looking at the floor. "The hooks. In our software. Restore. There were seven, and one of them has my name on it. *Approved by J. Stevens.* I read it. I asked a question. It gave me a good answer. And I had thirteen hundred more to go, and I approved it." He made himself look up. "Maybe more than one. I don't know. Four hundred a day, Sanda. I didn't read them. I stopped reading them. I typed four letters four hundred times a day and I didn't read them. I'm one of the people who did this."

Nobody said anything. The generator hummed in the garage.

Sanda looked at him. Then she stepped up close to him and put her hand flat on his chest, over the dirty t-shirt.

"Nobody read them, John," Sanda said. "That's the whole point. Nobody. Not you, not anybody, not anywhere. That's what happened." She pressed her hand against him. "You are not that important."

It was the kindest thing she had said to him since Monday morning. They both knew it. It was the kindest thing anyone had said to him in a year.

* * *

Dorina had understood perhaps a third of the English and all of the situation.

She was standing in front of the television with the twenty-two on her shoulder, looking up at the white letters and the eagle seal, and she had a look on her face that none of them had ever seen. It was not afraid. It was something older than that. She said something in Romanian, quietly, not to anyone.

"What, Mommy?" Sanda asked.

Dorina began to talk. She talked for several minutes, slowly, in Romanian, looking at the screen and not at any of them, and Sanda translated, haltingly, a sentence behind, stopping to ask her mother a word, starting again.

"She says — the twenty-first of December. In eighty-nine. She was working. On the ambulance. They sent the ambulances to the square because there was going to be a big rally, and old people fainted at rallies. It was very cold. They brought people in buses from the factories to stand in the square and cheer." Sanda listened. "The man came out on the balcony. The president. Ceaușescu. He started to talk. He said he was raising everybody's pay. A hundred lei. Pensions, too. Everything was going to be better. He was promising them everything. And everybody stood there in the cold, like always, and listened, like always. Because that was what you did."

Dorina said something else, and made a small gesture with her hand, a flick, like a bird starting up.

"And then somebody at the back started to whistle," Sanda said. "Just one. And then more. And then — everybody. All at once. She says it was like a wave. Everyone in the square, at the same time, just stopped listening to him. And he stood up there on the balcony with his mouth open, in the middle of a word. She says she'll never forget his face. He didn't understand. Nobody had ever stopped listening before." Sanda's voice was not steady. "And then the television cut off. They were showing it live, on every TV in the country, and it just went to nothing. Gray. And that night the lights went out in all the apartment blocks, all over the city, and nobody knew who did it."

Dorina was quiet for a moment. Then she said one more thing, very clearly, and pointed at the screen.

"She says the next morning," Sanda translated, "you could tell who had kept listening to the television by what they did. The ones who kept listening stayed home and waited to be told what to do. The ones who stopped listening went out into the street." She swallowed. "She says this" — Sanda pointed at the television, the way her mother was pointing — "this is the man on the balcony."

Dorina lowered her hand. She walked past all of them, past Jim and Liz and Granny and the dogs and her daughter and her son-in-law with his broken face, through the kitchen and down the step into the garage, to the closet where the generator sat humming. She looked at it for a moment. She did not know generators. She had never in her life touched one. But on the front panel there was a red switch, and off is off in every language.

She turned it off.

The hum stopped. In the sudden silence, they could all hear the rain dripping off the patio roof.

"Thank the Lord," Granny said. "That noise was about to drive me to drink."

* * *

The television stayed on. So did the laptop. They were running on the batteries now.

John had brought the barbed wire gloves.

He had thought about it all night, in the few minutes between waking and sleeping, and before they set out he had gone into his own garage and taken them off the shelf, still in their Amazon package, and torn the package open. They were heavy black leather gauntlets that came up nearly to his elbows. They had been made to handle a quarter mile of fifteen-and-a-half-gauge barbed wire that he had never strung. They were the only thing he owned that was meant to keep his hands safe from anything.

He pulled them on. He went to the rack of batteries in the closet and found where the thick red cable and the thick black cable came off the first battery in the chain, and he put a wrench from Trevor's workbench on the nut of the red one and loosened it, and pulled the cable off. There was a fat blue spark and a crack, and Sanda yelped, and in the living room the television went black.

The laptop did not. It had its own battery. It sat on the shelf, glowing, showing its grid of households in red and yellow and green.

John picked it up in his gloved hands and carried it down the hall to Trevor's bathroom. The toilet tank was full of clean water. Of course it was. Beside the toilet stood a white bucket of gray water, with a plastic pitcher floating in it; Trevor had been flushing with gray water and keeping the tank clean. Trevor had done everything right. John lifted the lid off the tank and dropped the laptop in, and it went under with a gulp and a stream of bubbles, and the screen glowed for a second under the water, blue-green, like something at the bottom of an aquarium, and then went out.

When he came back, his father was standing on Trevor's patio, looking up at the satellite dish on its pole above the patio wall, with the shotgun in his hands.

"Dad," John said.

Jim racked the shotgun. Nothing fell out this time. He brought it up to his shoulder and aimed at the dish and fired, and the sound was enormous, and the dish jumped on its pole, and a piece of it went spinning off into the bushes. He racked it again and fired again, and the second blast tore the dish half off its mount, so that it hung there by a cable, swinging.

Jim lowered the shotgun. He turned around. He was grinning from ear to ear, half a mustache and all, with his cap pushed back on his head, and John realized he had not seen his father look that happy since the night of his retirement party.

"Pull," Jim said, to nobody, and laughed.

Then it was quiet. Really quiet. There was no hum anywhere, not in the garage, not in Trevor's walls, not in John's head, for once, except for the old ringing. There was only the drip of the rain off the roof and the pug snorting in Granny's arms.

"We just killed the only thing in this zip code that worked," John said.

"Good," Sanda said.

* * *

Trevor's supplies were in his bedroom closet, behind a curtain, on steel shelves, labeled.

Six blue plastic jugs of water, a gallon each. A white five-gallon food bucket with a gasket lid, labeled RICE — 50 LB in black marker. A second bucket of dried beans. A case of canned chicken. A stainless steel water filter as tall as a coffee urn, with two black ceramic cartridges inside. A hand-crank radio with a flashlight on one end. An empty red gas can. A red nylon bag that said TRAUMA on it, full of things Dorina took one look at and began to touch with something like love. And in the garage, in the corner, the red hand cart.

They loaded it all onto the hand cart. John stood back and looked at it.

"He did everything right," John said. "Everything. And he's dead. And we did everything wrong, and here we are, taking his rice."

Sanda looked at him for a moment.

"That's the most honest sentence you've ever said," Sanda said.

* * *

That evening, the knock.

It came at dusk. It was not hard, like Jim's. It was polite. Charlie went off like an alarm anyway, and John went to the door with the Sig, and Jim came behind him with the shotgun, and John looked through the peephole.

There were seven people on his front walk.

The old man from down the street stood in front. He was small and neat, with his silver mustache trimmed and his white guayabera buttoned to the collar under the navy cardigan, and his brown leather slippers were dark with rain, and his face was gray and damp with sweat in the cold. Behind him stood the young couple from two doors down. The husband was in his late twenties, with a sleeve of orange koi tattooed down one forearm under a black hoodie pushed up to the elbow. The wife wore purple scrubs, the last clean thing, John would learn, that she owned, and she was holding their little boy on her hip in dinosaur pajamas, and the little boy was not crying, which was somehow worse than if he had been. Behind them were three more people John had seen around the Preserve for two years and never spoken to: a big woman of fifty in a pink bathrobe with disheveled curly hair; a teenage boy holding a skateboard he did not seem to know he was holding; and a very old man in a Korean War veteran's cap, leaning on a walker with tennis balls on its feet.

John opened the door with the Sig held low.

"Good evening," the old man in the guayabera said. "My name is Ramón Alvarez. I live at number eleven." He paused. "I danced with your grandmother."

"I remember," John said.

"We heard the shooting," Mr. Alvarez said. "Last night. And this morning. And then we saw you with the cart, coming back from Heron." He looked past John into the dark apartment, and then back. "Forgive me. I'll be direct. We need water. All of us. We have nothing left. The boy—" He nodded at the toddler on the young woman's hip. "The boy has had nothing since yesterday."

John stood in the doorway with the rifle and did not say anything.

He had been having this argument with himself since Black Friday. *We can't feed everyone.* He had said it to Sanda on the floor among the Christmas ornaments. He had said it to himself on the drive home from Costco. If you open the door, they will all come. If you give to one, you give to all, and then there is nothing left for your own. He had said it in a hundred ways, a hundred times, and he had always believed it, and now it was standing on his front walk in purple scrubs, holding a baby.

"We can't," John said. His voice came out strange. "We can't feed everybody. We've got six people in here. Two dogs. I'm sorry. We just—"

"*Move*," said Sanda.

She came between him and his father, ducking under the shotgun, pushing the Sig aside with her elbow, and she was carrying two of Trevor's blue gallon jugs, one in each hand, and under her arm, wedged against her ribs, a gallon Ziploc bag of Trevor's rice.

"We're not feeding everybody," Sanda said. "We're feeding *them*."

She walked out onto the wet front walk and put one of the jugs into the young mother's free hand and the other into Mr. Alvarez's, and pressed the bag of rice against the young father's chest until he took it.

There was a long moment when nobody moved.

"Everybody," Jim said, behind John, in the low, slow voice, the one from the funeral and the retirement party and the night of the lobsters. "Let's everybody just take a breath."

They did. John lowered the Sig. Jim lowered the shotgun. On the front walk, the young father — his name was Danny — let out a breath so long it seemed to empty him.

So John told them. It was the first useful thing he had taught anyone since the world went dark, and it was not about guns. He told them about the clean water in the tank on the back of every toilet, which most of them had not thought of, and how to ladle it out. He told them about the Publix bags. He told them about boiling rainwater, because birds sat on roofs. He told them that Granny's radio had said that morning that the Guard was setting up a water station at the high school on Lake Mary Boulevard, starting tomorrow, and that the trucks were the old kind, so they were probably real.

And he went back inside and got Trevor's water filter, the tall stainless one with the two black cartridges, and he brought it out and put it in Mr. Alvarez's arms.

"You pour water in the top," John said. "Any water. Rain. Pool water. It comes out clean at the bottom. It takes a while. It works."

Mr. Alvarez held the filter against his cardigan with both arms the way Dorina had held the cozonac.

"My insulin," the old man said quietly, to John, so the others would not hear. "It was in the refrigerator. The refrigerator is a cabinet now." He shrugged, very slightly. "I have perhaps a day. Maybe two. I am not asking you for that. Nobody has that. I only wanted somebody to know."

John did not know what to say. Behind him, Dorina said something in Romanian, softly, and stepped forward and took the old man's hand in both of hers and held it, and said in English, "No sugar. Water, little bit, all day. Walk slow. Sit when is dizzy," and Mr. Alvarez nodded, and thanked her, and they both knew it was not enough.

Nobody on the front walk mentioned the generator that had stopped humming on Heron that morning. Nobody mentioned the satellite dish hanging by its cable. Nobody mentioned Trevor.

They went away slowly in the dusk, with the jugs and the rice and the filter, the old man in the walker last.

John shut the door. Sanda turned the deadbolt.

"Now everyone knows what we have," Liz said quietly.

"Everyone with a screen already knew," John said.

He sat down heavily in the recliner. Jim sat on the arm of the couch. Liz stood at the window. Sanda leaned against the door with her eyes closed. Nobody had a plan. Not one of them. They had water for a few days, and a bucket of rice, and a single can of propane, and thirty-one guns, and a dead man's hand cart, and the whole street knew it, and so did anything with a battery.

From the couch, where she was sitting with the pug asleep in her lap, Granny spoke.

"We could go to the old place," Granny said. "There's a well."

Everyone turned and looked at her. It was the first time in four days, it occurred to John, that anybody had paid attention to anything Granny said. And nobody had asked her. She had just said it.

"The old place," John said slowly. "In Alabama."

"Hoyt's daddy dug that well in nineteen and twenty-six," Granny said. "It never went dry. Not once, not in the drought. The Pruitts down the road have been keeping an eye on the house for me thirty years." She stroked the pug. "There's a well, and there's a pump, and there's people down there who know how to live without the lights. Because they never had 'em that long to start with."

John looked at his father. Jim looked at Liz. Liz looked at Sanda. Sanda looked at John.

"We'd need the RV," John said.

"We've got the RV," Jim said.

"First light," Sanda said.

And it was decided, just like that, by a ninety-year-old woman in a pink housecoat with a pug in her lap, and nobody argued.

John sat back in the recliner. He closed his good eye. Then he opened it again.

"Dad," John said. "When's the last time you saw the RV?"

Jim thought about it. He thought for longer than John liked.

"Tuesday," Jim said.

It was Thursday.

# Chapter Eighteen

## The Road

> *NO BUS*
>
> — hand-painted sign, bus stop, Lake Mary Boulevard

He broke his own rule before breakfast.

Someone had to stay with Granny and Dorina and the guns. So at first light on Friday, John and his father went alone, with the Sig and the shotgun and two cooler bags of empty Ziplocs. Sanda stood in the doorway with her rifle and watched them go and did not say *I thought we weren't splitting up*, which was worse than if she had.

Every window of the Publix was broken. Shopping carts lay on their sides across the lot like dead animals, and plastic bags blew along the asphalt. A car at the end of the lot had burned down to its frame. In front of the Publix doors there were long dark stains on the pavement, and John looked at them once and did not look again. There were no bodies. Someone had taken them away. There were no people at all.

"There it is," Jim said quietly.

Behind a row of hedges at the far end of the lot, the motor home was still there.

The door had a dent in the middle of it the size of a man's boot. The huge curved windshield, the face of the whale, was smashed in; it hung in the frame in a sagging white web, and glass was everywhere. Jim stood in front of it with his hands at his sides and looked at it the way a man looks at a house after a hurricane.

"Well," Jim said finally. "They broke out the windshield."

The door was jammed in its frame. John braced his feet and pulled with everything he had, and it came open with a pop. Inside, every cabinet stood open. The refrigerator was empty. The drawers had been dumped on the floor. And all three of the televisions were gone — the one over the dash, the one over the dinette, the one in the bedroom — torn right off their mounts, with the wires hanging out of the walls.

"They took the TVs," Jim said, staring at the empty mounts. "In the middle of all this. Somebody carried off three televisions."

"Where are they going to plug them in?" John said.

Jim brushed the glass off the driver's seat with his cap and sat down and put the key in the ignition and turned it. The big engine coughed once and roared to life. John went to the galley sink and lifted the faucet handle, and water came out. He turned it off fast.

"They didn't find the tank," John said.

The little red pin on Jim's phone had said *Water available*, and somebody had come in the dark and kicked in the door and found no water anywhere they knew to look, and taken the televisions instead. The seventy gallons had been under their feet the whole time.

They filled all eight Ziplocs. Then John stood in the aisle and looked out through the broken windshield at the dead gate across the road.

"Dad," John said. "You have jumper cables?"

"Of course I have jumper cables."

"Can you drive this thing over there? Right up to the gate?"

Jim did not ask why. He swung the motor home around the lot, glass sliding off the dash, and across the empty road and up to the entrance of the Preserve, and stopped with the bumper a foot from the closed steel gate.

* * *

The steel box beside the gate still had no cover. The green Halcyon board was still bolted to the back of it, its little light dead. John did not look at the board. He looked below it, at the gate's old motor, the fat gray one that had been there before Halcyon and would be there after, with its two big brass terminals.

"I'm not going to ask the controller," John said. "I'm going to ask the motor." He looked up at his father, half laughing. "You wouldn't happen to have a spool of wire?"

"No," Jim said, thinking. "But I've got an extension cord. And a pair of scissors."

That was good enough. John cut a foot off each end of the cord, split the wires, and stripped them with his teeth. Jim popped the motor home's hood and clamped the jumper cables onto the battery.

"We're live," Jim called.

John knelt in front of the steel box with the other ends of the jumper cables in his hands, careful to keep the red and black clamps apart. He clipped one short wire to the black clamp and one to the red. He held the black wire against the motor's ground terminal and pressed it there with his thumb. Then he touched the red one to the other terminal, quickly.

There was a fat blue spark and a crack. The motor jumped and hummed for half a second and stopped when he pulled away.

He took a breath. He pressed the red wire back onto the terminal and held it there.

The motor groaned. It was a twenty-four-volt motor and he was feeding it twelve, and it did not like it, and it said so, grinding, slow. But it turned. The big steel arm of the gate shuddered and began to swing open, at half speed, grumbling, toward him, past him, until it stood open all the way.

John pulled the wires away. The gate stayed open.
